DFA construction & minimisation · 25
TOC-U1-Q01MCQeasy

Over the alphabet Σ = {a, b}, the minimal DFA that accepts exactly those strings which contain an even number of a's has how many states?

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. Infinitely many
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A DFA cannot count; it can only remember a finite amount of information. For the parity of the number of a's the only thing that matters is whether the count seen so far is even or odd — two facts, hence at most two states.

Step by step.

  1. Let E = "even number of a's so far" (start state, and final because the empty string has zero a's). Let O = "odd number so far".
  2. δ(E,a) = O, δ(E,b) = E — a b does not change the parity.
  3. δ(O,a) = E, δ(O,b) = O.
  4. Both states are reachable (ε reaches E, a reaches O) and distinguishable (E is final, O is not). So no further merging is possible.

Answer: Option (A) — 2 states.

Common misconception. Many candidates add a third "dead" state, imagining that a misplaced b must be rejected. It must not: b's are simply ignored, so the transition on b stays inside the two parity states. Equally wrong is "infinitely many", which forgets that a DFA only needs the parity, not the count.

TOC-U1-Q02MCQeasy

In DFA minimisation by the table-filling method, two states p and q of a complete DFA are distinguishable exactly when:

  1. there is a string w ∈ Σ* such that exactly one of δ̂(p,w) and δ̂(q,w) is a final state.
  2. p and q are both final states.
  3. p and q are both non-final states.
  4. no string takes p and q to different states.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. States are equivalent when no future input can tell them apart, i.e. when for every string w the two runs agree on acceptance. Distinguishable is the negation of that: some string w makes exactly one run end in a final state.

Step by step.

  1. Take w = ε. If one state is final and the other is not, they are already distinguishable.
  2. Otherwise try longer strings: p and q are distinguishable if for some symbol a the states δ(p,a) and δ(q,a) are distinguishable.
  3. Table filling propagates this rule until no new pair is marked; unmarked pairs are merged.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Options (B) and (C) are necessary conditions only for w = ε: two final states, or two non-final states, may still be told apart by a longer string. Option (D) describes equivalence, the opposite of distinguishability.

TOC-U1-Q09MCQmedium

Let L = {w ∈ {0,1}* : #₀(w) ≡ 0 (mod 3) and #₁(w) ≡ 0 (mod 2)}. The minimal DFA accepting L has how many states?

  1. 5
  2. 6
  3. 8
  4. 3
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A conjunction of two independent modular conditions is recognised by the product of the two machines; the state count multiplies.

Step by step.

  1. The condition #₀ ≡ 0 mod 3 needs 3 states (the residues 0, 1, 2).
  2. The condition #₁ ≡ 0 mod 2 needs 2 states (even, odd).
  3. The product machine tracks the pair (i, j) with i = #₀ mod 3 and j = #₁ mod 2: 3 × 2 = 6 states, and every pair is reachable.
  4. All six are pairwise distinguishable (residue classes of a mod-3 counter and a mod-2 counter are distinct), so the product is already minimal.

Answer: Option (B) — 6 states.

Common misconception. Adding the counts (3 + 2 = 5) instead of multiplying them, or forgetting that the two conditions are independent and must be tracked simultaneously.

TOC-U1-Q12MCQhard

Consider the complete DFA below over Σ = {0,1}, start state A, final states {A, E}. The minimal DFA equivalent to it has how many states?

| 0 | 1 ------+-----+----- A* | B | C B | A | D C | D | E D | C | B E* | B | C

  1. 5
  2. 4
  3. 3
  4. 2
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Minimisation first deletes unreachable states, then merges states that are indistinguishable by every input string.

Step by step.

  1. All five states are reachable: A → B, C; C → E; B → D.
  2. Compare A and E. Both are final. δ(A,0) = B = δ(E,0) and δ(A,1) = C = δ(E,1), so no input can separate them: A ≡ E.
  3. Every other pair is separated by a short string: B and C are non-final but δ(B,0) = A is final while δ(C,0) = D is not; D is separated from B by 0 (to C, non-final) versus A (final); and the final state A is separated from every non-final state by ε.
  4. Merging A and E leaves {[A,E], B, C, D} = 4 states.
  5. As a check, the language is "even number of 0's and even number of 1's", whose product DFA has exactly 4 states.

Answer: Option (B) — 4 states.

Common misconception. Answering 5 by assuming that two distinct final states must be distinguishable, or answering 3 by merging the two non-final states B and C that differ on input 0. Merging is allowed only when no string separates the pair.

TOC-U1-Q16MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about DFA minimisation are TRUE?

  1. A state that is not reachable from the start state can always be deleted without changing the accepted language.
  2. Two states p, q are equivalent if and only if for every string w, δ̂(p,w) ∈ F exactly when δ̂(q,w) ∈ F.
  3. Merging equivalent states never changes the language accepted by the automaton.
  4. A DFA in which every state is reachable and every pair of states is distinguishable can still be reduced further.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Minimisation = remove unreachable states, then merge equivalent states; the result is the unique minimal DFA up to renaming.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true. An unreachable state is never entered on any input, so it can never affect acceptance.
  2. (B) true. This is exactly the definition of state equivalence (≡ is the right congruence).
  3. (C) true. Merging equivalent states preserves the run of every string up to equivalence, hence preserves acceptance.
  4. (D) false. Reachability plus pairwise distinguishability is precisely the definition of a minimal DFA; nothing is left to remove or merge.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Believing that a "tidy-looking" DFA can always be squeezed further, or that merging states always risks changing the language — merging equivalent states cannot.

TOC-U1-Q21NATeasy

Count the states of the minimal DFA over {0,1} that accepts exactly the binary strings denoting a number divisible by 5. Take the usual convention: leading zeros are allowed and the empty string denotes 0, so ε is accepted.

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Divisibility by k is decided by the remainder of the value read so far, and reading a bit b maps r ↦ (2r + b) mod 5. The remainder is the state.

Step by step.

  1. States q₀ … q₄, where q_r means "the number read so far leaves remainder r". Start q₀, final q₀.
  2. Transitions: δ(q_r, 0) = q_{2r mod 5} and δ(q_r, 1) = q_{(2r+1) mod 5}.
  3. All five remainders are reachable (q₀ by ε, then q₁ by 1, q₂ by 10, q₃ by 11, q₄ by 100).
  4. They are pairwise distinguishable: q_0 is the only final state, and from q_r, q_s one can always append digits to reach a multiple of 5 for one and not the other. So 5 is minimal.

Answer: 5.

Common misconception. Answering 6 by adding a separate "no input yet" start state; with the stated convention ε denotes 0 and is accepted, so q₀ doubles as the start state. Another trap is writing 4 or 10 by confusing divisor and radix.

TOC-U1-Q22NATmedium

How many states does the minimal complete DFA have for L = {a^n b^m : n ≥ 1, m ≥ 2} over Σ = {a,b}?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The machine must remember whether the initial a-block has started, and how many b's have been seen (0, 1, ≥2) — plus a dead state for violations.

Step by step.

  1. q₀ (start, non-final): no a yet. On a → q₁; on b → d (violates n ≥ 1).
  2. q₁ (non-final): at least one a. On a → q₁; on b → q₂.
  3. q₂ (non-final): exactly one b. On b → q₃; on a → d (an a after b is illegal).
  4. q₃ (final): at least two b's. On b → q₃; on a → d.
  5. d (dead, non-final): self-loops on both symbols.
  6. Five states, all reachable, pairwise distinguishable (q₃ is the only final state; d is the only one from which nothing final is reachable; q₀,q₁,q₂ differ in how many b's they still need) — so 5 is minimal.

Answer: 5.

Common misconception. Answering 4 by dropping the dead state — but the DFA must be complete, and a b before any a has to go somewhere. Answering 6 by counting a separate start state for q₀.

TOC-U1-Q26MCQeasy

Over Σ = {a, b}, how many states does the minimal complete DFA have that accepts exactly those strings which end in ab?

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 5
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A DFA reads left to right and cannot look ahead, so "ends in ab" is decided by remembering only how much of the pattern has already been matched. The number of states equals the number of distinct residuals of the language.

Step by step.

  1. State q₀ — nothing of the pattern matched so far (also the start state, and the state reached after any symbol other than the one that advances the match).
  2. State q₁ — the last symbol read was a.
  3. State q₂ — the last two symbols read are ab; this is the only final state.
  4. Transitions: q₀ →a q₁, q₀ →b q₀, q₁ →a q₁, q₁ →b q₂, q₂ →a q₁, q₂ →b q₀.
  5. All three are reachable (ε, a, ab) and pairwise distinguishable: q₂ accepts ε while the others reject it; q₁ accepts b while q₀ rejects it. So no two states merge.

Answer: Option (B) — 3 states.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Two states cannot separate "ends in a" from "ends in ab"; the third residual is genuinely different.
  • (C) and (D) add a state for "ends in b", which is the same residual as "nothing matched" — the table-filling method merges them.

Common misconception. Candidates insert an extra start state distinct from "no partial match seen". Those two are the same residual, and a DFA has one state per residual, not one per event.

Takeaway. A language that constrains the last k symbols needs k + 1 states; the "ends in" pattern is the cheapest way GATE tests this.

TOC-U1-Q30MCQeasy

Which one of the following is TRUE of the minimal DFA of a regular language L?

  1. It is unique up to renaming of states.
  2. It always has strictly fewer states than every NFA that accepts L.
  3. Every one of its states must be a final state.
  4. It is never a complete DFA.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. By the Myhill–Nerode theorem the states of the minimal DFA are exactly the distinct residuals u⁻¹L = {v : uv ∈ L} of the language. A language determines its residuals uniquely, so it determines its minimal DFA uniquely.

Step by step.

  1. Every DFA for L assigns each state a residual: the set of suffixes accepted from it.
  2. Two states with the same residual are indistinguishable and can be merged.
  3. So every DFA for L has at least as many states as L has distinct residuals, and the minimal DFA has exactly that many.
  4. Two minimal DFAs for the same language therefore have the same states up to the naming of states and the choice of the start state's name.

Answer: Option (A) — unique up to renaming of states.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) fails: for {w : the n-th symbol from the right is 1} an NFA with n + 1 states suffices while every DFA needs 2ⁿ states, so the minimal DFA can be exponentially larger than an NFA.
  • (C) fails for any language that is not Σ*; a DFA needs non-final states too.
  • (D) fails: the minimal DFA of a regular language is always complete — missing transitions would have to lead to an implicit rejecting sink, which is a state.

Common misconception. Believing that different constructions produce genuinely different minimal machines. They can differ while being built, but the end result is the same machine up to names.

Takeaway. One regular language, one minimal DFA. This uniqueness is what lets GATE ask for "the number of states" without further qualification.

TOC-U1-Q34MCQmedium

A complete DFA over Σ = {0, 1} has 6 states. Exactly 2 of them cannot be reached from the start state, and among the 4 reachable states exactly 2 are equivalent to each other. How many states does the minimal equivalent DFA have?

  1. 3
  2. 4
  3. 5
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Minimisation is two operations in a fixed order: first delete unreachable states (they can never affect the accepted language), then merge equivalent reachable states (they are indistinguishable by any suffix).

Step by step.

  1. Start with 6 states.
  2. Remove the 2 unreachable states: 6 − 2 = 4 remain.
  3. Among those 4, one pair is equivalent, so the pair collapses into a single state: 4 − 1 = 3.
  4. The remaining 3 states are reachable and pairwise distinguishable, so no further merging is possible.

Answer: Option (A) — 3 states.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) 4 forgets to merge the equivalent pair.
  • (C) 5 removes only one unreachable state, or merges before pruning.
  • (D) 6 assumes no reduction at all.

Common misconception. Merging first and pruning afterwards. It happens to give the same count here, but it can go wrong: an unreachable state can be equivalent to a reachable one, and merging them before pruning can hide a real distinction. Prune, then merge.

Takeaway. Minimal states = (reachable states) − (merges among them). Do the two steps in that order, and never count a state that cannot be entered.

TOC-U1-Q38MCQhard

Consider the complete DFA over Σ = {0, 1} with start state A, final states {C, D} and the transition table below. The minimal DFA equivalent to it has how many states?

| 0 | 1 ------+-----+----- A | B | E B | D | C C* | F | A D* | A | C E | D | C F | B | F

  1. 4
  2. 5
  3. 6
  4. 7
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Minimality is decided by two tests: every state must be reachable from the start state, and no two states may be equivalent. States with identical rows and the same finality are equivalent and merge.

Step by step.

  1. Reachability. From A: A → {B, E}; from B: D, C; from C: F, A; from D: A, C; from E: D, C; from F: B, F. All six states are reachable, so nothing is deleted.
  2. First refinement. Split into final {C, D} and non-final {A, B, E, F}.
  3. Compare rows inside the non-final block. B and E both go to D on 0 and to C on 1, and both are non-final — the rows are identical, so B ≡ E. A (→B, E) and F (→B, F) differ from that and from each other, and they sit in different classes from {C, D}, so they stay separate.
  4. Compare inside the final block. C goes to F on 0 while D goes to A; A and F are already separated (they reach different classes), so C and D are distinguishable.
  5. Count. Classes are {A}, {B, E}, {C}, {D}, {F} — five states.

Answer: Option (B) — 5 states.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 4 would need a second merge, but no other pair shares both row and finality.
  • (C) 6 is the unminimised count: it misses B ≡ E.
  • (D) 7 exceeds the number of states in the given table, which is impossible for a minimal DFA.

Common misconception. Merging states with equal rows but different finality. Here B and E are both non-final, which is what licenses the merge; a final and a non-final state can never be equivalent because ε separates them.

Takeaway. Run table filling in order: split final from non-final, then refine by destination classes until nothing changes. Identical rows in the same block are an immediate merge.

TOC-U1-Q41MSQmedium

Which of the following languages over Σ = {a, b} have a minimal complete DFA with exactly 3 states? (Select all that apply.)

  1. {w : w ends in ab}
  2. {w : w has an even number of a's}
  3. {w : w contains aa as a substring}
  4. {w : |w| ≡ 1 (mod 3)}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The number of states of the minimal DFA equals the number of distinct residuals. Each option is checked by counting the residuals it forces.

Step by step.

  1. (A) ends in ab. Three residuals: "no partial match", "last symbol was a", "last two symbols are ab". All reachable, all distinguishable. 3 states ✓
  2. (B) even number of a's. Only the parity of the count matters, and b's change nothing. Two residuals. 2 states ✗
  3. (C) contains aa. Three residuals: no aa yet and last symbol not a; no aa yet and last symbol is a; aa already seen (absorbing, accepting). 3 states ✓
  4. (D) |w| ≡ 1 (mod 3). Three residuals, indexed by |w| mod 3, the one with residue 1 accepting. 3 states ✓

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Adding a separate dead state to (C) on the grounds that a wrong symbol must be rejected. After aa every further symbol is accepted, so the third state absorbs; and before aa there is no rejecting sink because every symbol keeps the run alive. Counting states "intuitively" instead of by residual is the usual slip.

Takeaway. Ask "what must be remembered?" For suffix patterns it is the matched prefix length; for counts it is the residue; for absorbing conditions it is a single flag state.

TOC-U1-Q46NATeasy

How many states does the minimal complete DFA over Σ = {a, b} have that accepts exactly those strings which contain aba as a substring?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. "Contains aba" is decided by remembering the longest prefix of aba that is a suffix of what has been read. That gives one state per prefix length plus one absorbing accepting state.

Step by step.

  1. q₀ — no prefix of aba matched.
  2. q₁ — the last symbol is a, a prefix of length 1.
  3. q₂ — the last two symbols are ab, a prefix of length 2.
  4. q₃ — aba has been seen; absorbing and accepting.
  5. Transitions: q₀ →a q₁, q₀ →b q₀; q₁ →a q₁, q₁ →b q₂; q₂ →a q₃, q₂ →b q₀; q₃ →a q₃, q₃ →b q₃.
  6. The four residuals are distinct (ε separates q₃ from the rest; ba separates q₂; ba against b separates q₁), so no merging is possible.

Answer: 4.

Common misconception. Answering 3 by forgetting the accepting state, or 5 by adding a dead state for "wrong symbol". After aba every input is accepted, so the accepting state absorbs; and before aba no input kills the run, so no dead state is needed.

Takeaway. A pattern of length k needs k + 1 states when the matched state absorbs. The subtle part is the fallback transitions: q₂ →b q₀ because abb ends in bb, not in a prefix of aba.

TOC-U1-Q47NATeasy

How many states does the minimal complete DFA over Σ = {0, 1} have that accepts exactly those binary strings whose value is congruent to 2 modulo 3? Leading zeros are allowed and the empty string denotes 0.

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Reading a binary string left to right, appending a bit b maps the value v to 2v + b. Modulo 3 only the residue of v matters, so three residues suffice — and are necessary.

Step by step.

  1. States r₀, r₁, r₂ meaning "value so far ≡ i (mod 3)". Start state r₀, because the empty string has value 0.
  2. Transitions follow r → (2r + b) mod 3.
  3. r₀ →0 r₀, r₀ →1 r₁; r₁ →0 r₂, r₁ →1 r₀; r₂ →0 r₁, r₂ →1 r₂.
  4. The single final state is r₂.
  5. Check: 10 = 2 → r₀ →1 r₁ →0 r₂ ✓ accepted; 11 = 3 ≡ 0 → r₁ →1 r₀ ✓ rejected.
  6. The three residues are pairwise distinguishable — from r₀ the string 10 is accepted, from r₁ the string 0 is accepted, from r₂ the empty string is accepted — so all three are needed.

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Treating the number of bits as the state count, or forgetting that leading zeros are allowed so 010 and 10 must land in the same state. The state is the residue, not the length.

Takeaway. For "value mod k" languages the minimal DFA has exactly k states, with r → (2r + b) mod k. Moduli other than 2 need the general rule, not the parity shortcut.

TOC-U1-Q51MCQeasy

Over Σ = {a, b}, how many states does the minimal complete DFA have that accepts the language {ε}, i.e. the empty string and nothing else?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. Infinitely many
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A complete DFA must define a transition on every symbol from every state, and the start state alone decides membership of ε. Every non-empty string must therefore be sent to a state that is not final and that no string can escape from.

Step by step.

  1. Let q₀ be the start state. Since ε ∈ L, q₀ must be final.
  2. Since a ∉ L and b ∉ L, both transitions out of q₀ must lead to a non-final state; call it d (the dead state).
  3. Completeness forces transitions out of d on a and b. Once a string has left q₀ it can never return, so δ(d, a) = δ(d, b) = d.
  4. Two states suffice and neither can be dropped: q₀ is final and d is not, so they are distinguishable.

Answer: Option (B) — 2 states.

Common misconception. Answering 1 on the grounds that only one string is accepted. A single final state accepts every string, and a single non-final state accepts none; rejecting all but one string needs a second state to reject into.

TOC-U1-Q52MCQeasy

A complete DFA over Σ = {0, 1} has exactly one state, that state is final, and both transitions from it lead back to itself. The language accepted is:

  1. {ε}
  2. ∅
  3. Σ*
  4. the strings of length exactly 1
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A DFA accepts a string exactly when the state reached after reading all of it is final. If the only state is final and every symbol loops, every string ends where it started.

Step by step.

  1. The run on ε stays in the start state, which is final, so ε ∈ L.
  2. Reading any symbol keeps the machine in the same state, so by induction on the length of w the run on every w ends in that state.
  3. That state is final, so w ∈ L for every w ∈ Σ*.
  4. The machine is already minimal: a single state is the fewest possible, and Σ* needs no rejection state.

Answer: Option (C) — Σ*.

Common misconception. Reading "one state" as "one symbol accepted". The number of states says nothing about the length of the strings accepted; it is the pattern of final states and loops that does.

TOC-U1-Q57MSQeasy

Which of the following languages over Σ = {a, b} have a minimal complete DFA with exactly 2 states? (Select all that apply.)

  1. {ε}
  2. Σ*
  3. {w : |w| is even}
  4. {w : w starts with a}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Count states by counting the distinctions a DFA must remember. Two states means exactly one binary distinction; the start state plus one more state, one of which is final.

Step by step.

  1. (A) {ε}. Start state final, one dead state for everything non-empty: 2 states, and they are distinguishable (ε separates them).
  2. (B) Σ*. Every string is accepted, so one final state with self-loops is complete and minimal: 1 state, not 2.
  3. (C) even length. Only the parity of the length matters: an even state (start, final) and an odd state, each flipping on every symbol. Both are reachable and distinguishable: 2 states.
  4. (D) starts with a. Three distinctions are needed — nothing read yet, first symbol was a, first symbol was b (dead) — so the minimal complete DFA has 3 states.

Answer: Options (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Assuming "starts with a" is a one-bit property because the answer is yes or no. The DFA also has to represent the third situation "the first symbol has already been read and it was not a", which cannot be merged with either of the others.

TOC-U1-Q58NATeasy

How many states does the minimal complete DFA over Σ = {a, b} have that accepts exactly the strings of even length?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A DFA counts only modulo what it can store. For a length condition the relevant residue is the length modulo the period, here 2.

Step by step.

  1. State E: an even number of symbols has been read. It is the start state and it is final, because ε has even length.
  2. State O: an odd number has been read. Not final.
  3. Every symbol flips the parity: δ(E, a) = δ(E, b) = O and δ(O, a) = δ(O, b) = E. The machine is complete and both states are reachable.
  4. The two states are distinguishable by ε, so no merging is possible: the minimal complete DFA has 2 states.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Adding a dead state "to reject the odd strings". Rejection needs no extra state — a non-final state already rejects — and the transition back to E is essential, because an odd prefix can still grow into an even string.

TOC-U1-Q61MCQmedium

How many states does the minimal complete DFA over Σ = {0, 1} have that accepts exactly those strings which contain an even number of 0's and end in 1?

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two independent facts — the parity of the number of 0's and whether the last symbol was a 1 — suggest the product of two 2-state machines, so at most 4 states. The product is an upper bound; a minimal machine counts only the classes that survive, so the answer has to be checked, not multiplied.

Step by step.

  1. Candidate states: (even, last = 1), (even, last ≠ 1), (odd, last = 1), (odd, last ≠ 1). Start in (even, last ≠ 1); the only final state is (even, last = 1).
  2. Reading 0 flips the parity and clears the "last = 1" bit; reading 1 keeps the parity and sets it. So (even, last ≠ 1) and (even, last = 1) both go to (odd, last ≠ 1) on 0 and to (even, last = 1) on 1.
  3. Those two have identical transitions, but one is final and the other is not, so ε separates them: they stay apart.
  4. (odd, last ≠ 1) and (odd, last = 1) also have identical transitions — both go to (even, last ≠ 1) on 0 and to (odd, last = 1) on 1 — and neither is final. Identical transitions plus identical finality means equivalent, so they merge.
  5. Three states remain, all reachable (ε, 1, 0) and pairwise distinguishable.

Answer: Option (B) — 3 states.

Common misconception. Answering 4 by multiplying the two parities. While the count of 0's is odd the machine cannot accept at all, and it must read another 0 first — which erases whatever the "last symbol" bit was holding. The bit is therefore worthless in the two odd states, and they collapse into one.

TOC-U1-Q65MSQmedium

A language L over Σ = {0, 1} is accepted by some complete DFA with 4 states. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. The minimal complete DFA of L has at most 4 states.
  2. Every state of the minimal complete DFA of L is reachable from its start state.
  3. No two distinct states of the minimal complete DFA of L are equivalent.
  4. The minimal complete DFA of L has exactly 4 states.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Minimality is a lower bound on states for the language, never an upper one, and it is characterised by two conditions: no unreachable states and no equivalent pairs.

Step by step.

  1. If M with 4 states accepts L, then minimising M — deleting unreachable states, then merging equivalent ones — yields the minimal DFA of L with at most 4 states. So (A) is true.
  2. Deleting unreachable states changes nothing about the language, and a minimal DFA has none, so (B) is true.
  3. Merging equivalent states also preserves the language, and a minimal DFA has no such pair, so (C) is true.
  4. (D) is false: a 4-state DFA can be non-minimal. For instance, a 4-state machine with two unreachable states accepts the same language as a 2-state minimal DFA.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Treating "accepted by a 4-state DFA" as "needs 4 states". The bound inherited from a given machine is an upper bound; the minimal count is what the language forces, and it can be strictly smaller.

TOC-U1-Q68NATmedium

How many states does the minimal complete DFA over Σ = {a, b} have that accepts exactly those strings which have even length and end in b?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two facts are needed at once — the parity of the length and the identity of the last symbol — so the product construction gives at most 4 states. The minimal count is the number of classes left after deleting unreachable states and merging equivalent ones.

Step by step.

  1. Candidate states (even, b), (even, ≠b), (odd, b), (odd, ≠b). Start in (even, ≠b), because the empty string has no last symbol. The only final state is (even, b).
  2. Every symbol flips the parity. Reading b sets the last-symbol bit, reading a clears it. Hence (even, b) and (even, ≠b) both go to (odd, ≠b) on a and to (odd, b) on b.
  3. Those two have identical transitions but differ in finality, so ε separates them — both survive.
  4. (odd, b) and (odd, ≠b) both go to (even, ≠b) on a and to (even, b) on b, and neither is final: identical transitions, identical finality, so they are equivalent and merge.
  5. Three states remain, reachable via ε, b, a, and pairwise distinguishable.

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Reporting 4 because the two parities and the two possible last symbols multiply. Once the length is odd the machine must read one more symbol before it can accept, and that symbol overwrites the "last" information, so the two odd states cannot be told apart.

TOC-U1-Q69NATmedium

For L = {w ∈ {a, b}* : w ends in ab}, how many equivalence classes does the Myhill–Nerode relation ≡_L have?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two strings are ≡_L-equivalent when no continuation distinguishes them. The number of classes is exactly the number of states of the minimal DFA, so the question can be answered by building that DFA.

Step by step.

  1. Class 1: strings that do not currently end in a — the start class, since ε has no suffix. Appending b keeps a string here.
  2. Class 2: strings that end in a but not in ab. Appending b finishes the word; appending a stays in class 2.
  3. Class 3: strings that end in ab — the accepting class. Appending b returns to class 1, appending a goes to class 2.
  4. These three are pairwise distinguishable: ε separates class 3 from the rest, and b separates class 2 from class 1. So no two merge, and 3 is the count.

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Counting 2, one class per "accepted / not accepted". Equivalence is about future behaviour, not present membership: class 1 and class 2 are both non-accepting but respond differently to the continuation b.

TOC-U1-Q71MCQhard

How many states does the minimal complete DFA over Σ = {0, 1} have that accepts exactly those strings whose value, read as a binary number with leading zeros allowed, is divisible by 6?

  1. 3
  2. 4
  3. 6
  4. 12
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Divisibility by m is decided by the residue of the prefix read so far, because reading a bit b maps the value v to 2v + b. That gives m candidate states — but m is the answer only when the doubling map is injective on residues, which fails whenever m is even.

Step by step.

  1. Candidate states q₀, …, q₅ for the residues 0 … 5, with δ(q_r, b) = q_{(2r + b) mod 6}; start and only final state q₀.
  2. Collapse q₁ with q₄: 2·1 + b = 2 + b and 2·4 + b = 8 + b ≡ 2 + b (mod 6) for both bits, so the two states have identical transitions and identical finality — equivalent.
  3. Collapse q₂ with q₅ the same way: 2·2 + b = 4 + b and 2·5 + b = 10 + b ≡ 4 + b (mod 6).
  4. q₀ and q₃ also have identical transitions, but q₀ is final and q₃ is not, so ε separates them; they stay apart.
  5. Four classes remain and all are reachable: ε reaches {0}, 1 reaches {1,4}, 11 reaches {3}, 10 reaches {2,5}. They are pairwise distinguishable: ε isolates {0}, the continuation 0 separates {3} from {1,4}, and 10 separates {1,4} from {2,5}.

Answer: Option (B) — 4 states.

Common misconception. Answering 6 on autopilot — "divisible by m needs m states". That shortcut is valid when 2 is invertible modulo m, as it is for m = 5 or m = 7; for m = 6 the doubling map sends both 1 and 4 to the same place, and the surplus states merge.

TOC-U1-Q74MSQhard

Let L = {w ∈ {a, b}* : #a(w) is odd and #b(w) is even}. Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. L is regular.
  2. The minimal complete DFA of L has 4 states.
  3. The complement of L has a minimal complete DFA with 4 states.
  4. L = {w : |w| is odd}.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Parity of two separate letter counts is a product of two 2-state machines, giving four states; complementation swaps final and non-final states without touching the transitions, so it never changes the state count of the minimal machine.

Step by step.

  1. States (p_a, p_b) ∈ {0,1}² with p_a the parity of the a-count and p_b that of the b-count; start at (0,0); accept at (1,0).
  2. All four states are reachable (ε, a, b, ab) and pairwise distinguishable: ε isolates (1,0), a separates (0,1) from (0,0), and b separates (1,1) from (1,0) after one more a. So (A) and (B) are true.
  3. For the complement, keep the same four states and declare (0,0), (0,1), (1,1) final instead. The machine is still complete, all states reachable, and every pair still distinguishable — the same witness strings work — so the minimal complement also has 4 states. (C) is true.
  4. (D) is false. bbb has odd length but an odd number of b's, so it is not in L. (In fact L forces odd length, but not every odd-length word qualifies.)

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Believing that the complement of a 4-state language must need a different number of states. Complementation is a relabelling of final states, so it preserves minimality exactly.

TOC-U1-Q75NAThard

Over Σ = {0, 1}, how many states does the minimal complete DFA have that accepts exactly those binary strings (leading zeros allowed) whose value is congruent to 1 or 2 modulo 7?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A congruence condition modulo m is decided by the residue of the value read so far, and the transition r ↦ (2r + b) mod m keeps the residue exact. The accepting states are the residues in the target set.

Step by step.

  1. States q₀, …, q₆ for residues 0 … 6; start at q₀ (the empty string denotes 0); final states q₁ and q₂.
  2. Transitions: δ(q_r, b) = q_{(2r + b) mod 7}. All 7 states are reachable, since 2 is invertible modulo the prime 7, so the map r ↦ 2r permutes the residues and appending bits walks through all of them.
  3. They are pairwise distinguishable: for r ≠ r′ the difference r − r′ is invertible modulo 7, so appending k zeros multiplies the difference by 2^k and never makes it 0; some continuation therefore lands in an accepting residue for exactly one of the two states.
  4. Hence exactly 7 states, the residue count itself.

Answer: 7.

Common misconception. Counting 2 states because "the answer is yes or no". The machine has to compute the residue before it can decide, and all seven residues are needed as intermediate memory even though only two of them accept.

NFA to DFA: subset construction · 16
TOC-U1-Q03MCQeasy

An NFA accepts a string w if and only if:

  1. at least one computation path labelled w leads from the start state to a final state.
  2. every computation path labelled w leads from the start state to a final state.
  3. the NFA has no ε-transitions on the path.
  4. exactly one computation path labelled w reaches a final state.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Nondeterminism is existential acceptance: the NFA guesses, and one successful guess is enough.

Step by step.

  1. Running an NFA on w produces a set of reachable states rather than a single state.
  2. The string is accepted when that set meets the set of final states.
  3. Dead-end branches are simply abandoned; they do not veto acceptance.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Option (B) is universal acceptance, which is not how an NFA accepts — an NFA with one accepting and one dead branch still accepts. Option (D) confuses "at least one" with "exactly one".

TOC-U1-Q04MCQeasy

An NFA with n states over Σ is converted to an equivalent DFA by the subset construction. The number of states of the resulting DFA is at most:

  1. 2^n
  2. n²
  3. n
  4. n + 1
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In the subset construction each DFA state is a set of NFA states, and there are 2^n such sets.

Step by step.

  1. The start state of the DFA is ε-closure(q₀).
  2. For a DFA state S and symbol a, the successor is the ε-closure of the union of δ(p,a) over all p ∈ S.
  3. Every DFA state is a subset of the n NFA states, so there can be no more than 2^n of them; only the reachable subsets actually appear.

Answer: Option (A) — at most 2^n states.

Common misconception. 2^n is an upper bound, not the usual size: most NFAs produce far fewer reachable subsets, and the DFA can then be minimised further. Candidates who read the bound as "exactly 2^n" lose the question.

TOC-U1-Q07MCQeasy

Which of the following statements is FALSE?

  1. Every NFA has an equivalent DFA.
  2. Every DFA is a special case of an NFA.
  3. A language accepted by an NFA with ε-transitions need not be regular.
  4. NFAs and DFAs accept exactly the same class of languages.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Adding nondeterminism and ε-moves does not add expressive power: both machines characterise the regular languages.

Step by step.

  1. ε-transitions are eliminated by taking ε-closures; the subset construction then yields a DFA for the same language.
  2. Hence every ε-NFA language is regular — statement (C) is false, and it is the required answer.
  3. (A), (B) and (D) are the standard equivalences.

Answer: Option (C) — the false statement.

Common misconception. Students expect that ε-moves buy extra power, because they look like spontaneous computation. They only shorten the description; the accepted class is unchanged.

TOC-U1-Q13MCQhard

An NFA over Σ = {0,1} has states {0,1,2,3}, start state 0, final state {3} and the transitions below (∅ means no move). The minimal DFA equivalent to this NFA has how many states?

| 0 | 1 ------+---------+-------- 0 | {0} | {0, 1} 1 | {2} | {2} 2 | {3} | {3} 3 | ∅ | ∅

  1. 4
  2. 5
  3. 8
  4. 16
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two steps are needed: subset construction to get a DFA, then minimisation. Skipping either step gives a wrong count.

Step by step.

  1. The NFA accepts w exactly when w has a 1 in the third position from the end — state 0 waits, guesses with 1 that the third-from-last symbol has been seen, and states 1, 2 count the remaining two symbols.
  2. Subset construction from {0}: {0} --1--> {0,1} --1--> {0,1,2} --1--> {0,1,2,3}, while 0-moves add 2, then 3: {0} --0--> {0}, {0,1} --0--> {0,2} --0--> {0,3}. Closing the table gives exactly {0}, {0,1}, {0,2}, {0,3}, {0,1,2}, {0,1,3}, {0,2,3}, {0,1,2,3} — 8 reachable subsets, so the 2³ bound is met.
  3. Minimisation cannot help: four of the eight subsets are accepting (those containing 3) and four are not, and the Myhill–Nerode argument for "third symbol from the right is 1" separates the 2³ strings 000, 001, …, 111 pairwise. So all 8 states survive.

Answer: Option (C) — 8 states.

Common misconception. Answering 4 (the NFA's own state count) forgets that determinisation is mandatory. Answering 16 assumes that all 2⁴ subsets are reachable, but this NFA reaches only 8. Answering 5 or 6 comes from dropping a subset state that is in fact reachable — check the table before deciding which subsets never appear.

TOC-U1-Q17MSQmedium

For an NFA with ε-transitions and ε-closure(q) written for the set of states reachable from q using only ε-moves, which statements are TRUE?

  1. q ∈ ε-closure(q) for every state q.
  2. ε-closure(q) is the set of states reachable from q by a path of ε-moves only, including q itself.
  3. In the subset construction, the start state of the DFA is ε-closure(q₀).
  4. ε-closure(q) can be empty when q has no ε-transitions.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. ε-closure is a reflexive, transitive closure: it always contains its argument and is closed under taking ε-steps again.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true. The empty path of length 0 reaches q, so q is always included.
  2. (B) true. That is the definition, made explicit about the empty path.
  3. (C) true. Before reading any symbol the NFA may slide along ε-moves, so the DFA starts at the whole closure.
  4. (D) false. Even with no ε-transitions the closure is {q}, never ∅.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Writing ε-closure(q) = ∅ for a state with no ε-moves. The closure of a single state always contains at least that state; only a closure of the empty set is empty.

TOC-U1-Q23NATmedium

An NFA has states {p, q, r}, start state p, final state {r} and the transitions below. Counting only the reachable subset states, how many states does the DFA produced by the subset construction have?

| 0 | 1 ------+---------+------ p | {p, q} | {p} q | ∅ | {r} r | {r} | {r}

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Subset construction: each DFA state is a set of NFA states, and the successor of a set is the union of the successors of its members.

Step by step.

  1. Start: {p}.
  2. {p} on 0 → {p,q}; on 1 → {p}.
  3. {p,q} on 0 → {p,q}; on 1 → {p} ∪ {r} = {p,r}.
  4. {p,r} on 0 → {p,q} ∪ {r} = {p,q,r}; on 1 → {p} ∪ {r} = {p,r}.
  5. {p,q,r} on 0 → {p,q,r}; on 1 → {p,r}. No new sets appear.
  6. Reachable subsets: {p}, {p,q}, {p,r}, {p,q,r} — four. (The ∅ state is never reached, since p is in every subset.)

Answer: 4.

Common misconception. Answering 3 (the NFA's state count) or 8 (the full 2³ bound). Only reachable subsets count, and here p survives every transition, so exactly four of the eight subsets appear.

TOC-U1-Q27MCQeasy

In the subset construction that converts an NFA with ε-transitions into an equivalent DFA, the start state of the DFA is:

  1. {q₀}
  2. ε-closure({q₀})
  3. the set of all states reachable from q₀ by any string
  4. the set of final states
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A state of the constructed DFA is a set of NFA states — all the states the NFA could be in. Before a single input symbol is read, the NFA may follow any number of ε-moves for free, so every state reachable that way is already possible.

Step by step.

  1. The NFA begins in q₀.
  2. It may take ε-moves without consuming input, so all of ε-closure({q₀}) is reachable at zero cost.
  3. Hence the DFA start state must be the whole set ε-closure({q₀}), not the singleton.
  4. The same closure is applied to the successor: δ_DFA(S, a) = ε-closure( ∪_{q ∈ S} δ(q, a) ).

Answer: Option (B) — ε-closure({q₀}).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) ignores the free ε-moves and would reject strings the NFA accepts through them.
  • (C) is the set of states reachable by arbitrary strings — a global property, not a start state; using it would make the DFA accept far too much.
  • (D) is not a start state at all; finality is decided afterwards by "does the set contain a final state?".

Common misconception. Treating ε-moves as if they consumed a symbol. They consume nothing, which is exactly why they must be folded into the state before any input is read.

Takeaway. Two rules carry the whole construction: start with ε-closure(q₀), and close every successor. A DFA state is final iff it contains an NFA final state.

TOC-U1-Q31MCQeasy

The class of languages accepted by nondeterministic finite automata with ε-transitions is:

  1. strictly larger than the class accepted by DFAs
  2. exactly the same as the class accepted by DFAs
  3. exactly the class of context-free languages
  4. incomparable with the class accepted by DFAs
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Nondeterminism lets a machine "guess", but a finite automaton has no memory to exploit the guess, so the extra freedom buys no extra power.

Step by step.

  1. Every DFA is trivially an NFA (a singleton transition set and no ε-moves), so DFA languages ⊆ NFA languages.
  2. Conversely, given any NFA the subset construction produces a DFA that accepts exactly the same language, so NFA languages ⊆ DFA languages.
  3. Adding ε-transitions is harmless: ε-closures absorb them into the subset construction.
  4. The two inclusions give equality; both classes are exactly the regular languages.

Answer: Option (B) — exactly the same class.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the natural but wrong guess: determinisation is always possible, only potentially expensive.
  • (C) overshoots: {aⁿbⁿ} is context-free but no finite automaton accepts it.
  • (D) is impossible — the two classes are related by inclusion in one direction.

Common misconception. Assuming nondeterminism adds power. For finite automata it does not; the leap in power happens only at the pushdown level, where NPDA ⊋ DFA.

Takeaway. NFA, ε-NFA, DFA and regular expressions all describe one class: the regular languages. They differ in succinctness, not in power.

TOC-U1-Q33MCQmedium

An NFA has n states, so the subset construction can produce up to 2ⁿ DFA states. Which of the following statements about this bound is TRUE?

  1. Some languages force exponentially many DFA states: for L_k = {w ∈ {0,1}* : the k-th symbol of w from the right is 1} every DFA needs 2ᵏ states, while an NFA with k + 1 states suffices.
  2. The bound can never be reached, because the empty set is never a state of the constructed DFA.
  3. The construction always produces exactly 2ⁿ states, whatever the NFA.
  4. A DFA obtained by the subset construction can never be minimised any further.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. DFA states are residuals: the DFA must remember exactly the information needed to decide every possible suffix. For L_k that information is the last k symbols, and there are 2ᵏ possibilities. An NFA can instead guess which symbol is the one k places from the end and verify it with a short chain.

Step by step.

  1. Two distinct strings u, v of length k differ in some position j. Appending the right suffix of length j exposes that position, so u and v are distinguishable.
  2. Hence all 2ᵏ strings of length k give distinct residuals, and every DFA for L_k has at least 2ᵏ states.
  3. An NFA does it with k + 1 states: loop on 0,1 at the start state, and on a 1 also branch into a chain of k states that must be walked to the end.
  4. So the gap between NFA and DFA size is genuinely exponential — the bound is not an artefact of the construction.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) fails: the empty set does appear whenever a subset has no successor on some symbol, so the bound is not reduced by it.
  • (C) confuses a worst case with an identity: most NFAs give far fewer than 2ⁿ subsets.
  • (D) fails: determinisation guarantees a DFA, not a minimal one — this paper's Q23 is exactly such a case, where the construction yields 4 states for a 3-state NFA.

Common misconception. Believing the subset construction gives the minimal DFA. It gives a DFA; minimisation is a separate step, and skipping it is a standard source of wrong state counts.

Takeaway. Nondeterminism is exponentially more succinct, not more powerful. When a question says "the DFA produced by the subset construction", count reachable subsets; when it says "the minimal DFA", minimise afterwards.

TOC-U1-Q39MCQhard

An NFA over Σ = {0, 1} has states {A, B, C, D}, start state A, final state {D} and the transitions below (∅ means no move). The minimal DFA equivalent to this NFA has how many states?

| 0 | 1 ------+---------+-------- A | {A} | {A, B} B | {C} | {C} C | {D} | {D} D | ∅ | ∅

  1. 4
  2. 6
  3. 8
  4. 16
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The NFA guesses when it has reached the third symbol from the end: at any 1 it may start a three-step chain, and it accepts only if exactly three symbols remain. So L = {w ∈ {0,1}* : the third symbol of w from the right is 1}.

Step by step.

  1. Trace the chain: from A on a 1 the NFA may move to B (one symbol consumed), then B → C (two), then C → D (three). D has no outgoing moves, so the run dies unless the input ends there.
  2. Hence acceptance needs the guessed 1 to be exactly the third symbol from the right.
  3. A DFA must remember the last three symbols, since each of them can matter. There are 2³ = 8 such windows, and any two different windows are distinguished by a suitable suffix of length at most 3 — so all eight residuals are distinct.
  4. The subset construction confirms it: the reachable subsets are {A}, {A,B}, {A,B,C}, {A,B,C,D}, {A,B,D}, {A,C}, {A,C,D}, {A,D} — exactly 8, and no two are equivalent.

Answer: Option (C) — 8 states.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 4 is the number of NFA states, not DFA states — determinisation can be exponentially larger.
  • (B) 6 would be the count if two of the eight windows collapsed; they do not, since each position of the window can independently be 0 or 1 and be exposed by a different suffix.
  • (D) 16 is 2⁴, the bound for a 4-state NFA; only 8 of the 16 subsets are reachable.

Common misconception. Answering with the NFA's state count, or with the worst-case 2ⁿ. The right number is the number of reachable subsets, then minimised — here 8, which happens to equal the worst case only because the language really needs every window.

Takeaway. "The k-th symbol from the right is 1" always costs 2ᵏ DFA states. Recognise the pattern and the count follows without drawing the table.

TOC-U1-Q42MSQmedium

Which of the following statements about ε-closures and the subset construction are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. ε-closure(q) always contains q itself.
  2. ε-closure is not transitive: ε-closure(ε-closure(S)) can be strictly larger than ε-closure(S).
  3. The subset construction always yields a DFA with exactly 2ⁿ states for an n-state NFA.
  4. Every state of the DFA produced by the subset construction is a subset of the NFA's state set.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. ε-closure(S) is the set of states reachable from S using ε-moves only. It is a closure operator: extensive, monotone and idempotent.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Zero ε-moves reach q, so q ∈ ε-closure(q). TRUE ✓
  2. (B) Closure is idempotent: everything reachable from ε-closure(S) by ε-moves was already reachable from S, because ε-moves compose. So the second application adds nothing. FALSE ✗
  3. (C) 2ⁿ is a worst case. Only reachable subsets become states, and many subsets are unreachable — this paper's Q23 builds 4 states from a 3-state NFA. FALSE ✗
  4. (D) Each DFA state is by definition a set of NFA states, so it is a subset of the NFA state set. TRUE ✓

Answer: Options (A) and (D).

Common misconception. Believing (B) — that closing a set twice can grow it. It cannot: closure is transitive by construction, which is why one application suffices at every step of the construction.

Takeaway. Closure is idempotent, and 2ⁿ is a bound, not a count. Both facts are needed to reason about determinisation without drawing tables.

TOC-U1-Q48NATeasy

An NFA has states {A, B, C}, start state A, final state {C} and the transitions below. Counting only reachable subset states, how many states does the DFA produced by the subset construction have?

| 0 | 1 ------+---------+------ A | {A, B} | {A} B | ∅ | {C} C | {C} | {C}

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Under the subset construction a DFA state is a set of NFA states. Start from the singleton start set, then repeatedly close each successor under the transition function; count only the sets that actually appear.

Step by step.

  1. Start: {A}. On 0 → {A, B}; on 1 → {A}.
  2. {A, B}: on 0 → {A, B} ∪ ∅ = {A, B}; on 1 → {A} ∪ {C} = {A, C}.
  3. {A, C}: on 0 → {A, B} ∪ {C} = {A, B, C}; on 1 → {A} ∪ {C} = {A, C}.
  4. {A, B, C}: on 0 → {A, B} ∪ ∅ ∪ {C} = {A, B, C}; on 1 → {A} ∪ {C} ∪ {C} = {A, C}.
  5. No new set appears, so the reachable subsets are {A}, {A, B}, {A, C}, {A, B, C} — four states. The final subsets are those containing C, namely {A, C} and {A, B, C}.

Answer: 4.

Common misconception. Answering 2³ = 8 by counting every subset. Three of the eight ({B}, {C}, {B, C}) are unreachable, so the construction never creates them. Equally common: dropping {A, B, C} because it "looks redundant" — it is reachable via {A, C} →0.

Takeaway. The subset construction is a reachability search over subsets. Enumerate successors until nothing new appears, and never assume the 2ⁿ bound is attained.

TOC-U1-Q53MCQeasy

An NFA over Σ = {a} has a single state q₀ that is both the start state and the only final state, with δ(q₀, a) = {q₀}. The language it accepts is:

  1. {ε}
  2. {a}
  3. a*
  4. a⁺
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Nondeterminism does not change the acceptance rule: a string is accepted if some run from the start state ends in a final state. Here there is only one run, and it exists for every string over {a}.

Step by step.

  1. On ε the run is empty and ends in q₀, which is final, so ε ∈ L.
  2. On aⁿ with n ≥ 1 the single run reads n symbols and returns to q₀ each time, ending final.
  3. No other strings exist over the one-letter alphabet, so L = a*.

Answer: Option (C) — a*.

Common misconception. Choosing a⁺ by assuming a non-empty string is needed. Because the start state is also final, the empty run already accepts ε; deleting the final mark from q₀ is what would turn a* into a⁺.

TOC-U1-Q60MCQeasy

An NFA over Σ = {a} has states {p, q}, start state p, final state {q}, and transitions δ(p, a) = {p, q}, δ(q, a) = {q}. The language it accepts is:

  1. {a}
  2. a⁺
  3. a*
  4. ∅
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In an NFA a symbol may lead to several states at once, and acceptance needs only one surviving path that ends final. The nondeterministic move p → q is a guess that "the string ends after this symbol".

Step by step.

  1. ε: the only run ends in p, which is not final, so ε ∉ L.
  2. a: the run may take p → q and stop in the final state q, so a ∈ L.
  3. aⁿ for n ≥ 1: stay in p for the first n − 1 symbols, then move to q on the last one, where the self-loop keeps the run alive. So every aⁿ with n ≥ 1 is accepted.
  4. Hence L = a⁺ = a·a*.

Answer: Option (B) — a⁺.

Common misconception. Answering a* by treating the NFA like a DFA whose start state is final. Here p is not final, so the empty string is rejected; making p final is exactly what would add ε.

TOC-U1-Q62MCQmedium

In the subset construction that converts an NFA N into an equivalent DFA D, which states of D are final?

  1. only the singleton sets {f} where f is final in N
  2. every subset that contains at least one final state of N
  3. the single subset consisting of all final states of N
  4. every subset that is contained in the set of final states of N
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A state of D records the set of all NFA states reachable on the input read so far. The NFA accepts if any one of those states is final, so the DFA must accept exactly when its set meets the final set.

Step by step.

  1. After reading w, the DFA is in the subset S = {q : some run of N on w ends in q}.
  2. w ∈ L(N) iff some run ends in a final state, that is iff S ∩ F ≠ ∅.
  3. So the final states of D are precisely the subsets that intersect F.
  4. Singletons are not enough: the subset {p, f} with f ∈ F is a perfectly ordinary DFA state, and it accepts.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Marking only the singleton {f} final. Because the machine tracks all live runs at once, a state such as {p, f} is final too; forgetting this is the classic way to build a DFA that rejects strings the NFA accepts.

TOC-U1-Q66MCQmedium

Which one of the following statements about nondeterministic finite automata is FALSE?

  1. An NFA may have several transitions on the same symbol from a single state.
  2. Every NFA has an equivalent DFA.
  3. A string is accepted if at least one computation path from the start state ends in a final state.
  4. A string is accepted only if every computation path from the start state ends in a final state.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Nondeterministic acceptance is existential: one successful run is enough, and dead or rejecting runs are simply ignored. Requiring all runs to succeed is a universal condition, which is not what an NFA does.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is true by definition of the transition relation δ : Q × Σ → 2^Q, which returns a set.
  2. (B) is true: the subset construction converts any NFA, ε-moves included, into an equivalent DFA with at most 2ⁿ states.
  3. (C) is exactly the acceptance rule.
  4. (D) is false. Take the NFA with δ(q₀, a) = {q₀, q₁} and q₁ final: the string a has one accepting run and one non-accepting run, and it is accepted.

Answer: Option (D).

Common misconception. Carrying the "all paths must work" intuition over from algorithm design. In automata theory nondeterminism means "a lucky guess suffices", which is why NFAs are no more powerful than DFAs.

Regular expressions · 18
TOC-U1-Q05MCQeasy

The language denoted by the regular expression (a+b)*abb is:

  1. the set of all strings over {a,b} that end in abb.
  2. the set of all strings over {a,b} that contain abb as a substring.
  3. the set of all strings over {a,b} that begin with abb.
  4. the set of all strings over {a,b} that contain exactly one occurrence of abb.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Concatenation in a regular expression fixes order: (a+b)* consumes an arbitrary prefix, and the trailing abb must be the last three symbols of the string.

Step by step.

  1. Any string of the form x·abb with x ∈ {a,b}* is generated: choose x from (a+b)* and append abb.
  2. Conversely, every generated string literally ends in abb.
  3. So the language is exactly the strings with suffix abb, e.g. abb, aabb, abbabb.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. (a+b)*abb(a+b)* — with the trailing star — is the "contains" language (option B). Dropping the final (a+b)* pins abb to the end.

TOC-U1-Q06MCQeasy

Which regular expression denotes exactly the set of all strings over {a,b} that contain at least one a?

  1. (a+b)*a(a+b)*
  2. a*
  3. (a+b)*
  4. b*ab*
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. "At least one a" is expressed by isolating one required a and letting arbitrary text surround it.

Step by step.

  1. (a+b)* before the required a generates any prefix.
  2. The single a is the witness that at least one a occurs.
  3. (a+b)* after it generates any suffix, including more a's.
  4. So (a+b)*a(a+b)* accepts a, ba, ab, aab, bbaab … and rejects only b*.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Option (D) b*ab* allows only one a; option (C) allows strings with no a at all; option (B) a* forces every symbol to be a.

TOC-U1-Q10MCQmedium

Taking a DFA to be complete (a transition is defined for every state and symbol), the minimal DFA accepting L(a(a+b)*) has how many states?

  1. 2
  2. 3
  3. 4
  4. 5
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. a(a+b)* is "every string that starts with a". Three facts must be remembered: nothing read yet, a good prefix, a hopeless prefix.

Step by step.

  1. q₀ (start, non-final): on a go to q₁, on b go to q_d.
  2. q₁ (final): the first symbol was a; every later symbol keeps the string valid, so δ(q₁,a) = δ(q₁,b) = q₁.
  3. q_d (dead, non-final): the first symbol was b; nothing can repair it, so δ(q_d,a) = δ(q_d,b) = q_d.
  4. The three states are reachable and pairwise distinguishable (q₀ and q_d differ on a; q₁ is the only final state), so 3 is minimal.

Answer: Option (B) — 3 states.

Common misconception. Answering 2, which is correct only for an incomplete transition graph where q_d is simply omitted. GATE convention uses complete DFAs, and the minimal complete DFA has 3 states.

TOC-U1-Q14MCQhard

Which regular expression denotes exactly the strings over {a,b} that contain an odd number of a's?

  1. b*(ab*ab*)*ab*
  2. b*(ab*a)*b*
  3. (a+b)*a(a+b)*
  4. a(a+b)*
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Read a regular expression as a machine: the part ab*ab* contains exactly two a's, so repeating it adds an even number of a's; a final a outside the loop makes the total odd.

Step by step.

  1. b* before the loop and b* inside it absorb all b's anywhere.
  2. Each iteration of (ab*ab*) contributes exactly two a's.
  3. The trailing ab* contributes exactly one a.
  4. Total = 2k + 1, odd — and every odd-a string can be parsed this way, so the expression is exact.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Option (B) b*(ab*a)*b* generates an even number of a's (including none), i.e. it is the parity-dual language. Options (C) and (D) only say "at least one a".

TOC-U1-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following regular-expression identities hold for all strings over {a,b}?

  1. (a+b)* = (a*b*)*
  2. (ab)* = a*b*
  3. (a+b)* = (a*+b*)*
  4. a*b* = (a+b)*
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Identities are settled by proving both inclusions, or by producing one string that separates the two sides.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true. (a*b*)* contains every string: write it as blocks of a's then blocks of b's, e.g. ba = (ε)(b)·(a)(ε) is two iterations. Conversely every generated string is over {a,b}.
  2. (B) false. a ∈ a*b* but a ∉ (ab)*; equally b ∈ a*b* ∖ (ab)*.
  3. (C) true. a* ⊆ (a*+b*)* and b* ⊆ (a*+b*)*, and the star of a union of subsets of {a,b}* stays inside (a+b)*; so both sides equal {a,b}*.
  4. (D) false. ba ∈ (a+b)* but ba ∉ a*b*, since a*b* forces all a's before all b's.

Answer: Options (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Assuming that (ab)* and a*b* are interchangeable because both "use only a and b". Order matters: a*b* is closed under concatenation in a fixed shape, (ab)* alternates.

TOC-U1-Q24NATmedium

How many strings of length exactly 4 over {a,b} belong to L((a+b)*ab(a+b)*)?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. (a+b)*ab(a+b)* is the set of strings containing ab. Counting the complement is faster than listing members.

Step by step.

  1. There are 2⁴ = 16 strings of length 4 in total.
  2. A string avoids ab exactly when no a is ever followed by a b, i.e. the string has the shape b*a*: all b's first, then all a's.
  3. The b*a* strings of length 4 are bbbb, bbba, bbaa, baaa, aaaa — five of them.
  4. So the count is 16 − 5 = 11. Check by listing: abab, abba, abbb, aabb, aaba, baab — every length-4 string except the five listed above.

Answer: 11.

Common misconception. Counting strings that end in ab (which gives 4), or forgetting that ab may occur more than once and must not be double-counted — the complement count avoids both traps.

TOC-U1-Q28MCQeasy

The regular expression a*b* over Σ = {a, b} denotes exactly:

  1. all strings with equal numbers of a and b
  2. all strings in which no a occurs after a b
  3. all strings that begin with a and end with b
  4. all strings of even length
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Concatenation runs left to right and is not commutative: a*b* first consumes a (possibly empty) block of a's, then a block of b's, and stops. Once the machine has moved into the b* part it can never return.

Step by step.

  1. Take any string with no a after a b. It has the shape a…a b…b, possibly with one of the blocks empty.
  2. That is precisely a block of a's followed by a block of b's.
  3. So the expression matches it.
  4. Conversely every string matched by a*b* has this shape, so no a follows a b.

Answer: Option (B) — strings with no a after a b.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) ab has equal counts, but so does ba, which is not in a*b*.
  • (C) the empty string ε belongs to a*b* but begins with nothing.
  • (D) a has odd length and is accepted; length is unconstrained.

Common misconception. Reading a*b* as "any mix of a's and b's". That would be (a + b)*. The absence of parentheses and a union matters.

Takeaway. a*b* is the language with at most one "boundary" between the two letters. Read a concatenation as a sequence of phases, not as a set of allowed characters.

TOC-U1-Q32MCQmedium

Which regular expression denotes exactly the strings over {a, b} that contain an even number of b's?

  1. (a*ba*ba*)*a*
  2. (a*b)*
  3. (ba*ba*)*
  4. (a*ba*b)*
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Counting modulo 2 is a regular condition: a DFA with two states, "even so far" and "odd so far", decides it. As a regular expression the b's must be consumed two at a time, with a's allowed anywhere.

Step by step.

  1. Pair the b's: a unit that adds two b's is a*ba*ba*. Reading it, we take some a's, one b, more a's, a second b, and some trailing a's.
  2. Any number of such units gives (a*ba*ba*)*, which covers all even-b strings except those with zero b's that end in a.
  3. A trailing a* covers that gap: with zero units the word is a block of a's, and with units the trailing a's of the last unit are already allowed.
  4. So the answer is (a*ba*ba*)*a*. Check: ε ✓, a ✓, bb ✓, bab ✓, ab ✗ (one b).

Answer: Option (A) — (a*ba*ba*)*a*.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) (a*b)* accepts ab, which has one b.
  • (C) (ba*ba*)* rejects a and abb, both of which have an even number of b's. The missing leading and trailing a* is the whole defect.
  • (D) (a*ba*b)* pairs only one b per unit, so it accepts ab.

Common misconception. Writing (a*ba*ba*)* and stopping there. It matches every even-b string that ends in b but rejects a, baa and the empty string — the "zero pairs" case needs its own slack.

Takeaway. For a modulo-k count, write a unit that adds exactly k occurrences, star it, and add slack for the zero-unit case. Verify against ε and a one-letter word before committing.

TOC-U1-Q36MCQmedium

The regular expression (a + b)*a(a + b)*a(a + b)* denotes exactly the strings over {a, b} that contain:

  1. exactly two a's
  2. at least two a's
  3. an even number of a's
  4. two consecutive a's
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a regular expression each concatenated factor must be matched, in order. Two explicit a's are concatenated, with (a + b)* allowed before, between and after them.

Step by step.

  1. The first (a + b)* consumes any prefix.
  2. Then one a must be read — the first witness.
  3. Then any middle string, then a second a — the second witness.
  4. Then any suffix.
  5. So a string is accepted exactly when it contains two a's in that order, i.e. at least two a's. A third or fourth a is simply swallowed by the surrounding (a + b)* factors.

Answer: Option (B) — at least two a's.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) aaa is accepted, so "exactly two" fails.
  • (C) aaa has three a's and is accepted; parity is not tested.
  • (D) aba is accepted although the two a's are separated.

Common misconception. Reading the sandwiched (a + b)* as forcing a gap. It is allowed to be empty, so aa is accepted — the two witnesses may be adjacent.

Takeaway. (a + b)*X(a + b)*Y(a + b)* means "contains X then Y somewhere". Count the mandatory letters, not the star factors.

TOC-U1-Q43MSQmedium

Which of the following regular-expression identities hold for all strings over Σ = {a, b}? (Select all that apply.)

  1. (a*b)* = a*b*
  2. (ab)*a = a(ba)*
  3. (a + b)* = (a*b*)*
  4. (ab + ba)* denotes exactly the strings with equal numbers of a's and b's
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two expressions are equal exactly when they accept the same set of strings. To refute an identity one separating string is enough; to establish one, argue over all strings.

Step by step.

  1. (A) (a*b)* allows several a*b units, so ba is in it (take the units b then a). But a*b* has no a after a b. FALSE ✗ — separated by ba.
  2. (B) Both sides describe an odd-length alternation that starts and ends with a: (ab)*a reads pairs ab then one a, and a(ba)* reads one a then pairs ba. The same strings, regrouped. TRUE ✓
  3. (C) (a*b*)* concatenates blocks of a's and b's, which can produce any string over {a,b}; conversely any string splits into such blocks. So it equals (a + b)*. TRUE ✓
  4. (D) aabb has two a's and two b's, but every factor of (ab + ba)* alternates, so no accepted string contains aa. FALSE ✗

Answer: Options (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Treating (a*b)* as "a's then b's, repeated" and therefore equal to a*b*. Repetition breaks the phase structure: the second unit may restart with b's after a's, which is how ba gets in.

Takeaway. Test identities on short strings first — ε, then all words of length 1 and 2. Most false identities die at ba, ab or aabb.

TOC-U1-Q49NATmedium

How many strings of length exactly 5 over Σ = {a, b} contain no two consecutive a's?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. "No aa" is a regular condition decided by the last symbol only, so the strings are counted by a two-case recurrence — equivalently they are exactly the strings matched by (b + ab)*(a + ε).

Step by step.

  1. Let f(n) be the number of valid strings of length n.
  2. A valid string of length n either ends in b (any valid prefix of length n − 1) or ends in ab (any valid prefix of length n − 2).
  3. So f(n) = f(n − 1) + f(n − 2), with f(0) = 1 (ε) and f(1) = 2 (a, b).
  4. Values: f(2) = 3, f(3) = 5, f(4) = 8, f(5) = 13.

Answer: 13.

Common misconception. Counting by subtracting "contains aa" carelessly and double-counting strings with several aa blocks. The recurrence avoids inclusion–exclusion altogether because the forbidden pattern is adjacent-only.

Takeaway. f(n) = f(n − 1) + f(n − 2) with f(0) = 1, f(1) = 2 gives 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 13, 21, … — the Fibonacci numbers shifted by one. Memorise the pair (f(0), f(1)) so you never start the sequence one place off.

TOC-U1-Q50NATmedium

How many strings of length exactly 6 over Σ = {a, b} contain an even number of a's?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The set of strings of length n with an even number of a's is the language of (a*ba*ba*)*a* restricted to length n. Counting it means choosing which positions hold the a's.

Step by step.

  1. A string of length 6 is fixed once the positions of its a's are chosen; the rest are b's.
  2. The number of a's must be even: 0, 2, 4 or 6.
  3. Count each case: C(6,0) + C(6,2) + C(6,4) + C(6,6) = 1 + 15 + 15 + 1.
  4. Total = 32, which is exactly half of 2⁶ = 64 — as it must be, since flipping the first symbol pairs every even-a string with an odd-a one.

Answer: 32.

Common misconception. Counting only C(6,2) + C(6,4) = 30 and forgetting the two extreme cases 0 and 6. Zero is even, and the all-b string is a perfectly good member.

Takeaway. For a parity condition on one letter, exactly half of the 2ⁿ strings qualify. Use that as a sanity check on the binomial sum, and remember that 0 is even.

TOC-U1-Q54MCQeasy

Which regular expression denotes exactly the set of strings over Σ = {a, b} of length exactly 2?

  1. (a + b)*
  2. (a + b)(a + b)
  3. aa + bb
  4. (ab)*
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Concatenation of two factors that each denote a single symbol forces exactly two symbols; the star, not the concatenation, is what allows a variable length.

Step by step.

  1. (a + b) denotes the two one-letter strings a and b.
  2. Concatenating it with itself gives {aa, ab, ba, bb} — every string of length 2 and no other.
  3. (a + b)* adds ε and all longer strings, so it is far too big.
  4. aa + bb keeps only two of the four strings, and (ab)* gives {ε, ab, abab, …}.

Answer: Option (B) — (a + b)(a + b).

Common misconception. Writing (a + b)² as (a + b)*, or thinking that a union must be repeated. A union inside a factor gives a choice at one position; two factors give two independent choices.

TOC-U1-Q55MCQeasy

The regular expression (ab)* over Σ = {a, b} denotes:

  1. all strings with equal numbers of a and b
  2. the strings ε, ab, abab, ababab, … and nothing else
  3. all strings of even length
  4. all strings that end in b
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A star repeats a fixed block. Here the block is the two-letter word ab, so every member is a whole number of copies of it.

Step by step.

  1. Zero repetitions give ε.
  2. One repetition gives ab; two give abab; in general (ab)ⁿ for n ≥ 0.
  3. aabb has equal counts but is not a repetition of ab, so the first option fails.
  4. aa has even length but no b, so the third fails; bb ends in b but is not in the language, so the fourth fails.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Reading (ab)* as "any arrangement of equal numbers of a and b". Equality of counts would need (ab + ba)*-style gymnastics at best and is in fact not regular; the block inside a star keeps the order fixed.

TOC-U1-Q59NATeasy

How many strings of length exactly 3 over Σ = {a, b} belong to L(a*b*)?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. a*b* contains exactly the strings in which no a occurs after a b: some a's, then some b's, either block possibly empty.

Step by step.

  1. A word of length 3 in the language is aⁱbʲ with i + j = 3.
  2. The possible pairs are (3,0), (2,1), (1,2), (0,3).
  3. They give aaa, aab, abb, bbb — four distinct strings.
  4. The remaining four strings of length 3 — aba, baa, bab, bba — are outside the language, because each of them has an a after a b.

Answer: 4.

Common misconception. Counting a*b* as if the two stars were independent choices at each position, which would give all 2³ = 8 strings. The order is fixed: the a-block comes first.

TOC-U1-Q63MCQmedium

Which regular expression denotes exactly the strings over Σ = {a, b} that contain no two consecutive b's?

  1. (a*b)*a*
  2. (a + b)*bb(a + b)*
  3. b*a*
  4. (ab)*
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A forbidden pattern is easiest to exclude by describing the string as a sequence of blocks in which the forbidden pattern cannot occur. If every b is followed by at least one a — except possibly at the very end — no two b's can be adjacent.

Step by step.

  1. Write any bb-free string as a^{i₁}b a^{i₂}b … a^{i_k}b a^{i_{k+1}}, where each block a^{i_j}b for j ≤ k is matched by one factor of (a*b) and the trailing a^{i_{k+1}} by the final a*.
  2. Conversely, every string of (a*b)*a* is a concatenation of blocks each ending in a single b, followed by a's; two b's from different blocks are separated by the next block's a's, so bb never appears.
  3. So the two descriptions agree.
  4. The others fail: (a+b)*bb(a+b)* describes the strings that do contain bb; b*a* forbids any a after a b; (ab)* omits ba, which is bb-free.

Answer: Option (A) — (a*b)*a*.

Common misconception. Reading a star as if it allowed any interleaving, so that (a*b)* is taken to be (a+b)*. It is not: every factor must end in b, which is exactly the structural fact that rules out bb.

TOC-U1-Q67MSQmedium

Which of the following regular expressions denote exactly the set of strings over Σ = {a, b} that end in b? (Select all that apply.)

  1. (a + b)*b
  2. (a*b*)*b
  3. b(a + b)*
  4. (a + b)*b(a + b)*
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. "Ends in b" is a suffix condition: an arbitrary prefix followed by one forced final symbol. Whether the prefix is written as (a + b)* or in some equivalent form is irrelevant, provided the form still denotes all of Σ*.

Step by step.

  1. (a + b)*b: every string is a prefix followed by b, so the condition holds exactly.
  2. (a*b*)* = Σ*: every string splits into a sequence of a-then-b blocks, so the star of the union of the two blocks covers everything. Hence (a*b*)*b = Σ*b, the same language.
  3. b(a + b)* forces the b to come first: it describes the strings that start with b, and it rejects ab.
  4. (a + b)*b(a + b)* allows symbols after the b: it describes the strings that contain b, and it accepts ba.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Rejecting (B) because a*b* alone is a proper subset of Σ*. The outer star repairs that: repeating the blocks in any order generates every string.

TOC-U1-Q72MCQhard

Which regular expression denotes exactly the strings over Σ = {a, b} that do not contain the substring ab?

  1. b*a*
  2. a*b*
  3. (ba)*
  4. (a*b)*
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The forbidden pattern ab is exactly "an a followed later by a b". Banning it means every b must precede every a, so the word is some b's followed by some a's.

Step by step.

  1. If w contains no ab, then once a b has been read no a may follow, so w = bʲaⁱ.
  2. Conversely every bʲaⁱ has all its b's before all its a's, so no a is ever followed by a b.
  3. Therefore the language is b*a*.
  4. The others fail: a*b* contains ab itself; (ba)* omits baa, which has no ab; and (a*b)* = Σ* because ab = (a*b) is a single factor, so it contains everything.

Answer: Option (A) — b*a*.

Common misconception. Choosing a*b* by pattern-matching on the letters in the order they appear in the forbidden word. The condition is about what may not follow, so the a-block has to come last, not first.

Pumping lemma for regular languages · 16
TOC-U1-Q08MCQeasy

The pumping lemma for regular languages is used to:

  1. prove that certain languages are not regular.
  2. prove that a given language is regular.
  3. minimise a given DFA.
  4. convert an NFA into an equivalent DFA.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The lemma is a necessary condition: every regular language satisfies it. Therefore failing it proves non-regularity, while satisfying it proves nothing.

Step by step.

  1. If L is regular with a DFA of p states, every w ∈ L with |w| ≥ p can be split w = xyz with |xy| ≤ p, |y| ≥ 1 and xy^i z ∈ L for all i ≥ 0.
  2. So a language that violates this for every valid split is not regular.
  3. No converse is available: {a^n b^n : n ≥ 0} fails it, but a non-regular language can still satisfy it.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Using the pumping lemma to claim a language is regular (option B). To prove regularity you must exhibit a DFA, an NFA or a regular expression.

TOC-U1-Q11MCQmedium

To prove that L = {a^n b^n : n ≥ 0} is not regular using the pumping lemma, which argument is correct?

  1. Assume the pumping length is p and take w = a^p b^p. Then |xy| ≤ p forces y = a^k with k ≥ 1, so xy²z = a^{p+k} b^p ∉ L.
  2. Assume the pumping length is p and take w = (ab)^p. Pumping y = ab gives (ab)^{p+1} ∉ L.
  3. Assume the pumping length is p and take w = a^p b^p. Then y must contain both an a and a b, so xy²z ∉ L.
  4. Assume the pumping length is p and take w = b^p a^p. Then y = a^k gives xy²z ∉ L.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A valid pumping-lemma proof must pick one string w ∈ L with |w| ≥ p such that every legal split w = xyz yields a pumped string outside L.

Step by step.

  1. Choose w = a^p b^p ∈ L; |w| = 2p ≥ p.
  2. |xy| ≤ p confines xy to the leading block of a's, so y = a^k for some k ≥ 1.
  3. Then xy²z = a^{p+k} b^p, which has more a's than b's and is not in L — contradiction for every legal split.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Option (B) fails because the lemma does not let you choose y: with w = (ab)^p a legal split may take y = a, which pumps into a string still in L. Option (C) misreads |xy| ≤ p: y cannot cross into the b block. Option (D) uses a string of the wrong shape (b^p a^p ∉ L).

TOC-U1-Q15MCQhard

For L = {a^i b^j : i > j ≥ 0}, a pumping-lemma proof takes w = a^{p+1} b^p. With w = xyz, |xy| ≤ p, |y| ≥ 1, the contradiction appears for which choice of the pump count i in xy^i z?

  1. i = 0
  2. i = 2
  3. i = 3
  4. every i ≥ 0 gives a contradiction
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. L only requires more a's than b's, so pumping up preserves membership; only pumping down can break it.

Step by step.

  1. w = a^{p+1} b^p ∈ L because p+1 > p; also |w| ≥ p.
  2. |xy| ≤ p forces y = a^k with 1 ≤ k ≤ p, so no b is inside y.
  3. For i ≥ 1: xy^i z = a^{p+1+(i-1)k} b^p, which still has more a's than b's and lies in L — no contradiction.
