  4. For i = 0: xy⁰z = a^{p+1-k} b^p with p+1-k ≤ p, so the number of a's no longer exceeds the number of b's and xy⁰z ∉ L — contradiction.

Answer: Option (A) — pump down, i = 0.

Common misconception. Pumping up by reflex (i = 2) is the standard habit for {a^n b^n} and fails here, because this language tolerates extra a's. When a language is one-sided, always check whether pumping down is the move that breaks it.

TOC-U1-Q19MSQeasy

Using the Myhill–Nerode theorem, which statements are TRUE?

  1. L is regular if and only if ≡_L has finitely many equivalence classes.
  2. If ≡_L has infinitely many equivalence classes, then L is not regular.
  3. The index of ≡_L equals the number of states of every DFA that accepts L.
  4. Every finite language is regular, so its ≡_L has finitely many classes.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Myhill–Nerode turns a language into a right-invariant equivalence: x ≡_L y iff for every z, xz ∈ L ⇔ yz ∈ L. The number of classes is the index.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true. This is the theorem: a finite-index right congruence yields a DFA whose states are the classes, and a DFA with n states yields at most n classes.
  2. (B) true. The contrapositive of the forward direction of (A).
  3. (C) false. The index equals the number of states of the minimal DFA only. A non-minimal DFA has more states and the same index.
  4. (D) true. A finite language is accepted by a finite trie-like DFA, so it is regular, and by (A) its index is finite.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Reading (C) as "some DFA" — the index is an invariant of the language, so it can only match the minimal machine.

TOC-U1-Q20MSQhard

Which of the following languages over {a,b} are NOT regular?

  1. {a^n b^n : n ≥ 0}
  2. {a^n b^m : n, m ≥ 0}
  3. {ww : w ∈ {a,b}*}
  4. {a^n : n is prime}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A language that needs an unbounded counter — equal counts, a duplicated block, or primality — is not regular; a language needing only a bounded amount of state is.

Step by step.

  1. (A) not regular. w = a^p b^p with y = a^k pumps to a^{p+k}b^p ∉ L.
  2. (B) regular. a*b* describes it exactly — the two counts are independent, so no counter is needed.
  3. (C) not regular. Take w = a^p b a^p b; a legal split keeps y inside the first a^p block, and xy²z no longer has two identical halves.
  4. (D) not regular. Take w = a^p with p prime; y = a^k gives a^{p + (i-1)k}, which for i = p+1 equals a^{p(1+k)} — composite. (Myhill–Nerode gives the same answer: the strings a^n with n prime are pairwise inequivalent.)

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D) — three non-regular languages.

Common misconception. Treating (C) as regular because it "looks like a star", or treating (B) as non-regular because it mentions two letters. Independence of the two counts is the deciding point.

TOC-U1-Q25NATmedium

How many of the following six languages over {a,b} are NOT regular?

(i) { a^n b^n : n >= 0 } (ii) { w : #a(w) = #b(w) } (iii) { a^n b^m : n, m >= 0 } (iv) { ww : w in {a,b}* } (v) { w : w = w^R } (palindromes) (vi) { w : #a(w) is even }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Regular languages cannot compare two unbounded counts, cannot remember an arbitrary block in order to repeat it, and cannot test arithmetic properties such as primality. Parity, however, is a bounded amount of state.

Step by step.

  1. (i) not regular. a^n b^n needs an unbounded counter; the pumping lemma with w = a^p b^p gives a^{p+k}b^p ∉ L.
  2. (ii) not regular. #a = #b again compares two unbounded counts; pump w = a^p b^p.
  3. (iii) regular. The expression a*b* describes it exactly.
  4. (iv) not regular. The copy language ww needs unbounded memory of the first half.
  5. (v) not regular. Palindromes need unbounded memory to compare the two halves.
  6. (vi) regular. Two states remember the parity of the number of a's.

Answer: 4 — the non-regular ones are (i), (ii), (iv) and (v).

Common misconception. Marking (iii) as non-regular because it "looks like" (i); the difference is that n and m are independent, so no comparison is required. Others mark (vi) non-regular, forgetting that parity needs only two states.

TOC-U1-Q29MCQeasy

The pumping lemma for regular languages states that for every regular language L there is a constant p ≥ 1 such that every w ∈ L with |w| ≥ p can be written w = xyz satisfying:

  1. |xy| ≤ p, |y| ≥ 1 and xyⁱz ∈ L for every i ≥ 0
  2. |xy| ≤ p, |y| ≥ 1 and xyz ∈ L only
  3. |y| ≤ p, |x| ≥ 1 and xyⁱz ∈ L for every i ≥ 0
  4. |xyz| ≤ p and xyⁱz ∈ L for every i ≥ 0
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A DFA with p states must repeat a state while reading the first p symbols, and the loop between the two visits to that state is the pumpable block y.

Step by step.

  1. Take a DFA for L with p states and a word w of length at least p.
  2. The first p + 1 configurations (before reading anything, then after each of the first p symbols) must repeat a state.
  3. The block between the repeats is y, with |y| ≥ 1, and it lies entirely inside the first p symbols, so |xy| ≤ p.
  4. Traversing that loop zero, one, two, … times leaves the DFA in the same state, so xyⁱz ∈ L for every i ≥ 0.

Answer: Option (A) — |xy| ≤ p, |y| ≥ 1, xyⁱz ∈ L for all i ≥ 0.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) states no pumping at all — it is just the hypothesis w ∈ L.
  • (C) swaps the bounds: it is xy that must fit in the first p symbols, not y alone bounded by p with |x| ≥ 1.
  • (D) bounds the whole word by p, which would make the lemma useless — every long word is a counterexample.

Common misconception. Confusing |xy| ≤ p with |xyz| ≤ p. The first confines the pumpable block to the prefix; the second is not part of the lemma.

Takeaway. Memorise the triple |xy| ≤ p, |y| ≥ 1, xyⁱz ∈ L ∀i ≥ 0. The bound on xy is the part that makes proofs work.

TOC-U1-Q35MCQmedium

To show that L = {0ⁿ1ⁿ : n ≥ 0} is not regular, a proof takes the pumping length p and the word w = 0ᵖ1ᵖ = xyz with |xy| ≤ p and |y| ≥ 1. Which of the following is then necessarily TRUE?

  1. y consists only of 0's
  2. y consists only of 1's
  3. y = 0ᵖ
  4. y may contain both 0's and 1's
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The bound |xy| ≤ p says the whole prefix xy lies within the first p symbols of the word. In 0ᵖ1ᵖ those first p symbols are all 0's.

Step by step.

  1. The word is 0ᵖ followed by 1ᵖ; the first p positions hold only 0's and position p + 1 is the first 1.
  2. |xy| ≤ p forces xy to end at or before position p, so both x and y consist entirely of 0's.
  3. |y| ≥ 1 then makes y a nonempty block of 0's.
  4. Pumping down with i = 0 removes y, giving 0^(p − |y|) 1ᵖ, which is not of the form 0ⁿ1ⁿ — the contradiction.

Answer: Option (A) — y is a nonempty block of 0's.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) would place y inside the 1-block, beyond position p, which |xy| ≤ p forbids.
  • (C) would need x = ε and |y| = p; nothing forces y to be the whole block.
  • (D) is impossible: a block spanning both letters would have to include position p + 1, so |xy| > p.

Common misconception. Forgetting that the bound is on xy, not on y alone, and then wondering whether y could be 1's. The bound is what pins it down, and it is the single most useful fact in these proofs.

Takeaway. Choose w so that |xy| ≤ p traps y in one homogeneous block. Then pumping changes one letter count and not the other, and the contradiction writes itself.

TOC-U1-Q37MCQhard

Let L = {w ∈ {a, b}* : #a(w) = 2·#b(w)}. A pumping-lemma proof takes the pumping length p and the word w = a²ᵖbᵖ = xyz with |xy| ≤ p and |y| ≥ 1, so that y = aᵏ for some 1 ≤ k ≤ p. Which of the following is TRUE?

  1. Both xy⁰z and xy²z lie outside L.
  2. xy⁰z lies in L but xy²z does not.
  3. xy²z lies in L but xy⁰z does not.
  4. xyⁱz lies in L for every i ≥ 0.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. L fixes a ratio between two letter counts, so it is not regular: a finite automaton cannot hold an unbounded count. In the proof, |xy| ≤ p traps y inside the a-block, so pumping changes only the number of a's and leaves the number of b's fixed.

Step by step.

  1. Write y = aᵏ with 1 ≤ k ≤ p.
  2. After pumping, xyⁱz = a^(2p + (i − 1)k) bᵖ, so #a = 2p + (i − 1)k and #b = p.
  3. Membership needs #a = 2·#b, i.e. 2p + (i − 1)k = 2p, i.e. (i − 1)k = 0.
  4. Since k ≥ 1, this holds exactly when i = 1. Every other value of i leaves the word outside L — in particular i = 0 and i = 2.

Answer: Option (A) — both xy⁰z and xy²z are outside L.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) claims xy⁰z ∈ L; with i = 0 the word is a^(2p − k)bᵖ and 2p − k ≠ 2p for k ≥ 1.
  • (C) claims xy²z ∈ L; there #a = 2p + k > 2p = 2·#b.
  • (D) is the pumping conclusion, which would mean L is pumpable as stated — it is exactly what the contradiction refutes.

Common misconception. Picking i = 2 on the instinct that "more a's must still be fine". Here the ratio is exact, so both directions break it; the algebra (i − 1)k = 0 is the reliable way to see that only i = 1 survives.

Takeaway. With a counting-ratio language, write the pumped word symbolically and solve for i. Do not guess: one value of i often keeps the ratio, and that is the whole point.

TOC-U1-Q40MCQhard

Which one of the following statements about the pumping lemma for regular languages is TRUE?

  1. Every non-regular language fails the pumping lemma.
  2. A language that satisfies the pumping lemma is necessarily regular.
  3. The pumping lemma gives a necessary condition for regularity, so satisfying it does not prove that a language is regular.
  4. The pumping lemma applies only to infinite languages.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The lemma says: L regular ⟹ L is pumpable. Read as an implication, it is a one-way tool. Its contrapositive — not pumpable ⟹ not regular — is what makes it useful.

Step by step.

  1. "Regular ⟹ pumpable" means pumpability is necessary for regularity.
  2. A necessary condition cannot certify regularity: satisfying it is consistent with being non-regular.
  3. The contrapositive certifies non-regularity, and that is the only direction the lemma gives.
  4. There are indeed non-regular languages that are pumpable — for example {aⁱbʲcᵏ : i = 1 ⟹ j = k} — which is exactly why the converse fails.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the converse-style error: some non-regular languages do satisfy the lemma, so "every" is false.
  • (B) is the converse itself, and it fails for the same reason.
  • (D) is false: a finite language is regular and satisfies the lemma trivially by taking p larger than its longest word, which makes the hypothesis |w| ≥ p vacuous.

Common misconception. Treating the pumping lemma as a characterisation of regular languages. It is one of the standard "necessary but not sufficient" conditions GATE likes to test.

Takeaway. Pumping lemma: disproves regularity only. Myhill–Nerode: decides regularity in both directions. Use the right tool for the direction you need.

TOC-U1-Q44MSQeasy

Which of the following statements are TRUE? (Select all that apply.)

  1. If L is regular then some pumping length p exists for L.
  2. To prove that L is not regular it suffices to show that for every p there is a word w ∈ L with |w| ≥ p that cannot be pumped.
  3. If L satisfies the pumping lemma then L is regular.
  4. The pumping lemma is used to prove that a language is regular.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The pumping lemma is an implication: regular ⟹ pumpable. It can be used in the direction of its contrapositive and in no other.

Step by step.

  1. (A) This is the statement of the lemma itself. TRUE ✓
  2. (B) "For every p some long word fails to pump" is the negation of "there exists p such that every long word pumps", which is the lemma's conclusion. Negating a necessary condition refutes regularity. TRUE ✓
  3. (C) The converse fails: pumpable non-regular languages exist. FALSE ✗
  4. (D) The lemma can only rule regularity out, never establish it. FALSE ✗

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Using the lemma as a sufficient condition. Candidates who prove "this language pumps" and conclude "so it is regular" lose the mark every time.

Takeaway. Pumping lemma: one direction only, and it points away from regularity. Note also that the quantifier order matters — ∀p ∃w is what refutes, not ∃w ∀p.

TOC-U1-Q45MSQhard

Which of the following languages over Σ = {a, b} are NOT regular? (Select all that apply.)

  1. {a²ⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 0}
  2. {w : #a(w) > #b(w)}
  3. {aⁿbᵐ : n, m ≥ 0 and n + m is odd}
  4. {aⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 0} ∪ {a}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A language that requires an unbounded count, or a comparison between two unbounded counts, is not regular. Regular languages are closed under intersection with a regular set and under union with a finite set, so both operations are useful in proofs.

Step by step.

  1. (A) {a²ⁿbⁿ} compares two unbounded counts in a fixed ratio 2 : 1. Pumping with |xy| ≤ p traps y in the a-block, so pumping changes the a-count but not the b-count and the ratio breaks. NOT regular ✓
  2. (B) {w : #a(w) > #b(w)} compares two unbounded counts. If it were regular, then L ∩ a*b* = {aⁿbᵐ : n > m} would also be regular (closure under intersection), but pumping shows it is not. NOT regular ✓
  3. (C) The condition n + m odd depends only on |w|, so the language is a*b* ∩ {w : |w| is odd} — the intersection of two regular sets. Regular ✗
  4. (D) Adding the single word a to the non-regular {aⁿbⁿ} leaves it non-regular: if the union were regular, intersecting it with a*b* (and removing a, a finite set) would make {aⁿbⁿ} regular. NOT regular ✓

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Thinking a finite union can "repair" a non-regular language, or that a parity condition makes a language irregular. Parity of length is about as regular as a condition gets; it is the comparison of counts that breaks regularity.

Takeaway. Two questions decide most cases: does the language force an unbounded count, and does it compare two of them? Then use closure under intersection with a*b* to strip away everything else.

TOC-U1-Q56MCQeasy

A complete DFA with n states accepts the regular language L. In a pumping-lemma proof for L, the pumping constant p may always be taken as:

  1. n
  2. n + 1
  3. 2ⁿ
  4. n²
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The constant in the pumping lemma is the number of states of a DFA for the language: reading p symbols visits p + 1 states, so a repeat is unavoidable and the loop can be pumped.

Step by step.

  1. Let M have n states and let w = a₁a₂…a_m ∈ L with m ≥ n.
  2. The run q₀, q₁, …, q_m has m + 1 ≥ n + 1 states, so two of q₀, …, q_n coincide.
  3. Choose i < j ≤ n with q_i = q_j, set x = a₁…a_i, y = a_{i+1}…a_j, z = a_{j+1}…a_m.
  4. Then |y| = j − i ≥ 1 and |xy| = j ≤ n, and the loop at q_i can be traversed any number of times, so xyⁱz ∈ L for every i ≥ 0.

Answer: Option (A) — p = n.

Common misconception. Believing the constant must be a tight "magic number" for the language. Any value at least as large as the number of states works; what matters is that the constant exists and is independent of the word being pumped.

TOC-U1-Q64MCQmedium

Which one of the following languages over Σ = {a, b} is regular, even though its definition involves counting?

  1. {w : #a(w) = #b(w)}
  2. {aⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 0}
  3. {w : #a(w) − #b(w) ≡ 0 (mod 3)}
  4. {ww : w ∈ {a, b}*}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A DFA can count only up to a fixed modulus. "Equal counts" is a comparison of two unbounded numbers; "counts equal modulo 3" is a three-valued residue, and three values fit in three states.

Step by step.

  1. Track r = (#a − #b) mod 3, which lies in {0, 1, 2}. Reading a does r ↦ (r + 1) mod 3; reading b does r ↦ (r − 1) mod 3.
  2. Start at r = 0, which is also the only final state. Three states, complete transitions, so the language is regular.
  3. {aⁿbⁿ} and {ww} need unbounded memory and fail the pumping lemma; {w : #a = #b} does too, with the same aᵖbᵖ word.
  4. Only the modular condition survives, because the modulus bounds the memory.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Assuming that any language defined by counting is non-regular. Counting modulo a fixed constant is regular; counting exactly without a bound is not.

TOC-U1-Q70MCQmedium

Which of the following languages over Σ = {a, b, c} satisfies the pumping lemma for regular languages and is nevertheless not regular?

  1. {aⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 0}
  2. {aⁱbʲcᵏ : i = 1 ⇒ j = k}
  3. {ww : w ∈ {a, b}*}
  4. {aᵖ : p prime}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The pumping lemma is a necessary condition only. A language that satisfies it need not be regular, so "pumps" never proves regularity; only failing it proves non-regularity.

Step by step.

  1. (B) is not regular. Intersect it with ab*c*, a regular set. The result is {abʲcʲ : j ≥ 0}, and the standard pumping argument on abᵖcᵖ shows this is not regular; regular languages are closed under intersection, so (B) cannot be regular either.
  2. (B) still pumps. Take p = 2 and any w ∈ L with |w| ≥ 2. If w does not start with a, pump the first symbol (b or c); the antecedent i = 1 stays false, so the word remains in L. If w = abʲcʲ, pump the leading a: the new word starts with aa, so again i ≠ 1 and it is in L. Every pumped word stays in L.
  3. (A), (C), (D) fail the lemma, so each is non-regular and cannot be the answer: aᵖbᵖ pumped in the a-block breaks the balance, ww with w = aᵖb cannot be pumped in the first half, and for a^q with q ≥ p prime, pumping up to q(1 + k) gives a composite length.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Treating the pumping lemma as a characterisation of regularity. It is one-way: no conclusion follows from a language that pumps.

TOC-U1-Q73MSQhard

Which of the following languages over Σ = {a, b} are regular? (Select all that apply.)

  1. {aⁿbᵐ : n ≤ m}
  2. {aⁿbᵐ : n + m ≡ 0 (mod 2)}
  3. {w : #a(w) ≡ #b(w) (mod 2)}
  4. {aⁿbⁿ : n is a multiple of 3}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A condition is regular when the information it needs is bounded: a residue modulo a fixed number qualifies, an unbounded comparison does not.

Step by step.

  1. (A) not regular. {aⁿbᵐ : n ≤ m} compares two unbounded counts. Intersecting with a*b* leaves the same language, and pumping aᵖbᵖ inside the a-block gives a^{p+k}bᵖ with p + k > p, outside the language.
  2. (B) regular. n + m is just the length of the word, so the condition says "even length" — two states.
  3. (C) regular. Track the parities of the two counts: four states (p_a, p_b) ∈ {0,1}², accepting exactly those with equal parities.
  4. (D) not regular. It is a sub-language of {aⁿbⁿ} cut out by a condition on n; pumping a^{3q}b^{3q} in the a-block destroys the balance, and no choice of split repairs it.

Answer: Options (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Judging (D) regular because "multiple of 3" is a modular condition. The modulus applies to n, which must be counted exactly against the number of b's; the modularity of the multiplier does not bound the memory needed.

Review the 75 solved questions from Unit 2 — Grammars & Context-Free Languages
CFG derivation trees · 20
TOC-U2-Q01NATeasy

Let G be the grammar with productions S → aS | b. How many internal (non-leaf) nodes does the derivation tree of the string aaab have?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a derivation tree each internal node is one application of a production, and its children are exactly the right-hand side of that production. A left-recursive production S → aS therefore adds one S node and one terminal leaf per step, so the number of internal nodes is the number of derivation steps.

Step by step.

  1. Derive the string: S ⇒ aS ⇒ aaS ⇒ aaaS ⇒ aaab.
  2. The last step uses S → b; the three earlier steps each use S → aS.
  3. Each of the four steps creates one internal node: S₀, S₁, S₂, S₃. The children are a, a, a, b — four leaves.
  4. Check with the edge count: four internal nodes with child counts 2, 2, 2, 1 give 2 + 2 + 2 + 1 = 7 edges, and a tree with 8 nodes has 7 edges.

Answer: 4

Common misconception. Counting the number of a's and answering 3, or counting the leaves and answering 4 by a different route. The reliable rule is: one internal node per production applied, and the derivation above applies four productions.

Takeaway. Internal nodes = derivation steps; leaves = length of the derived string (plus one leaf if a production A → ε is used).

TOC-U2-Q02MCQeasy

Which of the following is necessarily true of a complete derivation (parse) tree for a context-free grammar G = (V, T, P, S)?

  1. Every internal node is labelled by a terminal of T .
  2. The tree has one leaf for every production used in the derivation.
  3. Reading the leaves from left to right gives a string over T , and that string belongs to L(G) .
  4. Every leaf is labelled by a nonterminal of V .
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A derivation tree has the root labelled S; an internal node labelled A has children spelling some right-hand side of a production for A; and in a complete tree every leaf is labelled by a terminal (or by ε). The frontier read left to right is called the yield.

Step by step.

  1. Every leaf of a complete tree is a terminal, so the yield is a string over T.
  2. Reading the internal nodes top-down in breadth-first order and recording the productions used gives a valid derivation S ⇒* w, where w is the yield.
  3. Hence w ∈ L(G), which is exactly the statement in (C).

Answer: Option (C)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Internal nodes carry nonterminals; terminals appear only at the leaves.
  • (B) A production A → BC creates two children, not one leaf, so the leaf count tracks the length of the yield, not the number of productions.
  • (D) Exactly the reverse: leaves carry terminals in a complete tree.

Common misconception. Believing that leaves can be nonterminals in a finished parse. A leaf that still holds a nonterminal means the derivation is only partially expanded.

TOC-U2-Q03MCQeasy

For a context-free grammar G and a string w ∈ L(G), which of the following is TRUE?

  1. The number of distinct derivations of w equals the number of distinct derivation trees for w .
  2. The number of distinct leftmost derivations of w equals the number of distinct derivation trees for w .
  3. If w has two distinct derivation trees then G is unambiguous.
  4. Every derivation tree for w has exactly |w| internal nodes.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A derivation tree records only which production was applied where, not the order in which independent nonterminals were expanded. Fixing the order — always expand the leftmost nonterminal — removes the freedom without losing any tree, so leftmost derivations and derivation trees are in one-to-one correspondence.

Step by step.

  1. From any derivation tree, walk the internal nodes in preorder and expand the leftmost nonterminal each time: this yields one leftmost derivation, and it is unique.
  2. Conversely, a leftmost derivation records the exact production used at each node, so it rebuilds a unique tree.
  3. The two maps are inverse, hence the counts in (B) agree.
  4. All derivations of w are many more than the trees: once a sentential form has several nonterminals, any of them may be expanded next, so (A) fails.

Answer: Option (B)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) Overcounts: the same tree is reachable by many expansion orders.
  • (C) Two distinct trees is precisely the definition of ambiguity, so the grammar is ambiguous, not unambiguous.
  • (D) Internal nodes equal the number of production applications, which is generally larger than |w|.

Common misconception. Treating "two derivations" as ambiguity. Every nontrivial string has many derivations; ambiguity needs two derivations that differ in structure, i.e. two distinct trees.

TOC-U2-Q04MCQmedium

Let G be a context-free grammar with no ε-productions and no unit productions, and let w ∈ L(G) with |w| = n ≥ 1. What is the largest possible number of nodes — internal nodes plus leaves — in a derivation tree for w?

  1. 2n − 1
  2. n²
  3. 3n − 1
  4. n + 1
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two kinds of internal node can appear. A branching node comes from a production whose right-hand side has two or more symbols; a terminal-production node comes from A → a and has exactly one child. Banning ε- and unit productions removes neither kind, so both must be counted.

Step by step.

  1. The yield has n terminal leaves.
  2. Each leaf is the only child of the production that emitted it, so there are at most n terminal-production nodes — at most, because one production such as A → ab can emit two leaves at once.
  3. Let b be the number of branching nodes and u the number of terminal-production nodes. Counting edges: 2b + u = b + u + n − 1 when every branching node is binary, so b = n − 1. A ternary production would only reduce b, so n − 1 is the maximum.
  4. Total internal nodes ≤ (n − 1) + n = 2n − 1, and total nodes ≤ (2n − 1) + n = 3n − 1.
  5. The bound is attained: for n = 3 take S → AB, A → a, B → CD, C → b, D → c. The tree has 2 branching nodes, 3 terminal-production nodes and 3 leaves — 8 nodes = 3(3) − 1.

Answer: Option (C) — 3n − 1

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 2n − 1 is the count of internal nodes in a binary-branching tree, not the count of all nodes, and it also forgets the terminal-production nodes.
  • (B) Quadratic growth needs unary chains, which the ban on unit productions removes.
  • (D) n + 1 is far too small for any tree with more than one branching node.

Common misconception. Quoting 2n − 1 from the CNF derivation-length theorem and applying it to the total node count. There 2n − 1 counts the steps of the derivation, which are the internal nodes; the leaves are additional.

TOC-U2-Q05MSQmedium

Select every statement about derivation trees that is TRUE.

  1. If some string has two distinct derivation trees, the grammar is ambiguous.
  2. The children of any internal node, read from left to right, form the right-hand side of some production of G .
  3. Two different derivations of the same string can share one derivation tree.
  4. A grammar in which some nonterminal appears twice on a root-to-leaf path of a derivation tree is ambiguous.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The definition of ambiguity is stated in terms of derivation trees (equivalently leftmost derivations), while the tree itself is built strictly from production right-hand sides.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the definition of an ambiguous grammar. True.
  2. (B) is the construction rule for a derivation tree: a node labelled A with children X₁…Xₖ requires A → X₁…Xₖ in P. True.
  3. (C) is true whenever the sentential form has two or more nonterminals: expanding them in either order gives two different derivations but the same tree. True.
  4. (D) is false. A repeated nonterminal on a path is just recursion, and recursion is what every grammar for an infinite language needs. For example S → aS | b repeats S on every path yet is unambiguous. False.

Answer: (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Reading (D) as "recursion causes ambiguity". Ambiguity needs two structurally different trees for one string, not merely a nonterminal occurring more than once along a path.

TOC-U2-Q06NATmedium

Let G be the grammar S → AB, A → aA | a, B → bB | b. How many internal nodes does the derivation tree of a³b² have?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each internal node corresponds to one production application. Count the applications used inside the A part, inside the B part, and the root.

Step by step.

  1. The root S → AB contributes 1 internal node.
  2. A must produce three a's: A ⇒ aA ⇒ aaA ⇒ aaa, which uses A → aA twice and A → a once — 3 internal nodes.
  3. B must produce two b's: B ⇒ bB ⇒ bb, which uses B → bB once and B → b once — 2 internal nodes.
  4. Total: 1 + 3 + 2 = 6 internal nodes, with 5 leaves.
  5. Cross-check with edges: child counts are 2, 2, 2, 1, 2, 1, summing to 10, and a tree with 11 nodes has 10 edges. Consistent.

Answer: 6

Common misconception. Answering 5, the number of terminals, by treating the tree as if it had one internal node per symbol. Here the derivation needs 6 production applications for 5 terminals, because the root production S → AB produces no terminal at all.

TOC-U2-Q07MCQhard

Let G be the grammar S → aSc | T, T → bTc | ε. Which of the following strings is the yield of a derivation tree of G?

  1. a²b²c³
  2. ab²c²
  3. a³b²c⁴
  4. a²bc³
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Read each recursive pair as a matched pair: S → aSc wraps one a and one c around the inside, and T → bTc does the same with b and c. Counting the wrappers gives the shape of every string in the language.

Step by step.

  1. Using S → aSc exactly i times and then S → T gives aᵗ T cᵗ.
  2. Using T → bTc exactly j times and then T → ε gives bᵑ cᵑ.
  3. Substituting: the string is aᵗ bᵑ cᵑ⁺ᵗ, i.e. aᵗ bᵑ cᵗ⁺ᵑ.
  4. Test (D) a²bc³: take i = 2, j = 1, giving a² b c³. It matches.
  5. Test the others: a²b²c³ would need c-count 2 + 2 = 4; ab²c² would need 1 + 2 = 3; a³b²c⁴ would need 3 + 2 = 5. None matches.

Answer: Option (D) — a²bc³

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (C) each add the a-wrappers and the b-wrappers to the wrong total, so the c count is short or long by one.
  • (B) has one a but needs three c's for i + j = 3.

Common misconception. Assuming the two counts are independent. Both families of wrappers deposit c's in the same tail, so the c-block length is i + j, never j alone.

TOC-U2-Q26MCQeasy

In a derivation tree an internal node is labelled A, and its children, read from left to right, are labelled X₁, …, Xₖ. Which statement is necessarily true?

  1. A &#8594; X&#8321;&#8230;X&#8342; is a production of the grammar.
  2. X&#8321;&#8230;X&#8342; is a string belonging to L(G).
  3. k = 2 for every production used in the tree.
  4. A is a terminal symbol of the grammar.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A derivation tree is a picture of the productions that were applied: each internal node and its children spell out exactly one production, and nothing else is recorded in the tree.

Step by step.

  1. An internal node is created by expanding a nonterminal, so its label A is a nonterminal.
  2. The children are, by construction, the right-hand side of the production that was applied to A.
  3. So the node and its ordered children read X₁…Xₖ together encode A → X₁…Xₖ ∈ P.
  4. The string X₁…Xₖ need not be terminal, so it need not belong to L(G); k is not fixed at 2.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Reading the children as a word of the language. Only the leaves of a complete tree give a string of L(G); the children of one node give the right-hand side of one production.

Takeaway. Tree node + ordered children = one production. Leaves left to right = the derived string.

TOC-U2-Q27NATeasy

Let G be the grammar S → aSb | ε. The derivation tree of the string a⁴b⁴ is drawn completely, with the ε-production shown as a single child labelled ε. How many internal (non-leaf) nodes does that tree have?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A derivation tree for aⁿbⁿ under S → aSb | ε has one S-node per matched pair of a and b, plus one final S-node that closes the derivation with ε.

Step by step.

  1. Each application of S → aSb adds one S-node and one a and one b to the frontier.
  2. For a⁴b⁴ the production S → aSb is applied four times, creating four S-nodes.
  3. The innermost S is then expanded by S → ε, which is a fifth S-node; it has a single child labelled ε, so it is internal.
  4. Internal nodes = 4 + 1 = 5. (Leaves are a,a,a,a,ε,b,b,b,b.)

Answer: 5.

Common misconception. Counting only the four S-nodes that produced a and b and forgetting the innermost S that applies the ε-production. That node still has a child, so it is internal, not a leaf.

Takeaway. Under S → aSb | ε, the tree for aⁿbⁿ has n+1 internal nodes.

TOC-U2-Q28MCQeasy

Let G be the grammar S → aSb | SS | ε. Which of the following strings is not in L(G)?

  1. aabb
  2. abab
  3. abba
  4. aaabbb
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. S → aSb | SS | ε generates exactly the balanced strings over {a, b}, in which a and b behave like an opening and a closing bracket. A string is in L(G) only if every prefix has at least as many a's as b's and the totals are equal.

Step by step.

  1. Read a as +1 and b as −1 and track the running total.
  2. aabb: 1, 2, 1, 0 — never negative, ends at 0, so it is balanced.
  3. abab: 1, 0, 1, 0 — balanced.
  4. aaabbb: 1, 2, 3, 2, 1, 0 — balanced.
  5. abba: 1, 0, −1 — the running total goes negative at the third symbol, so no balanced decomposition exists and abba ∉ L(G).

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Checking only that the counts of a and b are equal. abba has two of each, yet it cannot be built from S → aSb | SS | ε, because the second S of SS must itself generate a balanced block.

Takeaway. For S → aSb | SS | ε, membership is exactly the Dyck (balanced-prefix) condition, not just equal counts.

TOC-U2-Q29MSQmedium

Select every statement about derivation trees and sentential forms that is TRUE.

  1. The yield of a complete derivation tree is always a string of terminals.
  2. Every internal node of a derivation tree is labelled by a nonterminal.
  3. A sentential form may contain both terminals and nonterminals.
  4. The yield of a subtree rooted at an arbitrary nonterminal A is always a string of L(G).
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A complete derivation tree has only terminals at its leaves, while an intermediate sentential form is the frontier of a partially grown tree and can mix terminals with nonterminals.

Step by step.

  1. A: true — a tree is complete exactly when every leaf carries a terminal, and the leaves read left to right give the derived word.
  2. B: true — leaves are the only nodes that carry terminals; every internal node is a nonterminal being expanded.
  3. C: true — after S → AB and A → aA, the frontier is aAB, which contains a terminal and two nonterminals.
  4. D: false — A ⇒* α only says α is derivable from A. It belongs to L(G) only when A = S, the start symbol.

Answer: Options (A, B and C).

Common misconception. Treating every nonterminal as a start symbol. L(G) is defined by derivations from S alone; a subtree rooted at A yields a string derivable from A.

Takeaway. Leaves of a complete tree → terminals; frontier of a partial tree → sentential form; subtree at A → a string in the language of A, not of G.

TOC-U2-Q30MCQhard

Let G be a context-free grammar with no ε-productions and no unit productions, and let the derivation tree of w ∈ L(G) contain a path on which the same nonterminal A labels two different nodes. Which conclusion is valid?

  1. G is ambiguous.
  2. |w| &#8804; |V|, where V is the set of nonterminals of G.
  3. The tree for w has strictly more than |V| leaves.
  4. w can be written as uvwxy with vx &#8800; &#949; such that uv&#7522;wx&#7522;y &#8712; L(G) for every i &#8805; 0.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two nodes labelled A on one root-to-leaf path enclose a sub-derivation that starts and ends at A. That sub-derivation can be repeated or deleted, which is exactly the pumping step in the CFL pumping lemma.

Step by step.

  1. Let the upper A be A1 and the lower one A2, both on the same path, with A1 ⇒* A2 inside the tree.
  2. The leaves under A2 are some vwx and the leaves under A1 are uvwxy, so A ⇒* uAy and A ⇒* vwx.
  3. Because there are no ε- or unit productions, vx ≠ ε — the sub-derivation A ⇒* vwx genuinely produces symbols.
  4. Substituting the second derivation into the first i times gives A ⇒* uvᵢwxᵢy, so every uvᵢwxᵢy lies in L(G).

Answer: Option (D).

Common misconception. Concluding ambiguity. A repeated nonterminal on a path proves the language can be pumped; it says nothing about whether the grammar has two trees for the same string.

Takeaway. A repeated nonterminal on one path is the tree-level statement of the pumping lemma; ambiguity is a different property entirely.

TOC-U2-Q31MCQeasy

A derivation tree for w ∈ L(G) contains a node labelled A, and the leaves below that node, read left to right, form the string α. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. A &#8658;* &#945; in G.
  2. &#945; &#8712; L(G).
  3. &#945; is always a single terminal.
  4. &#945; must contain the symbol A.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The subtree rooted at a node labelled A is a complete derivation of α from A, which is precisely the meaning of A ⇒* α.

Step by step.

  1. Replace the node A and everything below it by a tree whose root is A — that is the definition of a subtree of a derivation tree.
  2. Reading its leaves gives a string α obtained from A by applying productions, so A ⇒* α.
  3. α need not be terminal (the subtree may be partial), need not be a single symbol, and never contains A itself unless a production produced A.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Writing α ∈ L(G). Only the whole tree from S yields a word of the language; a subtree yields a word of the language generated from A.

Takeaway. Subtree at A = derivation from A; only the root S gives a string of L(G).

TOC-U2-Q51MCQeasy

A string w ∈ L(G) has exactly two distinct derivation trees in a context-free grammar G. Which one of the following is necessarily TRUE?

  1. <span class="mm">w</span> has exactly two distinct leftmost derivations and exactly two distinct rightmost derivations.
  2. <span class="mm">w</span> has at least two leftmost derivations but possibly only one rightmost derivation.
  3. <span class="mm">w</span> has exactly one leftmost derivation and exactly two rightmost derivations.
  4. <span class="mm">w</span> has exactly two derivations in total.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Derivation trees and leftmost (or rightmost) derivations are in exact one-to-one correspondence: a tree is recovered from a leftmost derivation by forgetting the order of expansion, and a leftmost derivation is recovered from a tree by always expanding the leftmost nonterminal leaf.

Step by step.

  1. Each derivation tree determines exactly one leftmost derivation: expand the leftmost nonterminal at every step. Two different trees therefore give two different leftmost derivations.
  2. The same reading works from the right, so each tree also determines exactly one rightmost derivation.
  3. Two trees therefore give exactly two leftmost and exactly two rightmost derivations, and no more, because every leftmost derivation maps back to one of those two trees.
  4. The total number of derivations is larger and unbounded: expanding the nonterminals in different orders gives many derivations that all correspond to the same two trees, so the total is at least 2·2 = 4.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Treating “derivation” and “leftmost derivation” as the same thing, or forgetting that one tree is compatible with several expansion orders. Only the leftmost (or rightmost) count is tied to the number of trees.

TOC-U2-Q52NATeasy

Let G be the grammar S → SS | a | b. The complete derivation tree of the string aabb is drawn with one node for every symbol occurrence. How many nodes does that tree have in total, counting internal nodes and leaves together?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a derivation tree the leaves spell the derived string, and every internal node is one application of a production of the form A → BC. The shape is therefore a binary tree whose leaves are exactly the symbols of the string.

Step by step.

  1. The yield is aabb, so the tree has exactly 4 leaves.
  2. Every internal node of this grammar has exactly two children (the only branching production is S → SS), so the tree is a full binary tree.
  3. A full binary tree with L leaves has exactly L − 1 internal nodes: 4 − 1 = 3.
  4. Total nodes = internal + leaves = 3 + 4 = 7.
  5. The grammar is ambiguous and aabb has five derivation trees, but every one of them is a full binary tree with 4 leaves, so the answer does not depend on which tree is drawn.

Answer: 7.

Common misconception. Counting only the three internal nodes, or adding the leaves twice. A full binary tree with L leaves has L − 1 internal nodes, so the total is 2L − 1 = 7 here.

TOC-U2-Q53MCQeasy

Let G = (V, T, P, S) be a context-free grammar. Which one of the following is NOT true of every derivation tree of G?

  1. The root is labelled <span class="mm">S</span>.
  2. Every internal node is labelled by a nonterminal.
  3. Every leaf is labelled by a terminal of <span class="mm">T</span>.
  4. The children of an internal node, read from left to right, spell the right side of some production whose left side is that node&#8217;s label.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A derivation tree is built by applying productions: a node labelled A gets children spelling the right side of a production A → α. Leaves are symbols that were never expanded.

Step by step.

  1. The root is always the start symbol S — that is the definition of a derivation tree for a string of L(G).
  2. An internal node is a symbol that was expanded, and only nonterminals have productions, so every internal node carries a nonterminal.
  3. A leaf is a symbol that was never expanded. Terminals can never be expanded, but neither can a nonterminal that was replaced by ε: in the tree for a derivation that uses A → ε, the node labelled A has a single child labelled ε, and ε is not a terminal of T.
  4. The children of a node are written by applying one production, so their labels in order are the right side of a production for that label.

Answer: Option (C) is the statement that can fail, so (C) is the answer.

Common misconception. Assuming every leaf is a terminal. Leaves labelled ε appear exactly when an ε-production is used, which is why ε-free grammars are the ones where “leaves = symbols of w” is safe.

TOC-U2-Q54MSQmedium

Let G = (V, T, P, S) be a context-free grammar and let α be a string over V ∪ T. Select every statement that is TRUE.

  1. If <span class="mm">S &rArr;* &alpha;</span>, then <span class="mm">&alpha;</span> is the frontier of some partial derivation tree whose root is <span class="mm">S</span>.
  2. The frontier of every partial derivation tree rooted at <span class="mm">S</span> is a sentential form of <span class="mm">G</span>.
  3. If a subtree of a derivation tree has root <span class="mm">A</span> and frontier <span class="mm">&beta;</span>, then <span class="mm">A &rArr;* &beta;</span>.
  4. In the frontier of any partial derivation tree, every terminal lies to the left of every nonterminal.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A partial derivation tree stops expanding some leaves; its frontier is read left to right. Frontier and derivability are two views of the same relation: A ⇒* β exactly when β is the frontier of a tree rooted at A.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: take the derivation S ⇒* α and draw the tree for each step; stopping after the last step gives a partial tree with frontier α.
  2. (B) true: the root is S, and the sequence of productions read off the tree is a valid derivation, so the frontier is reachable from S.
  3. (C) true: read the productions of that subtree from the root downwards; they form a derivation of β from A.
  4. (D) false: in S → aSb the sentential form aSb has the terminal a to the left of the nonterminal S and the terminal b to its right.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Believing a frontier must be “all terminals” or “terminals first”. A frontier mixes terminals and nonterminals in whatever order the productions produced them.

TOC-U2-Q55MCQmedium

Let G have the productions S → AB, A → aA | a and B → bB | ε. Which one of the following is L(G)?

  1. <span class="mm">{ a<sup>m</sup>b<sup>n</sup> : m &ge; 0, n &ge; 0 }</span>
  2. <span class="mm">{ a<sup>m</sup>b<sup>n</sup> : m &ge; 1, n &ge; 1 }</span>
  3. <span class="mm">{ a<sup>m</sup>b<sup>n</sup> : m &ge; 0, n &ge; 1 }</span>
  4. <span class="mm">{ a<sup>m</sup>b<sup>n</sup> : m &ge; 1, n &ge; 0 }</span>
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The language of a grammar is read off the reachable derivations of each nonterminal. Here A produces one or more a, B produces zero or more b, and S concatenates the two blocks.

Step by step.

  1. A has the derivations A ⇒ a, A ⇒ aA ⇒ aa, …, so A generates am for every m ≥ 1, and nothing else.
  2. B has the derivations B ⇒ ε, B ⇒ bB ⇒ b, …, so B generates bn for every n ≥ 0, and nothing else.
  3. Every derivation of S is S ⇒ AB followed by a derivation of A and then of B, so every string of L(G) has the form ambn with m ≥ 1 and n ≥ 0.
  4. Conversely every such string is generated by choosing S ⇒ AB and then the two derivations, so the two sets are equal.
  5. The empty string is not in L(G), because A cannot vanish.

Answer: Option (D).

Common misconception. Forgetting that A cannot produce ε, which wrongly admits strings with no a at all, or forgetting that B may produce ε, which wrongly forces at least one b.

TOC-U2-Q56NATmedium

Let G be the grammar S → aS | Sb | c. A complete derivation tree of the string aacbbb is drawn. How many internal (non-leaf) nodes does it contain?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each application of a production adds one level to the tree: the node being expanded becomes internal and its right side becomes its children. In this grammar every production emits exactly one terminal, so the number of internal nodes equals the number of symbols in the derived string.

Step by step.

  1. Every production of G adds exactly one terminal symbol and keeps exactly one nonterminal (aS and Sb) or none (c).
  2. The string aacbbb has 6 symbols, so the derivation uses exactly 6 productions.
  3. Each of those 6 productions makes one node internal, so the tree has 6 internal nodes; the 6 leaves are the 6 terminals.
  4. One such derivation: S ⇒ aS ⇒ aaS ⇒ aaSb ⇒ aaSbb ⇒ aaSbbb ⇒ aacbbb.
  5. The grammar is in fact ambiguous for strings that mix a and b (the prepends and appends can be interleaved in several orders), but every parse uses the same six productions, so the count is well defined.

Answer: 6.

Common misconception. Counting only the S-nodes that appear in one particular derivation, or counting leaves as internal nodes. Here every production emits one terminal, so internal nodes = |w|.

TOC-U2-Q57MCQhard

Let G be the grammar S → aSb | bSa | SS | ε. How many distinct derivation trees does G have for the string aabb?

  1. 0
  2. 1
  3. 2
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two derivations give different trees exactly when they use different productions at some node. For this grammar every tree for a string with equal numbers of a and b splits that string into balanced blocks, and the count is a small dynamic-programming table over substrings.

Step by step.

  1. aabb has equal numbers of a and b, so it can be derived at all.
  2. Consider the root. If the root uses S → aSb, the string must begin with a and end with b, which fails because aabb ends with b. The same argument rules out S → bSa.
  3. If the root uses S → SS, the split aabb = a·abb is the only one where both halves are derivable: a and abb. The other candidate splits (aa, bb) leave halves with unequal numbers of a and b.
  4. The substring abb itself has a unique tree, again forced to split as a·bb, and each of a and bb has exactly one tree.
  5. So exactly one tree exists, even though the grammar is ambiguous in general — for abab it has two trees.

Answer: Option (B): 1.

Common misconception. Concluding “ambiguous grammar, so more than one tree”. Ambiguity is a property of the grammar, not of each individual string: this grammar is ambiguous, yet aabb has a single parse.

Ambiguity in grammars · 18
TOC-U2-Q08MCQeasy

A context-free grammar G is ambiguous if and only if:

  1. some string in L(G) has two distinct derivation trees.
  2. some string in L(G) has two distinct derivations.
  3. some production of G has two nonterminals on its right-hand side.
  4. G generates an infinite language.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Ambiguity is a property of the grammar, and it is defined structurally: a string with two genuinely different parse trees has two different meanings.

Step by step.

  1. Every string of length ≥ 2 in any nontrivial grammar has several derivations, differing only in the order of expanding independent nonterminals. That is why (B) is too weak.
  2. Two derivations that correspond to the same tree carry the same meaning, so they do not make the grammar ambiguous.
  3. Two distinct trees — equivalently two distinct leftmost derivations — do. That is (A).

Answer: Option (A)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) Every string has multiple derivations in most grammars; the correct notion counts trees, or leftmost derivations.
  • (C) Binary productions are the hallmark of Chomsky Normal Form, which is not ambiguous by construction; S → AB alone is harmless.
  • (D) S → aS | b generates an infinite language and is unambiguous.

Common misconception. Equating "many derivations" with "ambiguous". Only distinct structures count.

TOC-U2-Q09MCQeasy

Let G be the grammar S → SS | a. How many distinct derivation trees does G have for the string aaa?

  1. 1
  2. 3
  3. 2
  4. 5
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. With S → SS the only branching production, a tree for aⁿ is a full binary tree with n leaves, and the count is the Catalan number Cₖ₋₁. For n = 3 that is C₂ = 2.

Step by step.

  1. The root must be S → SS (it cannot be S → a, since the string has length 3).
  2. The split point decides the tree: the first subtree derives a prefix of a's, the second the rest.
  3. Split 1 + 2: S ⇒ SS ⇒ aS ⇒ aSS ⇒ aaa — one tree.
  4. Split 2 + 1: S ⇒ SS ⇒ SSS ⇒ aSS ⇒ aaS ⇒ aaa, giving the other tree.
  5. So there are exactly 2 trees, and aaa has two distinct leftmost derivations — the grammar is ambiguous.

Answer: Option (C) — 2

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) 1 is the count for an unambiguous grammar; here the two splits are genuinely different trees.
  • (B) and (D) are not Catalan numbers for 3 leaves; C₂ = 2 is the only correct value.

Common misconception. Confusing the number of derivations (many) with the number of trees (2). Also, some candidates forget the SS split 2 + 1 because the string is symmetric.

TOC-U2-Q10NATmedium

Let G be the grammar S → SS | a. How many distinct derivation trees does G have for the string a⁵ (five a's)?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A tree for aⁿ is a full binary tree with n leaves in which every internal node is an S → SS application. The number of such trees is the Catalan number Cₖ₋₁ = (1/n)·C(2n−2, n−1).

Step by step.

  1. Here n = 5, so the answer is C₄.
  2. C₄ = C(8, 4)/5 = 70/5 = 14.
  3. Equivalently, with T(1) = 1 and T(n) = Σₖ₊₁ T(k)·T(n−k): T(2) = 1, T(3) = 2, T(4) = 5, T(5) = 14.
  4. The recursion is exactly the split of the root S → SS into a left part of k and a right part of n − k leaves.

Answer: 14

Common misconception. Answering 2⁴ = 16 by counting splits independently at each level, or 5 by confusing n with n − 1 and quoting C₃ = 5. The recurrence is the Catalans, not powers of two.

TOC-U2-Q11MCQhard

Which of the following languages is inherently ambiguous?

  1. {aⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 1}
  2. {aᵡbᵑcᵏ : i = j or j = k}
  3. {wwᵀ : w ∈ {a,b}*}
  4. {aⁿbⁿcᵐ : n, m ≥ 1}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A language is inherently ambiguous when every grammar that generates it is ambiguous — no clever rewriting can remove the ambiguity. A language is inherently ambiguous exactly when it is a union of two languages whose intersection needs unbounded agreement between two independent counts.

Step by step.

  1. (B) is L₁ ∪ L₂ with L₁ = {aᵡbᵡcᵏ} and L₂ = {aᵡbᵏcᵏ}. Their intersection is {aⁿbⁿcⁿ}, which is not context-free.
  2. Strings of the form aⁿbⁿcⁿ lie in both halves, and a single derivation cannot decide which count to bind first. The standard pumping argument shows every grammar for L₁ ∪ L₂ must be ambiguous. Hence (B) is inherently ambiguous.
  3. (A) has the unambiguous grammar S → aSb | ab.
  4. (C) is deterministic, hence unambiguous: S → aSa | bSb | ε.
  5. (D) is the concatenation of {aⁿbⁿ} with c⁾, again unambiguous.

Answer: Option (B)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (C) are deterministic context-free languages, and every DCFL has an unambiguous grammar.
  • (D) is a concatenation of two unambiguous pieces with a separating symbol, so no ambiguity arises.

Common misconception. Assuming that every context-free language can be made unambiguous with enough care. Inherent ambiguity is a property of the language, not of a poorly written grammar, and {aⁿbⁿcⁿ} sitting in the intersection of the two halves is what makes (B) unavoidable.

TOC-U2-Q12MSQhard

Select every statement that is TRUE.

  1. Every regular language has an unambiguous context-free grammar.
  2. If a language has an unambiguous grammar, then every grammar for it is unambiguous.
  3. If G is ambiguous, then some string in L(G) has two distinct leftmost derivations.
  4. Every language accepted by a deterministic pushdown automaton is inherently ambiguous.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Ambiguity lives on grammars; inherent ambiguity lives on languages. Determinism is the cleanest route to unambiguity.

Step by step.

  1. (A) Take a DFA for the regular language and turn each transition δ(q, a) = q′ into Qₓ → aQₓ′, with Qₓ → ε for final states. Every string has exactly one leftmost derivation, since the DFA run is unique. True.
  2. (B) is false: {aⁿbⁿ} has the unambiguous S → aSb | ab and also the ambiguous S → SS | aSb | ε. False.
  3. (C) restates the definition: two distinct trees is equivalent to two distinct leftmost derivations. True.
  4. (D) inverts the truth. Every DFA is a special DPDA, and DCFLs are exactly the unambiguous languages that have a deterministic parser; they are the opposite of inherently ambiguous. False.

Answer: (A) and (C)

Common misconception. Thinking unambiguity is inherited by the language rather than chosen by the grammar. A language can be unambiguous while some of its grammars are ambiguous — which is exactly why "inherently ambiguous" is a separate and stronger notion.

TOC-U2-Q13MCQmedium

The grammar E → E + E | E * E | (E) | id is ambiguous. Which replacement makes it unambiguous, keeps the same language, and gives * higher precedence than +?

  1. E → E + T | T , T → T * F | F , F → (E) | id
  2. E → E + E | T , T → T * T | (E) | id
  3. E → E * T | T , T → T + F | F , F → (E) | id
  4. E → E + E | E * E | id
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Precedence is encoded by layering: the lowest-precedence operator sits at the top nonterminal, the next one below it, and atoms at the bottom. Left recursion at a layer gives left-associativity.

Step by step.

  1. In (A), E generates sums of T's, and each T generates products of F's.
  2. So a + can never appear inside a T: id + id * id can only be parsed as id + (id * id), which is the required precedence.
  3. Each layer is left-recursive at exactly one production, so every string has a unique leftmost derivation: the grammar is unambiguous.
  4. The three layers E, T, F together generate the same language as the original, since parentheses are still available at the atom level.

Answer: Option (A)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) keeps E → E + E, so id + id + id still has two trees — still ambiguous — and it also collapses the * layer into one self-recursive nonterminal.
  • (C) reverses precedence: now + binds tighter than *, so id + id * id parses as (id + id) * id.
  • (D) drops the parentheses production, so it no longer generates the same language.

Common misconception. Believing that removing left recursion is the same as removing ambiguity. (B) shows the two are different problems: precedence needs layering, and associativity needs a single recursive production per layer.

TOC-U2-Q32MCQeasy

Which one of the following grammars is ambiguous?

  1. S &#8594; aSb | ab
  2. S &#8594; SS | a
  3. S &#8594; aA, A &#8594; b
  4. S &#8594; aSb | &#949;
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A grammar is ambiguous when some single string has two different derivation trees. The surest way to build one is to let a nonterminal derive a concatenation of itself, because the split point can then be chosen in more than one way.

Step by step.

  1. S → aSb | ab generates {aⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 1}. Each string forces exactly n applications of aSb, so the tree is unique.
  2. S → SS | a generates a⁾. For aaa the split S ⇒ SS can put one a on the left and two on the right, or two on the left and one on the right — two distinct trees, so it is ambiguous.
  3. S → aA, A → b generates the single string ab, with one tree.
  4. S → aSb | ε generates {aⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 0}, again with a unique tree per string.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Assuming that any grammar with a recursive production is ambiguous. S → aSb is recursive but unambiguous, because the recursion is tied to matching a with b.

Takeaway. Recursion is not ambiguity; a nonterminal deriving itself twice in a concatenation (S → SS) is what creates the choice.

TOC-U2-Q33NATmedium

Let G be the grammar S → S + S | a. How many distinct derivation trees does G have for the string a + a + a?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. With S → S + S | a, a derivation tree is a full binary tree whose leaves are the three a's; the number of trees is the number of ways to bracket the expression, which is the Catalan number C₂.

Step by step.

  1. Every tree for a + a + a has exactly three leaves, one per a, and each internal node is one application of S → S + S.
  2. The root splits the three a's into a left group of i a's and a right group of 3 − i a's, with i = 1 or i = 2 only (both groups must be non-empty).
  3. i = 1 gives (a) + (a + a); i = 2 gives (a + a) + (a).
  4. That is 2 trees, matching the Catalan number C₂ = 2 for three leaves.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Answering 1 by assuming the grammar is unambiguous. The grammar is precisely the ambiguous left/right-association grammar, and the two bracketings are genuinely different trees.

Takeaway. For S → S + S | a the count for n a's is the Catalan number Cₙ₋₁.

TOC-U2-Q34MCQmedium

The grammar S → aS | Sa | a generates a⁾. Which is the shortest string in L(G) that has two distinct derivation trees?

  1. a
  2. aaa
  3. aa
  4. aaaa
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The two recursive productions S → aS and S → Sa attach the next a to the left end or to the right end, and both describe the same string, so ambiguity appears as soon as there are two a's to attach.

Step by step.

  1. For a: only S → a applies, so there is exactly one tree.
  2. For aa: S ⇒ aS ⇒ a(a) and S ⇒ Sa ⇒ (a)a are two distinct trees for the same string.
  3. For aaa and aaaa the count grows further (4 and 8 trees), so the shortest ambiguous string is aa.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Choosing aaa because it 'looks' more ambiguous. The question asks for the shortest witness, and aa already has two trees.

Takeaway. Left-attachment and right-attachment productions create ambiguity as soon as two symbols must be attached.

TOC-U2-Q35MSQmedium

Select every statement about ambiguity that is TRUE.

  1. If a language has an unambiguous grammar, then every grammar for that language is unambiguous.
  2. A grammar is unambiguous if and only if every string in its language has exactly one derivation tree.
  3. Whether a given context-free grammar is ambiguous is a decidable problem.
  4. There exist context-free languages for which every grammar is ambiguous.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Ambiguity is a property of a grammar, not of a language. A language is called inherently ambiguous only when no grammar for it is unambiguous, and the existence of such languages is the main reason the property is worth separating.

Step by step.

  1. A: false — an unambiguous grammar can always be padded with useless duplicate productions to make a new grammar for the same language that is ambiguous.
  2. B: true — that is the definition, since a derivation tree corresponds to a leftmost derivation.
  3. C: false — ambiguity of a context-free grammar is undecidable; there is no algorithm that decides it for every grammar.
  4. D: true — languages such as {aᵐbᵑcᵒ : i = j or j = k} are inherently ambiguous.

Answer: Options (B and D).

Common misconception. Treating ambiguity as a property of the language. Most ambiguous grammars can be rewritten into an equivalent unambiguous one; only inherently ambiguous languages cannot.

Takeaway. Grammar-level ambiguity is undecidable; language-level inherent ambiguity exists but is a stronger, rarer condition.

TOC-U2-Q36MCQhard

Let G be the grammar S → AB | ε, A → aA | ε, B → bB | ε. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. G is unambiguous, because every non-empty string has exactly one derivation tree.
  2. G is ambiguous, because ab has two derivation trees.
  3. L(G) is empty.
  4. G is ambiguous, because &#949; has two derivation trees: S &#8594; &#949; and S &#8658; AB &#8658; &#949;&#949;.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Ambiguity is a property of the grammar, and the empty string is a string like any other. Two different trees that both yield ε are enough to make the grammar ambiguous, even if every non-empty string is unambiguous.

Step by step.

  1. L(G) = a*b*, since A generates a* and B generates b*.
  2. For ε: one tree is the single node S expanded by S → ε.
  3. A second tree is S ⇒ AB, then A ⇒ ε and B ⇒ ε, giving two children both yielding ε.
  4. Two distinct trees for the same string ε ∈ L(G) make G ambiguous. Every non-empty string, by contrast, has a unique tree.

Answer: Option (D).

Common misconception. Checking only non-empty strings. The empty string is the usual hiding place for ambiguity in a grammar that also has an explicit S → ε.

Takeaway. Always test ε when a grammar has both an ε-production on S and a nullable concatenation.

TOC-U2-Q37NAThard

Let G be the grammar S → SS | a. How many distinct derivation trees does G have for the string aaaa?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each tree for aⁿ is a full binary tree with n leaves, and the number of full binary trees with n leaves is the Catalan number Cₙ₋₁.

Step by step.

  1. Every internal node is one application of S → SS and every leaf is one application of S → a, so a tree with n leaves has n − 1 internal nodes.
  2. The count therefore depends only on the shape, i.e. the number of full binary trees with 4 leaves.
  3. C₀ = 1, C₁ = 1, C₂ = 2, C₃ = 5.
  4. So there are 5 derivation trees for aaaa.

Answer: 5.

Common misconception. Answering 4 by doubling the count for three a's, or 8 by counting bracketings of a chain. The count for aⁿ grows as a Catalan number, not as a power of 2.

Takeaway. S → SS | a: number of trees for aⁿ = Cₙ₋₁, i.e. 1, 1, 2, 5, 14, 42, …

TOC-U2-Q58MCQeasy

Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. Every regular language has an unambiguous context-free grammar.
  2. Every context-free language has an unambiguous grammar.
  3. A language that has an ambiguous grammar cannot be context-free.
  4. Every ambiguous grammar generates an infinite language.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Ambiguity is a property of a grammar; a language is called unambiguous when some grammar for it is unambiguous, and inherently ambiguous when no such grammar exists.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: take a DFA for the regular language and convert it to a right-linear grammar with one nonterminal per state. Each string labels exactly one accepting path, so each string has exactly one derivation.
  2. (B) false: inherently ambiguous context-free languages exist, for example { aibjck : i = j or j = k }.
  3. (C) false: an ambiguous grammar is still a grammar, and it generates a context-free language.
  4. (D) false: S → S | a generates the finite language {a} and is ambiguous for it.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Reading “some grammar for L is ambiguous” as “L is ambiguous”. Every nonempty language with a context-free grammar also has silly ambiguous grammars; the question is whether an unambiguous one exists.

TOC-U2-Q59MCQeasy

Which one of the following grammars is unambiguous?

  1. <span class="mm">S &rarr; SS | a</span>
  2. <span class="mm">S &rarr; aSb | ab</span>
  3. <span class="mm">S &rarr; aS | Sa | a</span>
  4. <span class="mm">S &rarr; S + S | a</span>
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A grammar is unambiguous when every string in its language has exactly one derivation tree. Grammars that offer two independent ways to build the same string — splitting it, or adding symbols on the left or the right — are usually ambiguous.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is ambiguous: aaa has the two trees S ⇒ SS ⇒ aS ⇒ aSS … and S ⇒ SS ⇒ SSa ….
  2. (B) is unambiguous: every string it generates is anbn, n ≥ 1, and the only possible first step is S → aSb or S → ab. For anbn the first step must be S → aSb (the string is longer than ab), which forces the outermost pair of symbols and leaves a shorter string of the same shape; induction on the length gives exactly one tree.
  3. (C) is ambiguous: aa has one tree from aS followed by Sa and another from Sa followed by aS.
  4. (D) is ambiguous: a + a + a has two trees, one adding before the other.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Judging ambiguity by how complicated the grammar looks. S → aSb | ab looks recursive but is unambiguous, while S → SS | a looks simple but is ambiguous.

TOC-U2-Q60MSQmedium

Select every statement about context-free grammars that is TRUE.

  1. If <span class="mm">G</span> is ambiguous, <span class="mm">L(G)</span> may still have an unambiguous grammar.
  2. An unambiguous grammar can have two distinct derivations of the same string.
  3. If every string in <span class="mm">L(G)</span> has exactly one derivation tree, then <span class="mm">G</span> is unambiguous.
  4. Ambiguity of a context-free grammar is decidable in polynomial time.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A grammar is ambiguous when some string of its language has two distinct derivation trees. Derivations themselves are not unique in an unambiguous grammar: only the tree is.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: a language can be generated by an ambiguous grammar and also by an unambiguous one, for example anbn with S → aS | Sb | ab is ambiguous, while S → aSb | ab is unambiguous and generates the same language.
  2. (B) true: the leftmost and the rightmost derivation of a string with two or more symbols differ as sequences of sentential forms even though they describe the same tree.
  3. (C) true: that is exactly the definition of unambiguity, stated string by string.
  4. (D) false: deciding ambiguity of a context-free grammar is undecidable in general; only for restricted classes (such as deterministic grammars, or bounded lookahead) is it decidable.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Confusing “unique derivation” with “unique derivation tree”. Only the second is the definition of unambiguity.

TOC-U2-Q61NATmedium

Let G be the grammar S → SS | a | b. How many distinct derivation trees does G have for the string aab?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. With a single branching production S → SS, a derivation tree is a full binary tree whose leaves spell the string, and the number of such trees is counted by splitting the string into a nonempty prefix and a nonempty suffix.

Step by step.

  1. The root must use S → SS, because aab has three symbols and no other production of G produces more than one symbol.
  2. The two possible splits into nonempty parts are a·ab and aa·b.
  3. For a·ab the right part ab has the single tree S ⇒ SS ⇒ ab, so this split gives one tree.
  4. For aa·b the left part aa has the single tree S ⇒ SS ⇒ aa, so this split gives one tree.
  5. Total: 1 + 1 = 2 trees, so the grammar is ambiguous for aab.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Answering 1 by assuming the grammar is unambiguous because it is so small, or answering 3 by allowing the empty split ε·aab.

TOC-U2-Q62MCQhard

Let L = { aibjck : i = j or j = k, i, j, k ≥ 1 }, with L. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. <span class="mm">L</span> is not context-free.
  2. <span class="mm">L</span> is context-free and has an unambiguous grammar.
  3. <span class="mm">L</span> is context-free, and every grammar for <span class="mm">L</span> is ambiguous.
  4. <span class="mm">L</span> is regular.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. L is the union of the two context-free languages {aibick} and {aibjcj}, so it is context-free. It is the standard example of an inherently ambiguous context-free language: the two conditions i = j and j = k pull in different directions, and any grammar must sometimes delay the decision past the point where the information needed to make it is still available.

Step by step.

  1. Closure under union gives that L is context-free: S → S1 | S2 with S1 → aS1c | X, X → aXb | ab generates the first part and S2 → aS2 | Y, Y → bYc | bc generates the second.
  2. L is not regular, since intersecting it with a*b*c* gives {anbncn}, which is not regular.
  3. For a string anbncn with i = j = k the two reasons coincide, and the classic pumping argument shows that no single grammar can decide early enough which of the two equalities holds without also being able to count all three blocks; the language is therefore inherently ambiguous.
  4. So L is context-free and every grammar for it is ambiguous.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Concluding “not context-free” because {anbncn} is not, or “unambiguous” because each of the two parts is a deterministic language. Union of two unambiguous languages need not be unambiguous.

TOC-U2-Q63MSQhard

Select every statement that is TRUE.

  1. Every deterministic context-free language has an unambiguous grammar.
  2. The union of two unambiguous context-free languages can be inherently ambiguous.
  3. It is decidable whether a given context-free grammar is ambiguous.
  4. If every string of <span class="mm">L(G)</span> has exactly one rightmost derivation, then <span class="mm">G</span> is unambiguous.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Unambiguity is preserved by determinism (a DPDA makes one choice at every step), but not by union, and ambiguity of an arbitrary grammar is undecidable.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: run the deterministic pushdown automaton on a string. Its run is unique, so the string has one leftmost derivation, hence one tree.
  2. (B) true: {aibick} and {aibjcj} are unambiguous, but their union is inherently ambiguous, so no grammar for the union is unambiguous.
  3. (C) false: ambiguity of context-free grammars is undecidable — it is one of the standard consequences of the Post correspondence problem.
  4. (D) true: rightmost derivations and trees are in one-to-one correspondence, so a unique rightmost derivation per string means a unique tree per string.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming decidability because the definition of ambiguity is a simple statement about trees. Deciding whether two different trees exist for some string is exactly the hard part.

CNF & GNF normal forms · 19
TOC-U2-Q14MCQeasy

Which condition must every production of a grammar in Chomsky Normal Form satisfy?

  1. A → BC with both B and C nonterminals, or A → a with a a terminal.
  2. A → aα , where a is a terminal and α a string of nonterminals.
  3. A → α with |α| ≤ 2 .
  4. A → aB , where a is a terminal and B a nonterminal.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Chomsky Normal Form allows exactly two shapes: a binary split into two nonterminals, or a single terminal. The optional exception is S → ε, allowed only when S never appears on a right-hand side.

Step by step.

  1. A binary production A → BC doubles the tree height but keeps every derivation binary, which is what makes CYK run in O(n³).
  2. A terminal production A → a supplies the leaves.
  3. Any other production is rewritten: long right-hand sides are split with fresh nonterminals, terminals mixed with nonterminals are isolated, ε- and unit productions are removed.
  4. Hence (A) is the definition.

Answer: Option (A)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is the definition of Greibach Normal Form, not CNF.
  • (C) would permit A → aB and A → BC alike; the mixed case A → aB is not allowed in CNF.
  • (D) is precisely the mixed shape CNF forbids; it must be rewritten as A → AₐB, Aₐ → a.

Common misconception. Allowing A → aB because it "looks binary". CNF counts nonterminals on the right, not symbols.

TOC-U2-Q15MCQeasy

Which of the following grammars is in Chomsky Normal Form?

  1. S → aB , A → a , B → b
  2. S → ABC , A → a , B → b , C → c
  3. S → Sb , A → a , B → b
  4. S → AB , A → a , B → b
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Check every production against the two permitted shapes A → BC and A → a. One violating production disqualifies the grammar.

Step by step.

  1. (A) S → aB mixes a terminal with a nonterminal — not CNF.
  2. (B) S → ABC has three symbols on the right — not CNF.
  3. (C) S → Sb mixes a nonterminal with a terminal, and is also left-recursive — not CNF.
  4. (D) S → AB is binary, A → a and B → b are terminal productions. Every production fits, so (D) is in CNF.

Answer: Option (D)

Common misconception. Overlooking that in (A) and (C) a terminal and a nonterminal appear together. In CNF a terminal may appear on a right-hand side only as the whole right-hand side.

TOC-U2-Q16NATeasy

A grammar is in Chomsky Normal Form, and the derivation tree of a string w with |w| = 9 is drawn in the usual way, with the terminals as leaves and every internal node having exactly two children. How many internal nodes does that tree have?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In CNF the only internal nodes come from productions A → BC, and each such node has exactly two children. A tree in which every internal node has exactly two children is a full binary tree, so leaves = internal nodes + 1.

Step by step.

  1. The yield has length 9, so the tree has 9 terminal leaves.
  2. Full binary tree identity: L = I + 1, hence I = L − 1 = 9 − 1 = 8.
  3. Equivalently, the derivation uses n − 1 = 8 binary productions and n = 9 terminal productions, so 2n − 1 = 17 steps in total, of which 8 create internal nodes.

Answer: 8

Common misconception. Answering 9 by assuming one internal node per leaf, or 17 by counting derivation steps and calling them internal nodes. The 9 terminal productions create leaves, not internal nodes.

TOC-U2-Q17MCQmedium

Which of the following is TRUE of Greibach Normal Form?

  1. Every production is A → BC or A → a .
  2. Every production is A → aα with a a terminal and α a string of nonterminals, and every context-free language without ε has such a grammar.
  3. It is the class of grammars accepted by LL(1) parsers, and it permits ε -productions anywhere.
  4. It guarantees that the grammar is unambiguous and that every string has exactly one derivation tree.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In Greibach Normal Form every right-hand side starts with exactly one terminal, followed by any number of nonterminals. Every production therefore consumes exactly one input symbol, which makes the number of steps to derive a string of length n exactly n.

Step by step.

  1. The definition is the first half of (B).
  2. Existence: every CFL without ε can be converted to GNF by first reaching CNF and then repeatedly substituting so that each right-hand side begins with a terminal. So the second half of (B) is also true.
  3. (A) describes CNF, not GNF.
  4. (C) and (D) overreach: GNF grammars can still be ambiguous, and the class of LL(1) grammars is strictly smaller and defined by the FIRST/FOLLOW condition, not by GNF.

Answer: Option (B)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is Chomsky Normal Form.
  • (C) confuses a normal form with a parsing restriction; LL(1) is about disjoint FIRST sets, and GNF permits ε only for the start symbol, not "anywhere".
  • (D) GNF constrains the shape of productions, not the number of parse trees: S → aS | aS | a is in GNF and ambiguous.

Common misconception. Believing a normal form buys determinism. Normal forms standardise production shapes for algorithms; they say nothing about ambiguity.

TOC-U2-Q18NATmedium

Apply the standard ε-elimination procedure to the grammar S → AB, A → aA | ε, B → bB | ε. How many productions does the resulting grammar have?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A nonterminal is nullable if it derives ε. The procedure adds, for every production, all variants obtained by deleting any nonempty subset of nullable symbols from its right-hand side; it then drops all ε-productions except possibly S → ε when S is nullable.

Step by step.

  1. Nullable nonterminals: A and B, and then S because S → AB and both are nullable.
  2. A → aA gives the extra variant A → a: 2 productions.
  3. B → bB gives the extra variant B → b: 2 productions.
  4. S → AB with both nullable gives S → AB, S → A, S → B; and since S is nullable, S → ε is retained: 4 productions.
  5. Total 2 + 2 + 4 = 8 productions.

Answer: 8

Common misconception. Dropping S → ε along with the others. Because S is the start symbol and is nullable, that production must stay — otherwise the empty string leaves the language. Also, deleting both nullable symbols at once is only one of the four variants; S → A and S → B are needed too.

TOC-U2-Q19MSQhard

Select every statement that is TRUE.

  1. Every context-free grammar whose language does not contain ε has an equivalent grammar in Chomsky Normal Form.
  2. In a CNF grammar, every derivation of a string of length n ≥ 1 from S uses exactly 2n − 1 productions.
  3. In a GNF grammar the right-hand side of every production begins with a terminal, so the next input symbol restricts the applicable productions to those starting with that terminal.
  4. Every grammar in Chomsky Normal Form is unambiguous.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Normal forms are about production shape; they are used to make algorithms uniform, and they deliberately do not constrain ambiguity.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the standard CNF conversion theorem: remove ε- and unit productions, isolate terminals, then split long right-hand sides with fresh nonterminals. True.
  2. (B) In CNF every internal node is binary and every leaf is one terminal production. A tree for n terminals has n − 1 internal nodes and n leaves, and each node is one production: (n − 1) + n = 2n − 1. True.
  3. (C) is the defining structural property of GNF and the reason GNF grammars admit a left-to-right predictive parse. True.
