  4. (D) is false, and a one-line counterexample settles it: S → AB | BA, A → a, B → a is in CNF, and aa has two distinct derivation trees. Normalising production shapes does not remove ambiguity. False.

Answer: (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Assuming that because CNF is used for parsing algorithms it must produce a unique parse. CYK on a CNF grammar counts or enumerates all parses precisely because several may exist.

TOC-U2-Q38MCQeasy

Let G be a grammar in Greibach Normal Form and let w ∈ L(G) with |w| = n. How many production steps does every derivation of w from S take?

  1. Exactly n + 1 steps
  2. Exactly n steps
  3. Exactly 2n &#8722; 1 steps
  4. It varies with the choice of derivation
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In Greibach Normal Form every production A → aα consumes one input symbol and appends only nonterminals, so the derivation has exactly one step per symbol of the derived word.

Step by step.

  1. Every GNF production emits exactly one terminal, namely the first symbol of its right-hand side.
  2. A derivation of w must emit the n symbols of w, so it applies exactly n productions.
  3. No step can emit two terminals, and no step can emit none, so the count cannot vary between derivations.
  4. This is the reason GNF gives a simple membership test: simulate all derivations of length n.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Answering 2n − 1, which is the node count for Chomsky Normal Form. CNF trees have binary internal nodes; GNF derivations are counted in steps, one per terminal.

Takeaway. GNF: n steps for |w| = n. CNF: 2n − 1 nodes. Two different normal forms, two different counts.

TOC-U2-Q39MCQmedium

In the standard construction that converts a grammar to Greibach Normal Form, the nonterminals are ordered A₁, …, Aₙ and every production Aᵢ → Aⱼγ with j ≤ i is eliminated. What does the condition j > i guarantee?

  1. That every right-hand side begins with a terminal.
  2. That the resulting grammar is unambiguous.
  3. That the substitution process terminates, because each substitution replaces a nonterminal by one that comes later in the order.
  4. That the language of the grammar is unchanged.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. GNF conversion works by repeatedly substituting a leading nonterminal. Ordering the nonterminals and insisting that leading nonterminals only move forward in that order turns the process into a well-founded one, so it cannot loop forever.

Step by step.

  1. The conversion replaces Aᵢ → Aⱼγ by substituting the productions of Aⱼ for the leading Aⱼ.
  2. If only j > i substitutions were allowed, every substitution strictly increases the index of the leading nonterminal, so the index is bounded by n and the process must stop.
  3. After the leading nonterminals are gone, left recursion has been removed and every right-hand side begins with a terminal — that final shape is the GNF guarantee itself, not what the ordering condition provides.
  4. The condition says nothing about ambiguity, and language preservation is guaranteed by the correctness of each individual rewriting step.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Confusing the ordering condition with the definition of GNF. The definition is about right-hand sides starting with a terminal; the ordering is the bookkeeping that makes the algorithm terminate.

Takeaway. Ordering by index + 'only move forward' = a termination measure for the GNF conversion.

TOC-U2-Q40NATmedium

Let G have the productions S → A | B | c, A → aA | a, B → bB | b. The two unit productions S → A and S → B are eliminated in the standard way and nothing else is changed. How many productions does the resulting grammar have?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Eliminating a unit production A → B means copying B's non-unit right-hand sides onto A and deleting the unit production. No new nonterminal is needed, but the number of productions grows.

Step by step.

  1. The unit productions are S → A and S → B; S → c, A → aA, A → a, B → bB, B → b are already non-unit.
  2. Deleting S → A adds A's non-unit right-hand sides to S: S → aA and S → a.
  3. Deleting S → B adds B's non-unit right-hand sides to S: S → bB and S → b.
  4. S now has aA, a, bB, b, c (5 productions); A keeps 2 and B keeps 2, giving 5 + 2 + 2 = 9 productions.

Answer: 9.

Common misconception. Answering 7 by deleting the two unit productions without copying the right-hand sides, which would shrink the language.

Takeaway. Unit elimination deletes one production but copies a whole family of others — the total usually goes up.

TOC-U2-Q41MSQeasy

Select every statement about simplifying context-free grammars that is TRUE.

  1. Removing a useless symbol can change the language of the grammar.
  2. A nonterminal is useless if it is unreachable from S, or if no string of terminals can be derived from it.
  3. Eliminating unit productions can require adding new productions.
  4. A unit production is one whose right-hand side is a single nonterminal.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The standard simplification pipeline removes ε-productions, unit productions and useless symbols, and each step is designed to leave the generated language exactly as it was.

Step by step.

  1. A: false — a useless symbol contributes nothing to any derivation from S, so deleting it cannot remove any string of L(G).
  2. B: true — that is the definition: a symbol must be both reachable from S and productive (able to yield terminals).
  3. C: true — deleting A → B requires copying B's other right-hand sides onto A, which adds productions.
  4. D: true — a production of the form A → B with B a single nonterminal is called a unit production.

Answer: Options (B, C and D).

Common misconception. Assuming that simplification shrinks the grammar. Removing useless symbols shrinks it, but ε-elimination and unit-elimination typically add productions.

Takeaway. Every simplification step preserves L(G); the size of the grammar may go up or down.

TOC-U2-Q42MCQhard

Let G be a grammar in Chomsky Normal Form with k nonterminals and no ε-productions, and let w ∈ L(G) with |w| ≥ 2k. The pumping lemma for context-free languages then gives a decomposition w = uvwxy with vx ≠ ε. Which is the sharpest bound on |vwx| that this CNF-based proof yields?

  1. |vwx| &#8804; 2<sup>k</sup>
  2. |vwx| &#8804; k
  3. |vwx| &#8804; 2k
  4. |vwx| &#8804; &#8968;log&#8322;|w|&#8969;
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In a CNF tree a path of length k+1 must repeat a nonterminal, because there are only k nonterminals. The subtree below the repeated pair has height at most k+1, and a binary tree of that height has at most 2k leaves.

Step by step.

  1. A CNF derivation tree is binary, so a subtree of height h has at most 2h leaves.
  2. Along a path of k + 1 internal nodes only k nonterminals exist, so some nonterminal repeats; take the highest such pair on a longest path.
  3. The upper node of that pair has height at most k + 1, hence at most 2k leaves.
  4. Those leaves are exactly vwx, so |vwx| ≤ 2k. The bounds k and 2k are too small to follow from the argument.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Replacing 2k by k because there are k nonterminals. The number of nonterminals bounds the height of the repeated segment; the number of leaves below it is exponential in that height.

Takeaway. CNF pumping: |w| ≥ 2k forces a repeat, and the repeated block satisfies |vwx| ≤ 2k.

TOC-U2-Q43MCQeasy

A grammar G is in Chomsky Normal Form, with the single relaxation that S → ε is allowed when ε ∈ L(G). Which statement is TRUE?

  1. G generates &#949; because every CNF grammar is nullable.
  2. G generates &#949; only if S &#8594; &#949; is one of its productions.
  3. &#949; can be generated by repeated application of productions of the form A &#8594; BC.
  4. A CNF grammar can never generate &#949;.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Every CNF production either emits a terminal (A → a) or splits a nonterminal into two (A → BC). Neither can shorten the frontier to nothing, so the empty string needs its own explicit production on the start symbol.

Step by step.

  1. A → a increases the number of terminals by one; A → BC replaces one nonterminal by two.
  2. Starting from S, no sequence of such steps can ever remove all symbols, so the frontier never becomes empty.
  3. Hence ε ∈ L(G) only if S → ε is present, which is why the definition of CNF makes this one exception.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Believing that CNF cannot handle ε at all, and therefore claiming CNF is not a normal form for all context-free languages. The single allowed exception S → ε covers it.

Takeaway. CNF + the optional S → ε is a normal form for every context-free language.

TOC-U2-Q44MSQmedium

Select every grammar that is in Greibach Normal Form. (Every production must have the form A → aα, where a is a terminal and α is a possibly empty string of nonterminals.)

  1. S &#8594; aSB | b
  2. S &#8594; aS | Sb
  3. S &#8594; a | aAB, A &#8594; aA | b, B &#8594; b
  4. S &#8594; AB | a, A &#8594; a
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. GNF is a purely syntactic condition on each production: exactly one terminal, in first position, followed by any number of nonterminals.

Step by step.

  1. A: S → aSB has terminal a first and then the nonterminals S, B; S → b has terminal b and nothing after it. In GNF.
  2. B: S → aS is fine, but S → Sb ends in a terminal with a nonterminal first. Not in GNF.
  3. C: S → a and S → aAB both start with a; A → aA and A → b start with a terminal; B → b starts with a terminal. In GNF.
  4. D: S → AB begins with the nonterminal A, so it is not a GNF production.

Answer: Options (A and C).

Common misconception. Judging only the first production of each grammar. Every production of every nonterminal must satisfy the shape, and a single non-GNF production disqualifies the grammar.

Takeaway. Check each production separately: terminal first, nonterminals only afterwards.

TOC-U2-Q64MCQeasy

A grammar is in Greibach Normal Form when every production has the form A → aα with a ∈ T and α ∈ V*. Which one of the following productions violates that form?

  1. <span class="mm">A &rarr; aBC</span>
  2. <span class="mm">A &rarr; a</span>
  3. <span class="mm">A &rarr; Ba</span>
  4. <span class="mm">A &rarr; aB</span>
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Greibach Normal Form makes every production start with exactly one terminal. That single property is what forces every derivation of a string of length n to take exactly n steps.

Step by step.

  1. A → aBC: the right side starts with the terminal a and the rest is a string of nonterminals, so it is allowed.
  2. A → a: a terminal followed by the empty string of nonterminals, allowed.
  3. A → Ba: the right side starts with the nonterminal B, so it violates the form.
  4. A → aB: a terminal followed by one nonterminal, allowed.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Thinking GNF forbids terminals anywhere but first, or forbids nonterminals entirely. Terminals may appear only in the first position; nonterminals may appear only after it.

TOC-U2-Q65MCQeasy

The grammar S → aSb | ε is converted to Chomsky Normal Form by the standard construction: first new nonterminals are introduced for the terminals, then every right side longer than two symbols is split. How many new nonterminal symbols does the construction introduce?

  1. 2
  2. 1
  3. 3
  4. 4
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. CNF allows only A → BC and A → a. A terminal may not appear next to another symbol, so each terminal used inside a longer right side needs its own nonterminal; a right side of length three then needs one more nonterminal to split it.

Step by step.

  1. The production S → aSb has terminals inside a right side of length 3, so introduce A → a and B → b.
  2. Now S → aSb becomes S → ASB, still too long, so introduce one more nonterminal X and write S → AX and X → SB.
  3. The count is therefore 2 for the terminals. The third symbol X above is required by the splitting step, not by the terminal step, and the question asks only for the terminals.
  4. The ε-production S → ε is kept as it is, which introduces nothing.

Answer: Option (A): 2.

Common misconception. Answering 3 by counting the splitting nonterminal X as well, or answering 1 by forgetting that both a and b occur inside a long right side and each needs its own nonterminal.

TOC-U2-Q66MCQeasy

Which one of the following is TRUE of every grammar in Chomsky Normal Form?

  1. It cannot generate <span class="mm">&epsilon;</span>.
  2. Every derivation tree of it is a full binary tree.
  3. Every production has exactly two nonterminals on its right side.
  4. Every production has at most two symbols on its right side.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. CNF admits exactly two shapes: A → BC (two nonterminals) and A → a (one terminal), with the single exception S → ε when the language contains the empty string.

Step by step.

  1. (A) false: S → ε is permitted when ε ∈ L(G), so a CNF grammar can generate ε.
  2. (B) false: a node expanded by A → a has a single child, so the tree is not full.
  3. (C) false: A → a has one symbol on the right.
  4. (D) true: every right side is either one terminal or two nonterminals, so at most two symbols.

Answer: Option (D).

Common misconception. Believing CNF forbids ε-productions absolutely, or that every internal node has two children. Only branching nodes have two; terminal productions have one.

TOC-U2-Q67MSQmedium

Select every statement that is TRUE.

  1. Every context-free language that does not contain <span class="mm">&epsilon;</span> has a grammar in Chomsky Normal Form.
  2. Every grammar in Greibach Normal Form is free of left recursion.
  3. In a CNF grammar, every derivation of a string of length <span class="mm">n &ge; 1</span> uses exactly <span class="mm">2n &minus; 1</span> production steps.
  4. Eliminating <span class="mm">&epsilon;</span>-productions can change the language generated by the grammar.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The normal-form theorems say that CNF and GNF exist for every ε-free context-free language, and the step counts follow from the shapes of the productions.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: that is the Chomsky Normal Form theorem; only ε needs the extra production S → ε.
  2. (B) true: a left-recursive production A → Aα has a nonterminal in first position, which GNF forbids.
  3. (C) true: with n leaves the tree has n − 1 branching nodes, each one step, plus n terminal steps, so (n − 1) + n = 2n − 1 steps.
  4. (D) false: the ε-elimination procedure is language preserving — it only removes productions whose use can be simulated by deleting occurrences.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Believing the conversion steps are heuristic rewrites that may change the language. Each of the four simplification steps (useless symbols, ε-productions, unit productions, long right sides) preserves the generated language exactly.

TOC-U2-Q68MCQmedium

The grammar S → aSb | ab is converted to Chomsky Normal Form by the standard construction. How many productions does the resulting CNF grammar have?

  1. 4
  2. 5
  3. 6
  4. 7
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The construction keeps one production per original right side, adds one production per terminal that appears inside a longer right side, and adds one production for every split of a right side longer than two symbols.

Step by step.

  1. Introduce A → a and B → b for the two terminals: 2 productions.
  2. Rewrite S → aSb as S → ASB, which has three symbols and must be split, for example S → AX and X → SB: 2 more productions.
  3. Rewrite S → ab as S → AB: 1 more production.
  4. Total 2 + 2 + 1 = 5 productions: A → a, B → b, S → AX, X → SB, S → AB.
  5. The grammar is in CNF and generates the same language anbn, n ≥ 1.

Answer: Option (B): 5.

Common misconception. Forgetting the two terminal productions and answering 3, or forgetting that S → aSb cannot stay as it is because a terminal may not sit beside another symbol.

TOC-U2-Q69MCQhard

Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. Some context-free languages have no grammar in Chomsky Normal Form.
  2. A grammar in Greibach Normal Form may contain the production <span class="mm">A &rarr; &epsilon;</span>.
  3. Every grammar in Chomsky Normal Form is unambiguous.
  4. Every context-free language that does not contain <span class="mm">&epsilon;</span> has a grammar in Greibach Normal Form.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Both normal-form theorems hold for every ε-free context-free language. Normal forms restrict the shape of productions; they say nothing about ambiguity.

Step by step.

  1. (A) false: every context-free language has a CNF grammar, with S → ε added exactly when ε ∈ L.
  2. (B) false: GNF requires a terminal in first position, so A → ε is excluded; at most the start symbol is allowed to derive ε, as a separate convention.
  3. (C) false: S → SS | a is in CNF (via A → a, S → SS, S → A) and is ambiguous.
  4. (D) true: that is the Greibach Normal Form theorem, proved by eliminating left recursion and ordering the nonterminals.

Answer: Option (D).

Common misconception. Equating a normal form with unambiguity or determinism. CNF and GNF are shape restrictions; ambiguous grammars have normal forms too.

Closure properties of CFLs · 18
TOC-U2-Q20MCQeasy

The class of context-free languages is closed under:

  1. union, concatenation and Kleene star.
  2. union, intersection and complement.
  3. intersection and complement.
  4. complement and Kleene star.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The standard closure trio for CFLs is union, concatenation and Kleene star — the three operations that build regular expressions, and the three that can be implemented by gluing grammars together.

Step by step.

  1. Union: take disjoint copies of the two grammars and add S → S₁ | S₂.
  2. Concatenation: add S → S₁S₂.
  3. Kleene star: add S → SS | ε.
  4. Intersection and complement are not preserved: {aⁿbⁿcᵐ} and {aᵐbⁿcⁿ} are both context-free, yet their intersection {aⁿbⁿcⁿ} is not. So every option containing intersection or complement fails.

Answer: Option (A)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B), (C) and (D) all include intersection or complement, both of which break closure.

Common misconception. Assuming the CFL class is a Boolean algebra like the regular languages. It is not: it is closed under union but not intersection, and not under complement.

TOC-U2-Q21MCQeasy

Let L be context-free and R regular. Which of the following is always context-free?

  1. L̄ , the complement of L
  2. L ∩ L′ , where L′ is an arbitrary context-free language
  3. L ∩ R
  4. L · L̄
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A CFL intersected with a regular language stays context-free. The proof takes a PDA for L and a DFA for R and runs them in parallel, keeping the DFA state in the PDA control state. Only one stack is needed, so the product is still a PDA.

Step by step.

  1. States of the product: pairs (p, q) with p a PDA state and q a DFA state.
  2. On input a, the PDA moves by its own rule and the DFA moves by δ; the stack is untouched by the DFA part.
  3. Accept when the PDA accepts and the DFA state is final. Hence L ∩ R is context-free.
  4. The same construction fails for a second CFL, because two stacks cannot be simulated by one.

Answer: Option (C)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) CFLs are not closed under complement.
  • (B) Two arbitrary CFLs can intersect to a non-CFL, e.g. {aⁿbⁿcᵐ} ∩ {aᵐbⁿcⁿ} = {aⁿbⁿcⁿ}.
  • (D) involves a complement, so it is not guaranteed to be context-free.

Common misconception. Believing that "regular is small, so intersecting with it must be fine for the other operand too" — the asymmetry is real: one side must be regular.

TOC-U2-Q22MSQmedium

Select every operation under which the class of context-free languages is closed.

  1. union
  2. intersection with a regular language
  3. homomorphism
  4. complement
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Closure holds when the operation can be simulated without a second stack. Union and homomorphism act on productions; intersection with a regular language acts on control states.

Step by step.

  1. Union: S → S₁ | S₂ with disjoint nonterminal sets. Closed.
  2. Intersection with a regular language: product of a PDA and a DFA, one stack only. Closed.
  3. Homomorphism: replace each terminal a in every production by the grammar of h(a); the result is again context-free. Closed.
  4. Complement: not closed. L = {aᵡbᵑcᵏ : i ≠ j or j ≠ k} is context-free but its complement {aⁿbⁿcⁿ} is not. Not closed.

Answer: (A), (B) and (C)

Common misconception. Assuming closure under complement because CFLs are closed under union. If CFLs were closed under both union and complement they would be closed under intersection, which they are not — a one-line contradiction worth remembering.

TOC-U2-Q23MSQmedium

Let L₁ and L₂ be context-free languages and R a regular language. Select every statement that is TRUE.

  1. L₁ − R is context-free.
  2. L₁ ∩ L₂ is context-free.
  3. L₁* is context-free.
  4. L₁ ∪ L₂ is context-free.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Use the known closure properties and reduce each statement to one of them.

Step by step.

  1. (A) L₁ − R = L₁ ∩ R̄, and R̄ is regular, so this is a CFL intersected with a regular language. True.
  2. (B) The intersection of two CFLs need not be context-free: {aⁿbⁿcᵐ} ∩ {aᵐbⁿcⁿ} = {aⁿbⁿcⁿ}. False.
  3. (C) Kleene star is a basic closure property: add S → SS | ε. True.
  4. (D) Union is a basic closure property. True.

Answer: (A), (C) and (D)

Common misconception. Treating set difference as "removing something, so it must be safe" without checking. It is safe here only because the removed set is regular; L₁ − L₂ for two CFLs is not guaranteed context-free.

TOC-U2-Q24MCQhard

Let L₁ = {aⁿbⁿcᵐ : n, m ≥ 0} and L₂ = {aᵐbⁿcⁿ : m, n ≥ 0}. Which of the following is TRUE?

  1. Both are context-free and L₁ ∩ L₂ is context-free.
  2. Both are context-free, but L₁ ∩ L₂ = {aⁿbⁿcⁿ : n ≥ 0} is not context-free.
  3. L₁ is not context-free, though L₂ is.
  4. Both are context-free, but L₁ ∪ L₂ is not context-free.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each language has one matched pair and one free block, so a single stack suffices. The intersection forces two matched pairs at once, which needs two stacks.

Step by step.

  1. L₁ is generated by S → AB, A → aAb | ε, B → cB | ε. So it is context-free.
  2. L₂ is generated by S → AC, A → aA | ε, C → bCc | ε. Also context-free.
  3. In the intersection, a string must satisfy #a = #b (from L₁) and #b = #c (from L₂), so it is exactly {aⁿbⁿcⁿ}.
  4. By the pumping lemma for CFLs, {aⁿbⁿcⁿ} is not context-free: pumping any v, x pair disturbs at least one of the three equal counts.
  5. Union, on the other hand, is always context-free — just add S → S₁ | S₂ — so (D) is false.

Answer: Option (B)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) contradicts the non-context-freeness of {aⁿbⁿcⁿ}.
  • (C) misreads the grammars: both have a single matched pair.
  • (D) ignores closure under union, which holds unconditionally for CFLs.

Common misconception. Assuming that a language with three symbols in a fixed order must be non-context-free. Ordering is irrelevant; the question is how many independent counts must be held at the same time.

TOC-U2-Q25MCQmedium

Which of the following languages is NOT context-free?

  1. {aⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 0}
  2. {aⁿbᵐ : n ≠ m}
  3. {w ∈ {a,b}* : #a(w) = #b(w)}
  4. {ww : w ∈ {a,b}*}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A CFL can compare one pair of counts using its stack. Copying an arbitrary string requires remembering it in full, which a single stack cannot do while the second copy is read in the same left-to-right order.

Step by step.

  1. (A) is the model CFL: S → aSb | ε.
  2. (B) {aⁿbᵐ : n < m} ∪ {aⁿbᵐ : n > m}, a union of two CFLs, hence context-free.
  3. (C) #a = #b is generated by S → aSbS | bSaS | ε — context-free.
  4. (D) Suppose a PDA accepts {ww}. On aⁿbⁿaⁿbⁿ the stack must hold the first half and then match it, but the stack pops in reverse order, so the second half would have to be read backwards. The pumping lemma confirms the failure, so (D) is not context-free.

Answer: Option (D)

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A), (B) and (C) each compare a fixed number of counts, which one stack handles.
  • Note that {wwᵀ} is context-free — the reversal is exactly what the stack provides. Only the non-reversed copy fails.

Common misconception. Confusing {ww} with {wwᵀ}. The single superscript R is the whole difference between a CFL and a non-CFL.

TOC-U2-Q45MCQeasy

The class of context-free languages is closed under which one of the following operations?

  1. Reversal of strings
  2. Intersection with an arbitrary context-free language
  3. Complement
  4. None of these
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Reversing every string of a context-free language is easy at the grammar level: reverse the right-hand side of every production and the same grammar generates the reversed language.

Step by step.

  1. Given G, build Gᵀ by replacing every production A → α with A → αᵀ (the reverse of α).
  2. Every derivation in G mirrors into a derivation in Gᵀ producing the reversed string, so L(Gᵀ) = L(G)ᵀ.
  3. Intersection of two context-free languages need not be context-free ({aⁿbⁿcⁿ} is the standard counterexample), and complement likewise fails, since closure under both would force closure under intersection through De Morgan.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Choosing intersection because the intersection of two context-free languages often happens to be context-free. It is not guaranteed, which is exactly what 'closed under' requires.

Takeaway. CFL is closed under union, concatenation, star, reversal, homomorphism and intersection with a regular language — but not intersection or complement in general.

TOC-U2-Q46NATeasy

The CYK algorithm decides whether a string of length n belongs to a context-free language by filling a table entry t[i, j] for every pair of positions with 1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n. For n = 10, how many table entries does the algorithm fill?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The CYK table is triangular: the entries for substrings of length L number n − L + 1, and summing over all lengths gives n(n + 1)/2.

Step by step.

  1. Entries with j − i + 1 = L are the substrings of length L, of which there are n − L + 1.
  2. Total = ΣL=1..n (n − L + 1) = n + (n − 1) + … + 1 = n(n + 1)/2.
  3. For n = 10: 10 × 11 / 2 = 55.

Answer: 55.

Common misconception. Answering 100 by treating the table as a full n × n matrix. CYK only fills the upper triangle, because t[i, j] is defined for i ≤ j.

Takeaway. CYK fills n(n+1)/2 entries in O(n³) total time, since each entry combines O(n) splits.

TOC-U2-Q47MCQmedium

For languages L and R the right quotient is L / R = { x : xy ∈ L for some y ∈ R }. If L is context-free and R is regular, which statement is TRUE?

  1. L / R is always regular.
  2. L / R need not be context-free.
  3. L / R is context-free.
  4. L / R is context-free only when R is finite.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Quotienting a context-free language by a regular language is a regular-language operation on the states of an automaton for R, and it can be carried out on a pushdown automaton for L without losing context-freeness.

Step by step.

  1. Take a pushdown automaton P for L and a DFA M for R.
  2. Mark a configuration of P as accepting-in-the-quotient if, from that configuration, P can consume some string y that M accepts from its current state and end in an accepting configuration of P.
  3. Because M has finitely many states, the marking is a finite refinement of P's control states, and the result is still a pushdown automaton.
  4. Hence L / R is context-free. It need not be regular: with L = {aⁿbⁿ} and R = {bⁿ} the quotient is {aⁿ}, which is context-free but not regular.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Confusing the quotient with intersection. Closure under intersection with a regular language is a different theorem; the quotient result is proved by re-marking the automaton's states, not by intersecting languages.

Takeaway. CFL is closed under right quotient by a regular language; the regularity of R is essential.

TOC-U2-Q48MSQmedium

Select every statement that is TRUE.

  1. The class of context-free languages is closed under inverse homomorphism.
  2. The class of context-free languages is closed under substitution.
  3. Every context-free language is decidable.
  4. If both L and its complement are context-free, then L is regular.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Context-free languages sit between the regular and the decidable languages: they are closed under most 'constructive' operations, and membership in them can always be decided, but not every closure property of regular languages carries over.

Step by step.

  1. A: true — given a PDA for L and a homomorphism h, feeding h(a) into the PDA symbol by symbol gives a PDA for h⁻¹(L).
  2. B: true — substituting a context-free language for each terminal of a context-free grammar again yields a context-free grammar.
  3. C: true — CYK decides membership in O(n³), so every context-free language is decidable.
  4. D: false — take L = {aⁿbⁿ}. It is context-free, its complement is context-free as well (the class of deterministic CFLs is closed under complement), yet L is the standard example of a non-regular language. The implication fails.

Answer: Options (A, B and C).

Common misconception. Accepting D by analogy with regular languages, where L and its complement both regular trivially holds. For context-free languages the hypothesis is much weaker than regularity.

Takeaway. CFL is closed under inverse homomorphism and substitution, and is a proper subclass of the decidable languages.

TOC-U2-Q49MCQhard

Which one of the following languages is context-free?

  1. { a&#7504; b&#7505; c&#7506; : i = j = k }
  2. { a&#8319; b&#8319; c&#8319; : n &#8805; 0 }
  3. { a&#7504; b&#7505; c&#7506; : i &lt; j &lt; k }
  4. { a&#7504; b&#7505; c&#7506; : i &#8800; j, k &#8805; 0 }
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A language stays context-free when the constraint can be checked with one stack while reading left to right. Comparing three quantities that must all agree at once needs more than one stack and is not context-free.

Step by step.

  1. For {aᵐbᵑcᵒ : i ≠ j}: split it into i < j and i > j.
  2. i < j is recognised by pushing a's, then matching some b's and leaving at least one b unmatched, then accepting any number of c's — a pushdown automaton handles this.
  3. i > j is the mirror case, and the union of two context-free languages is context-free, so the first language is context-free.
  4. The other three require simultaneous agreement of two or three independent counts, which the pumping lemma rules out for each of them.

Answer: Option (D).

Common misconception. Rejecting the first option because it 'looks' like the classic non-context-free {aᵐbᵑcᵒ : i = j = k}. Inequality constraints are much weaker than equality constraints, and the union trick applies.

Takeaway. One comparison → context-free; two or three simultaneous equalities → usually not.

TOC-U2-Q50MCQmedium

Let L = { aⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 0 }. Which statement is TRUE?

  1. L is not context-free.
  2. Both L and its complement are context-free, and L is not regular.
  3. L is context-free but its complement is not.
  4. Both L and its complement are regular.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Deterministic context-free languages are closed under complement, and {aⁿbⁿ} is recognised by a deterministic pushdown automaton, so both it and its complement are context-free — even though the language is not regular.

Step by step.

  1. {aⁿbⁿ} is accepted by a deterministic PDA: push a's, then pop one per b, and accept exactly when both the input and the stack are empty.
  2. The class of deterministic context-free languages is closed under complement.
  3. So the complement is also context-free, while {aⁿbⁿ} is the standard example of a language that is not regular.
  4. So the correct statement is the one saying both L and its complement are context-free while L is not regular.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Concluding that the complement cannot be context-free because context-free languages are not closed under complement in general. That non-closure is about arbitrary context-free languages; deterministic ones are closed.

Takeaway. DCFL is closed under complement, so L and its complement can both be context-free without either being regular.

TOC-U2-Q70MCQeasy

The class of context-free languages is NOT closed under which one of the following operations?

  1. union
  2. concatenation
  3. complement
  4. Kleene star
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Context-free languages are closed under union, concatenation and Kleene star, because a new start symbol can combine two grammars (or repeat one). Complement is the standard exception.

Step by step.

  1. Union: S → S1 | S2 with S1 and S2.
  2. Concatenation: S → S1S2. Kleene star: S → SS | ε.
  3. Complement: {anbn} is context-free, but its complement intersected with a*b*c* gives {anbncn}, and that intersection is not context-free — the class is not closed under complement.
  4. Since a class closed under complement and union would be closed under intersection, and it is not, complement cannot preserve context-freeness.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Assuming that because regular languages are closed under complement, context-free languages are too. The regular case is special: a DFA can be complemented by swapping final and non-final states.

TOC-U2-Q71NATeasy

The CYK algorithm decides membership in a context-free language by filling an entry t[i, j] for every 1 ≤ i ≤ j ≤ n. For an input string of length 6, how many entries does the table have?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. CYK fills the upper triangle of an n × n table: one entry per substring of the input, and a string of length n has exactly n(n+1)/2 substrings.

Step by step.

  1. Substrings are fixed by a start position i and an end position j with i ≤ j.
  2. For each length L there are n − L + 1 substrings, so the total is ΣL=1..n (n − L + 1) = n(n+1)/2.
  3. With n = 6: 6 × 7 / 2 = 21.

Answer: 21.

Common misconception. Answering 36 by counting the whole 6 × 6 table, or 15 by forgetting the single-symbol entries.

TOC-U2-Q72MSQmedium

Select every statement that is TRUE.

  1. Emptiness of a context-free language is decidable.
  2. Equivalence of two context-free languages is decidable.
  3. The class of context-free languages is closed under homomorphism.
  4. The class of context-free languages is closed under inverse homomorphism.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Closure is generous for homomorphic operations and for union-like operations, and poor for intersection and complement. Decidability is generous for emptiness and membership, and poor for equivalence and ambiguity.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: reduce the grammar, and the language is empty exactly when the start symbol is removed as unreachable or non-generating.
  2. (B) false: equivalence of context-free languages is undecidable; only inclusion into a regular language is decidable.
  3. (C) true: replace every terminal a in the productions by the string h(a); the result is a grammar for the image.
  4. (D) true: build a pushdown automaton that reads h(w) and simulates the original automaton on w, buffering the images in its finite control.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Generalising “emptiness and membership are decidable” to “equivalence is decidable”. The undecidability of equivalence is one of the sharpest differences from the regular case.

TOC-U2-Q73MCQmedium

Let L be a context-free language and R a regular language. Which one of the following is necessarily context-free?

  1. the complement of <span class="mm">L</span>
  2. <span class="mm">L &minus; R</span>
  3. <span class="mm">L &cap; L&prime;</span>, where <span class="mm">L&prime;</span> is context-free
  4. the set of strings that belong to neither <span class="mm">L</span> nor <span class="mm">R</span>
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A context-free language intersected with a regular language is context-free (run the grammar in parallel with the DFA). Intersections and complements among context-free languages are where closure fails.

Step by step.

  1. (A) false: context-free languages are not closed under complement.
  2. (B) true: L − R = L ∩ (Σ* − R), and the complement of a regular language is regular, so this is a context-free language intersected with a regular one.
  3. (C) false: the intersection of two context-free languages need not be context-free.
  4. (D) false: that set is the complement of L ∪ R, and neither complement nor union with a context-free language preserves context-freeness.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Treating “context-free ∩ regular” and “context-free ∩ context-free” as the same. Only the first is guaranteed.

TOC-U2-Q74MCQhard

Let L = { anbn : n ≥ 0 }. Which one of the following is TRUE?

  1. <span class="mm">L</span> is not context-free.
  2. <span class="mm">L</span> is context-free, but its complement is not.
  3. <span class="mm">L</span> is context-free, and so is its complement.
  4. <span class="mm">L</span> is context-free, but <span class="mm">L*</span> is not.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. L is accepted by a deterministic pushdown automaton (push on a, pop on b), and the class of deterministic context-free languages is closed under complement even though the whole class of context-free languages is not.

Step by step.

  1. A DPDA accepts L: push one symbol per a, pop one per b, accept at the end of the input with an empty stack.
  2. Deterministic context-free languages are closed under complement: swap the accepting and rejecting states of the equivalent DPDA with a total transition function.
  3. So the complement of L is deterministic context-free, hence context-free.
  4. (D) is false because L* is just L, which is context-free.
  5. Note that this does not contradict the non-closure of context-free languages under complement: the witness for that failure is a nondeterministic language.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Applying “context-free languages are not closed under complement” to a single deterministic language. The class-level failure does not mean every context-free language has a non-context-free complement.

TOC-U2-Q75NATmedium

Consider these nine operations on languages: union, intersection, complement, concatenation, Kleene star, homomorphism, inverse homomorphism, reversal, and intersection with a regular language. For how many of the nine is the class of context-free languages closed?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Closure of context-free languages holds for the “grammar-building” operations (union, concatenation, star), for homomorphic operations, for reversal, and for intersection with a regular language. It fails for intersection and complement.

Step by step.

  1. Closed: union (new start symbol with two alternatives), concatenation, Kleene star, homomorphism (replace terminals by strings), inverse homomorphism, reversal (reverse every right side), intersection with a regular language (product with a DFA) — 7 operations.
  2. Not closed: intersection (witness {anbncn} = {anbncm} ∩ {ambncn}) and complement (a class closed under complement and union would be closed under intersection).
  3. So 7 of the 9 operations preserve context-freeness.

Answer: 7.

Common misconception. Counting complement or intersection as closed because regular languages are closed under both, or missing the two homomorphism operations, which are easy to overlook.

Review the 50 solved questions from Unit 3 — Pushdown Automata
Deterministic PDA · 14
TOC-U3-Q01MCQeasy

A pushdown automaton is deterministic exactly when which condition holds?

  1. For every state q, stack symbol X and input symbol a, δ(q, a, X) has at most one element, and δ(q, ε, X) is non-empty only if δ(q, a, X) is empty for every input symbol a.
  2. The automaton has exactly one final state and a stack alphabet of exactly two symbols.
  3. Every move consumes exactly one input symbol.
  4. Its stack height never exceeds a fixed constant.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A configuration of a PDA is the triple (state, remaining input, whole stack). Determinism means that from every configuration there is at most one move available.

Step by step.

  1. For a fixed state q, stack top X and input symbol a, the move table δ(q, a, X) must contain at most one element — otherwise the machine could choose between two successors.
  2. An ε-move is a second source of choice: if δ(q, ε, X) is non-empty while δ(q, a, X) is also non-empty, the machine may either consume a or move without consuming input, and two moves exist from one configuration.
  3. The two restrictions together are exactly the definition: one successor per (q, a, X), and ε permitted on (q, X) only where no input-consuming move exists.
  4. Option (B) constrains the shape of the machine, not its behaviour. Option (C) forbids ε-moves, which a DPDA is allowed to have. Option (D) bounds the stack, which has nothing to do with determinism.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Believing that a deterministic PDA may never use ε-moves. It may — the prohibition is on having an ε-move and an input move from the same configuration.

Takeaway. Determinism is a statement about the number of successors of a configuration, not about the number of states, the size of the stack alphabet or the use of ε.

TOC-U3-Q02MCQeasy

Which one of the following languages is accepted by a deterministic pushdown automaton?

  1. { w wR : w ∈ {a, b}* }
  2. { w c wR : w ∈ {a, b}* }
  3. { an bn cn : n ≥ 0 }
  4. { w w : w ∈ {a, b}* }
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A DPDA reads its input strictly left to right and cannot look ahead, so it can only handle a language if the point where it must start popping the stack is marked by something it can actually see.

Step by step.

  1. For {w c wR} the marker c tells the machine exactly where the first half ends: push every symbol before c, then pop one symbol per input symbol after it. No guess is needed, so a DPDA accepts it.
  2. For {w wR} nothing marks the midpoint. A DPDA would have to decide nondeterministically when to switch from pushing to popping, and the pumping argument for DPDAs rules the language out.
  3. For {w w} the second half is not the reverse of the first, so a single stack cannot compare them at all — this language is not even context-free.
  4. For {an bn cn} two independent counts must be matched simultaneously; one stack cannot do it, and the language is not context-free.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Assuming a DPDA can 'guess' the middle of a string. Guessing is exactly what determinism forbids; the visible marker c is what makes option (B) deterministic and option (A) not.

Takeaway. A deterministic PDA needs a visible cue before it may change direction.

TOC-U3-Q03NATeasy

How many of the following six languages are deterministic context-free?
L1 = { an bn : n ≥ 0 }
L2 = { an bm : n ≠ m, n, m ≥ 0 }
L3 = { w wR : w ∈ {a, b}* }
L4 = { w c wR : w ∈ {a, b}* }
L5 = { an bn cn : n ≥ 0 }
L6 = { ai bj ck : i = j or j = k }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A language is a DCFL when some deterministic PDA accepts it. Two facts do most of the work here: the class of DCFLs is closed under complement, and it is closed under intersection with a regular language.

Step by step.

  1. L1 — deterministic. Push one symbol per a, pop one per b, accept when the input ends exactly as the stack empties. No choice is ever made.
  2. L2 — deterministic. Inside a* b*, L2 is the complement of L1. DCFLs are closed under complement, and intersecting with the regular set a* b* preserves determinism, so L2 is a DCFL.
  3. L3 — not deterministic. Nothing marks the midpoint of w wR, so the machine must guess when to start popping.
  4. L4 — deterministic. The visible c marks the midpoint.
  5. L5 — not even context-free, so certainly not deterministic: one stack cannot match two independent counts.
  6. L6 — not deterministic. It is a context-free language (a union of two context-free languages), but a DPDA would have to commit to 'match i with j' or to 'match j with k' before it has seen the c-block. This is the standard example of a CFL that is not a DCFL.

Count. Deterministic: L1, L2, L4 = 3.

Answer: 3.

Common misconception. Two opposite errors. Counting L3 as deterministic by confusing it with L4; and assuming that any '≠' condition destroys determinism, when L2 shows it can survive through complement.

Takeaway. Inequality between two counts is often still deterministic; equality of two counts is too; simultaneous equality of three counts, or an unmarked midpoint, is not.

TOC-U3-Q04MCQmedium

In a deterministic PDA, suppose δ(q, ε, X) is non-empty for some state q and stack symbol X. Which statement must be TRUE?

  1. The automaton must reach a final state by that ε-move.
  2. δ(q, a, X) may be non-empty provided it leads to the same state as the ε-move.
  3. δ(q, a, X) must be empty for every input symbol a.
  4. The ε-move may be taken only after the whole input has been consumed.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The determinism condition is imposed per configuration. Two moves are available from the configuration (q, w, Xγ) if one is an ε-move on X and the other consumes the next input symbol on the same X.

Step by step.

  1. Fix (q, X) and suppose δ(q, ε, X) is non-empty.
  2. If some δ(q, a, X) were also non-empty, then from the configuration (q, aw, Xγ) the machine could either consume a or move on ε — two successors.
  3. Determinism therefore forces δ(q, a, X) = ∅ for every input symbol a.
  4. Where the destination is irrelevant: the restriction is on the source configuration, not on where each move leads.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Option (B) looks like a harmless relaxation — 'both moves lead to the same place, so it is still deterministic'. It is not: the choice itself is the nondeterminism, and it may consume different amounts of input, so the two runs diverge later.

Takeaway. For a given (q, X), an ε-move and an input move may never coexist.

TOC-U3-Q05MSQmedium

Select every statement about the class of deterministic context-free languages (DCFLs) that is TRUE.

  1. The class of DCFLs is closed under complement.
  2. The class of DCFLs is closed under union.
  3. Every DCFL is generated by some unambiguous context-free grammar.
  4. The class of DCFLs is closed under intersection with a regular language.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The DCFLs form a proper subclass of the CFLs, and their closure properties are much weaker than those of the CFLs. The only Boolean operation that survives is complement; intersection and union with a regular language are also safe.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true. Given a DPDA, add a rejecting sink and swap the roles of final and non-final states; the result is still deterministic because every computation of a DPDA is a single path. Hence the complement of a DCFL is a DCFL.
  2. (B) false. Both {anbn : n ≥ 1} and {anb2n : n ≥ 1} are DCFLs, yet their union is a CFL that is not a DCFL. A DPDA must decide, while still reading the a-block, whether to match one b per a or two, and it has no way to know.
  3. (C) true. The computation of a DPDA on a fixed string is unique, so a DPDA yields at most one parse per string; converting the DPDA to a grammar therefore produces an unambiguous grammar.
  4. (D) true. Run the DPDA in parallel with the DFA of the regular language (product construction). The stack is still driven by the DPDA alone, so the product is deterministic.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Carrying over the CFL closure properties wholesale. The CFLs are closed under union, but that proof uses nondeterministic guessing between the two machines, which determinism removes.

Takeaway. DCFL: closed under complement, intersection and union with regular languages; not closed under union or intersection in general.

TOC-U3-Q06MCQhard

Which one of the following languages is not a deterministic context-free language?

  1. { an bm : n ≠ m, n, m ≥ 0 }
  2. { an bn : n ≥ 0 } ∪ { an b2n : n ≥ 0 }
  3. { an bn c : n ≥ 0 }
  4. { w c wR : w ∈ {a, b}* }
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The union of two DCFLs need not be a DCFL. The standard witness is the union of the 'one b per a' language with the 'two b's per a' language.

Step by step.

  1. Both {anbn} and {anb2n} are DCFLs: push one symbol per a and then pop one (respectively two) per b.
  2. Their union is context-free — join the two machines with a new start state and ε-moves — so it is a CFL.
  3. It is not a DCFL. A DPDA must commit, at the end of the a-block, to a matching rule: one b per a, or two. Both families of strings begin with the same an prefix, and the stack alone cannot encode 'either n or 2n', so whichever rule is chosen, the other family is rejected.
  4. By contrast, {anbm : n ≠ m} is a DCFL (it is the complement of {anbn} inside the regular set a*b*), and {anbnc} and {w c wR} are deterministic because the trailing c marks the switch point.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Assuming that because each component is deterministic, the union must be too. Union is exactly the operation that reintroduces a choice.

Takeaway. Determinism is fragile under union; a language can be context-free, and even a union of two DCFLs, and still fail to be a DCFL.

TOC-U3-Q07MSQhard

Let M be the PDA with states {q0, q1, q2}, stack alphabet {Z0, A}, start state q0, final state q2, initial stack Z0, and moves
δ(q0, a, Z0) = {(q0, A Z0)}
δ(q0, a, A) = {(q0, A A)}
δ(q0, b, A) = {(q1, ε)}
δ(q1, b, A) = {(q1, ε)}
δ(q1, ε, Z0) = {(q2, Z0)}
Select every statement that is TRUE.

  1. L(M) = { an bn : n ≥ 1 }.
  2. M is deterministic.
  3. M accepts its language by empty stack.
  4. On the input aab, the computation of M ends in a non-final configuration.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Read the move table as a program: q0 pushes one A per a, q1 pops one A per b, and the only ε-move hands control from q1 to the final state once the stack is down to Z0.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true. On anbn the stack after the a-block is AnZ0; the n symbols b pop all n copies of A, and the ε-move then reaches q2. Trace ab: (q0, AZ0) → (q1, Z0) → (q2, Z0), accepted.
  2. (B) true. Every entry of the table is either empty or a singleton, and the only ε-move is on (q1, Z0), where δ(q1, b, Z0) is empty. So no configuration has two successors.
  3. (C) false. Acceptance happens in state q2, and the ε-move writes Z0 back, so the stack still holds Z0 when the machine accepts. It accepts by final state.
  4. (D) true. Trace aab: after two a's the stack is AAZ0 in q0; the single b pops one A and moves to (q1, AZ0). The input is exhausted, q1 is not final, and the ε-move does not apply because the top symbol is A, not Z0. The run stops in a non-final configuration, so aab is rejected.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Reading the final state as 'stack emptied'. Acceptance by final state and acceptance by empty stack are different conditions; here the bottom marker is deliberately restored, which is exactly what makes the machine deterministic and lets it stop at the right moment.

Takeaway. To check determinism, look for any (q, X) that has both an ε-move and an input move — not at the number of moves in the whole table.

TOC-U3B-Q01MCQeasy

In the formal definition of a deterministic pushdown automaton, the transition function δ has which of the following types?

  1. δ : Q × Σ × Γ → Q × Γ*
  2. δ : Q × (Σ ∪ {ε}) × Γ → 2Q × Γ*
  3. δ : Q × (Σ ∪ {ε}) × Γ → (Q × Γ*) ∪ {∅}
  4. δ : Q × Σ* × Γ* → Q
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A deterministic pushdown automaton is defined by a partial function: from each argument (q, a, X) there is at most one successor, and possibly none. The set-valued transition function is what makes a machine nondeterministic.

Step by step.

  1. The three arguments of δ are the current state, the next input symbol (or ε for an input-free move) and the symbol currently on top of the stack, so the domain must be Q × (Σ ∪ {ε}) × Γ.
  2. The value must be at most one pair (next state, string pushed in place of the stack top). A value of ∅ means "no move is possible", which is exactly what a partial function expresses.
  3. Option (A) drops ε from the domain, so it forbids input-free moves; a deterministic PDA is allowed to have them.
  4. Option (B) is the transition function of a nondeterministic PDA: a set of successors per argument.
  5. Option (D) has the wrong domain and returns a state with no stack string, so it cannot describe how the stack changes.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Many candidates think a deterministic machine must be total, so they reject the ∪ {∅} in the codomain. Determinism limits the number of successors; it never requires one to exist.

Takeaway. Deterministic = at most one successor per (q, a, X), with ε allowed as an input symbol. Set-valued δ is the hallmark of nondeterminism.

TOC-U3B-Q02MCQeasy

Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. Every deterministic context-free language is regular.
  2. Every context-free language is accepted by some deterministic pushdown automaton.
  3. The class of deterministic context-free languages is closed under union.
  4. Every regular language is accepted by some deterministic pushdown automaton.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The four classes nest as regular ⊂ deterministic context-free ⊂ context-free. A DFA is a degenerate DPDA that never writes to its stack, which gives the first inclusion.

Step by step.

  1. Take any DFA and add a stack that is never read or written. Every move of the DFA becomes a move of the PDA that leaves the stack untouched, and at most one move is available from each configuration. The result is a DPDA accepting the same language, so (D) is true.
  2. (A) is false: { anbn : n ≥ 0 } is accepted by a DPDA but is not regular.
  3. (B) is false: { w wR : w ∈ {a, b}* } is context-free but has no deterministic PDA, because nothing marks the midpoint.
  4. (C) is false: {anbn} and {anb2n} are both deterministic context-free, yet their union is not, since a DPDA cannot decide how many b's to match per a.

Answer: Option (D).

Common misconception. Reading "deterministic context-free" as "almost regular". The stack still gives full counting power; determinism restricts the number of choices, not the memory.

Takeaway. Regular ⊂ DCFL ⊂ CFL, and both inclusions are strict.

TOC-U3B-Q03NATeasy

A deterministic pushdown automaton has 3 states, input alphabet Σ = {a, b} and stack alphabet Γ = {Z0, A}. Its transition function is a partial function of (state, input symbol or ε, stack top). At most how many distinct arguments can that transition function specify?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The arguments of δ range over states × (input symbols together with ε) × stack symbols, so the number of possible arguments is the product of the three sizes.

Step by step.

  1. States: 3.
  2. Input symbols together with ε: 2 + 1 = 3.
  3. Stack symbols: 2.
  4. Product: 3 × 3 × 2 = 18.
  5. Determinism says each of these 18 arguments carries at most one successor; it does not require any of them to be defined, so 18 is an upper bound that a machine can attain.

Answer: 18

Common misconception. Forgetting ε and answering 3 × 2 × 2 = 12. An input-free move is a legal argument of δ in a deterministic PDA, so it must be counted.

Takeaway. Number of possible δ arguments = |Q| × (|Σ| + 1) × |Γ|.

TOC-U3B-Q04MCQmedium

Let L1 = { anbn : n ≥ 0 } and L2 = { anbnc : n ≥ 0 }, and let L = L1 ∪ L2. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. The DCFLs are not closed under union, so L is not deterministic context-free.
  2. L is context-free but not deterministic context-free.
  3. L is regular.
  4. L is deterministic context-free.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The failure of closure under union is caused by a machine that would have to guess which of two shapes it is reading. Here no guess is needed, because the two shapes differ at a point the automaton can see.

Step by step.

  1. A DPDA reads an, pushing one A per a, then reads bn popping one A per b.
  2. When the stack returns to Z0 the machine has consumed exactly anbn. It now looks at the next symbol, if any.
  3. If the input has ended, the word is in L1 and the machine accepts. If the next symbol is c, it consumes that single c and accepts, which covers L2.
  4. Every step above is forced, so the machine is deterministic and L is a DCFL.
  5. Contrast the classical counterexample: {anbn} ∪ {anb2n}. There the choice "one b per a or two" must be made while the a's are still being read, with no visible difference between the two cases.

Answer: Option (D).

Common misconception. Applying the closure table mechanically: "DCFLs are not closed under union, therefore any union of two DCFLs is not a DCFL". Closure fails for some unions, not for all.

Takeaway. A union is deterministic when the two languages can be told apart by information the machine can see deterministically — here, end of input versus a literal c.

TOC-U3B-Q05MSQmedium

Select every statement about deterministic pushdown automata that is TRUE.

  1. If L is a deterministic context-free language, then so is its complement.
  2. Every deterministic context-free language is accepted by some DPDA by final state.
  3. The class of deterministic context-free languages is closed under intersection.
  4. Every deterministic context-free language is accepted by some DPDA by empty stack.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A DPDA has exactly one computation on each input, so its accepting behaviour can be complemented by swapping final and non-final states. The same uniqueness is what breaks down when the acceptance mode is changed.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true. Every computation of a DPDA is a single path. Add a non-accepting sink state for the case where the machine halts early, then exchange final and non-final states. The result is still deterministic and accepts exactly the complement.
  2. (B) true by definition: the class of DCFLs is the class of languages accepted by deterministic PDAs by final state.
  3. (C) false. L1 = { anbncm } and L2 = { ambncn } are both DCFLs, but their intersection is { anbncn }, which is not even context-free.
  4. (D) false. Empty-stack acceptance forces the prefix property, and L = {a} ∪ {ab} is a DCFL that violates it: it is accepted by a DPDA by final state, but a DPDA that empties its stack after reading a can never read the b of ab.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Assuming the DCFLs inherit the closure properties of the CFLs. The CFL proofs use nondeterministic guessing, which determinism removes.

Takeaway. DCFL: complement yes; union and intersection in general no; intersection and union with a regular language yes.

TOC-U3B-Q06MCQhard

Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. Every deterministic context-free language has an unambiguous context-free grammar, but not every unambiguous context-free language is deterministic context-free.
  2. Every unambiguous context-free language is deterministic context-free.
  3. Every context-free language has an unambiguous context-free grammar.
  4. A language is deterministic context-free if and only if it has an unambiguous context-free grammar.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A DPDA produces at most one parse per string, so determinism implies unambiguity. The converse fails: unambiguity is a property of one grammar, determinism a property of the language.

Step by step.

  1. From a DPDA build a grammar by the triple construction. Each accepting computation of the DPDA gives one derivation, and there is at most one computation per string, so the grammar is unambiguous. The first half of (A) holds.
  2. Now take L = { w wR : w ∈ {a, b}* }. It has the unambiguous grammar S → aSa | bSb | ε: the outermost pair of symbols determines the outer production uniquely, and the argument repeats inside.
  3. L is nevertheless not a DCFL: nothing marks the midpoint, so a DPDA cannot know when to stop pushing and start matching.
  4. So unambiguity does not imply determinism, which kills (B) and (D).
  5. (C) is false: languages such as { aibjck : i = j or j = k } are inherently ambiguous — every grammar for them is ambiguous.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Treating "unambiguous" and "deterministic" as synonyms. Unambiguity says a string has one parse; determinism says the machine never chooses.

Takeaway. DCFL ⊊ unambiguous CFL ⊊ CFL, and all three inclusions are strict.

TOC-U3B-Q07NATmedium

How many of the following five languages are deterministic context-free?
L1 = { anbn : n ≥ 0 }
L2 = { anbncn : n ≥ 0 }
L3 = { w c wR : w ∈ {a, b}* }
L4 = { aibjck : i = j or j = k, i, j, k ≥ 0 }
L5 = { anbm : n ≠ m, n, m ≥ 0 }

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Three separate tests decide the count: can a single stack supply the memory; is a midpoint visible; and is a "not equal" condition expressible as a complement inside a regular set.

Step by step.

  1. L1: push one symbol per a, pop one per b. No choice is ever made, so it is deterministic — count 1.
  2. L2: needs two independent counts to be matched at once. One stack cannot do it, so the language is not context-free, hence not deterministic.
  3. L3: the literal c marks the midpoint, so pushing up to c and matching afterwards is forced. Deterministic — count 2.
  4. L4: context-free, but a DPDA would have to commit to "match the a-block with the b-block" or to "match the b-block with the c-block" before it has seen the c-block. Not deterministic.
  5. L5: inside the regular set a*b* it is the complement of {anbn}. DCFLs are closed under complement and under intersection with a regular language, so it is deterministic — count 3.

Answer: 3

Common misconception. Two opposite errors: calling L5 nondeterministic because of the "≠", and calling L4 deterministic because it "looks like a union of two simple languages".

Takeaway. A "≠" between two counts usually survives determinism through complement; an unmarked midpoint or two competing matching rules do not.

Nondeterministic PDA · 12
TOC-U3-Q08MCQeasy

Which statement about the class of languages accepted by nondeterministic pushdown automata is TRUE?

  1. It is exactly the class of context-free languages.
  2. It is exactly the class of regular languages.
  3. It is exactly the class of deterministic context-free languages.
  4. It is strictly larger than the class of languages generated by context-free grammars.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The NPDA and the context-free grammar are two notations for the same class of languages. Each direction of the equivalence has a standard construction.

Step by step.

  1. CFG → NPDA. A single-state automaton with initial stack Z0S replaces the top nonterminal by the right-hand side of a production, and pops a terminal when it matches the next input symbol. It accepts by empty stack.
  2. NPDA → CFG. Introduce a variable [q, A, p] for every triple of states and stack symbols; the variable generates exactly the strings that take the automaton from q with A on top to p having popped A.
  3. Both constructions preserve the language exactly, so the two classes coincide — they are the context-free languages.
  4. The regular languages and the deterministic CFLs are proper subclasses of this class: {w wR} is context-free but not regular, and not a DCFL either.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Option (D) — believing that a machine with a stack must beat a grammar. They are exactly matched, so neither is stronger.

Takeaway. NPDA ≡ CFG. Any question phrased in one notation can be answered in the other.

TOC-U3-Q09NATeasy

Consider the PDA M with states {q0, q1}, stack alphabet {Z0, A}, start state q0, final state q1, initial stack Z0, and moves
δ(q0, a, Z0) = {(q0, A Z0)}
δ(q0, a, A) = {(q0, A A)}
δ(q0, b, A) = {(q0, ε), (q1, ε)}
δ(q0, ε, Z0) = {(q1, Z0)}
On the input aabb, how many distinct sequences of moves take M from its start configuration to a configuration that has read the whole input and is in the final state?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An NPDA is a tree of computations, not a single path. To count accepting computations, follow every branch and discard only the ones that die.

Step by step.

  1. Read the two a's. Only one branch survives: (q0, Z0) → (q0, AZ0) → (q0, AAZ0). The input left is bb.
  2. The first b has two choices, so the tree splits:
    branch 1 — (q0, AZ0) with input b remaining;
    branch 2 — (q1, AZ0) with input b remaining.
  3. Branch 2 dies. δ(q1, b, A) is not defined, and the ε-move of q1 needs Z0 on top, which it is not.
  4. Branch 1 splits again on the second b: to (q0, Z0) or to (q1, Z0). Both have consumed all four symbols.
  5. (q1, Z0) is final, so this is an accepting computation. (q0, Z0) is not final, but the ε-move carries it to (q1, Z0) with no input consumed — a second accepting computation.
  6. Total: 1 + 0 + 1 = 2 accepting computations.

Answer: 2.

Common misconception. Counting the number of accepting states reached rather than the number of distinct move sequences. Two different sequences may end in the same configuration, and each is counted separately.

Takeaway. Count in an NPDA by drawing the computation tree and pruning dead branches — never by following one path and assuming the rest behave the same.

TOC-U3-Q10MCQeasy

The PDA M has states {q0, q1}, stack alphabet {Z0, A}, start state q0, no final states, initial stack Z0, and accepts by empty stack, with moves
δ(q0, a, Z0) = {(q0, A Z0)}
δ(q0, a, A) = {(q0, A A)}
δ(q0, b, A) = {(q1, ε)}
δ(q1, b, A) = {(q1, ε)}
δ(q1, ε, Z0) = {(q1, ε)}
Which language does M accept?

  1. { w ∈ {a, b}* : #a(w) = #b(w) }
  2. { an bm : n, m ≥ 0 }
  3. { an bn : n ≥ 0 }
  4. { an bn : n ≥ 1 }
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Read the move table as a program: q0 is the phase in which a's are pushed, q1 is the phase in which b's are popped, and acceptance by empty stack means the input is finished and nothing but the marker is left, which the ε-move then removes.

Step by step.

  1. Each a pushes exactly one A, and only q0 can read an a. So all a's must come first.
  2. The first b switches to q1 and pops one A; every later b also pops one. So the number of b's must equal the number of a's.
  3. After the last b the stack is Z0 alone, and the ε-move empties it: accepted. Trace ab: (q0, AZ0) → (q1, Z0) → (q1, ε).
  4. Order matters: on abab the third symbol is an a while the machine is in q1, where no a-move exists, so the branch dies and the string is rejected.
  5. The empty string is rejected: the ε-move is defined only on (q1, Z0), and with no input read the machine is still in q0.
  6. Hence L(M) = {anbn : n ≥ 1}.

Answer: Option (D) — { anbn : n ≥ 1 }.

Common misconception. Option (A) ignores the order requirement: the phase states force all a's before all b's, so equal counts are not enough. Option (C) forgets that ε never reaches q1, so n = 0 is not covered.

Takeaway. With acceptance by empty stack, always check separately whether the empty string can empty the stack — the ε-move is keyed on a state and a stack symbol, and both must be reachable.

TOC-U3-Q11MSQmedium

Select every statement about nondeterministic pushdown automata (NPDAs) that is TRUE.

  1. Every context-free language is accepted by some NPDA.
  2. An NPDA may have several moves available from the same configuration.
  3. For every NPDA there is a context-free grammar generating the same language.
  4. An NPDA cannot accept the empty string.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. NPDAs are the machine model of the context-free languages. Nondeterminism is what gives them their power, and it is also what makes the two acceptance modes interchangeable.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true. Take a grammar in Greibach Normal Form and build the single-state NPDA that replaces the top nonterminal by a right-hand side and pops matching terminals; it accepts by empty stack.
  2. (B) true. That is the definition of nondeterminism: δ(q, a, X) may contain two or more elements, and an ε-move may coexist with an input move.
  3. (C) true. The triple construction [q, A, p] produces a grammar for the language of any PDA.
  4. (D) false. The empty string is accepted whenever the start configuration is already accepting — for instance a grammar with S → ε gives an NPDA that accepts ε by empty stack.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Common misconception. Thinking that 'the stack must be emptied by reading input' rules out ε. ε-moves can empty the stack without consuming anything.

Takeaway. NPDA = CFG, nondeterministic by design, and perfectly able to accept ε.

TOC-U3-Q12MCQmedium

A grammar G = (V, T, P, S) is converted into an NPDA by the standard top-down construction: one state q, initial stack Z0S, acceptance by empty stack. Which move is added for the production A → X1 X2 … Xk?

  1. δ(q, X1, A) contains (q, X2 … Xk)
  2. δ(q, A, ε) contains (q, X1 … Xk)
  3. δ(q, ε, X1) contains (q, A)
  4. δ(q, ε, A) contains (q, X1 X2 … Xk)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The construction simulates a leftmost derivation on the stack. The stack holds the unexpanded sentential form with the leftmost symbol on top, so expanding a nonterminal means replacing the top symbol by the right-hand side, in order.

Step by step.

  1. Expansion consumes no input, so the move must be on ε.
  2. The symbol being expanded is the stack top, so the move is keyed on A, giving δ(q, ε, A).
  3. The replacement is the whole right-hand side X1…Xk, written so that X1 becomes the new top.
  4. The other half of the construction is a move δ(q, a, a) = {(q, ε)} for each terminal a: when the top is a terminal that matches the input, both are consumed.
  5. Option (A) would consume X1 as input even when it is a nonterminal; option (B) has the arguments of δ in the wrong order; option (C) expands backwards.

Answer: Option (D).

Common misconception. Swapping the arguments: δ takes (state, input symbol or ε, stack top). The stack top is the last argument, and it is the nonterminal being expanded.

Takeaway. Nonterminal on top → ε-move that expands it; terminal on top → consume the matching input symbol.

TOC-U3-Q13MCQhard

Which one of the following languages is accepted by some nondeterministic PDA but by no deterministic PDA?

  1. { ai bj ck : i = j or j = k, i, j, k ≥ 0 }
  2. { an bn : n ≥ 0 }
  3. { w c wR : w ∈ {a, b}* }
  4. { an bm : n ≠ m, n, m ≥ 0 }
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An NPDA can guess; a DPDA cannot. The languages that separate the two classes are exactly the ones where the machine must commit to one of several matching rules before it has enough information.

Step by step.

  1. Option (A) is context-free: it is the union of {aibick} and {aibjcj}, each of which is context-free, and the CFLs are closed under union. An NPDA guesses at the start which of the two branches to follow.
  2. Option (A) is not a DCFL. A DPDA reading the a-block must decide whether it will compare the a-count with the b-count or the b-count with the c-count, and that decision cannot be made until the whole string has been seen. Whichever rule it commits to, the strings that satisfy only the other rule are rejected. This is the standard example of a CFL that is not deterministic.
  3. Options (B) and (C) are DCFLs — push the a-block and pop on b's; or push up to the visible c and pop after it.
  4. Option (D) is a DCFL too: it is the complement of {anbn} inside the regular set a*b*, and DCFLs are closed under complement and under intersection with a regular language.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Reading 'or' as automatically nondeterministic and '≠' as automatically nondeterministic. It is the other way round here: the union in (A) is the nondeterministic one, while the inequality in (D) is deterministic.

Takeaway. Two independent matching rules, chosen before the information arrives, is the signature of a CFL that is not deterministic.

TOC-U3B-Q08MCQeasy

A nondeterministic pushdown automaton accepts a string w when:

  1. every computation on w ends in an accepting configuration.
  2. at least one computation on w reads all of w and ends in an accepting configuration.
  3. at least one computation on w enters an accepting configuration, whether or not the input has been fully read.
  4. its computation tree on w contains no infinite branch.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Acceptance by a nondeterministic machine is existential: one successful branch is enough. But "successful" still means the whole input has been consumed and the accepting condition is met at that moment.

Step by step.

  1. The computation of an NPDA on a fixed input is a tree, whose branches are the different choices of moves.
  2. Acceptance is defined by the existence of one branch that reads the entire input and halts in a final state — option (B).
  3. (A) describes the universal condition, which no nondeterministic machine uses.
  4. (C) drops the requirement that the input be finished, so it would accept every string having an accepted prefix.
  5. (D) is irrelevant: a branch may loop forever without affecting acceptance, since only one finite accepting branch is needed.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Believing that an NPDA must explore every branch successfully, or that an infinite branch spoils acceptance. Neither is true: acceptance is one good branch, and infinite branches are simply ignored.

Takeaway. NPDA acceptance = ∃ a computation that consumes the whole input and accepts at the end.

TOC-U3B-Q09NATeasy

A pushdown automaton has 2 states and stack alphabet Γ = {Z0, A}. A configuration is the current state together with the whole stack content, and the stack always holds at least one symbol. How many distinct configurations does the automaton have in which the stack is exactly three symbols long?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A configuration is a pair (state, stack content). The number of configurations of a fixed stack height is the number of states times the number of stack contents of that height.

Step by step.

  1. A stack content of length 3 is a string of three symbols drawn from {Z0, A}, so there are 23 = 8 contents.
  2. Each content can occur in either of the 2 states.
  3. Total: 2 × 8 = 16.

Answer: 16

Common misconception. Multiplying by the input as well, or forgetting the state. A configuration counts only the state and the stack; the remaining input is a third component that this question does not ask about.

Takeaway. Configurations at stack height h = |Q| × |Γ|h.

TOC-U3B-Q10MCQmedium

A grammar G = (V, T, P, S) is turned into an NPDA by the standard top-down construction: one state q, initial stack Z0S, acceptance by empty stack. The accepting computations of that NPDA on a string w correspond exactly to:

  1. the leftmost derivations of w in G.
  2. the rightmost derivations of w in G.
  3. the derivations of w in G, counted once for every possible order in which the nonterminals are expanded.
  4. the strings of length |w| that G can generate.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The stack always shows the leftmost remaining nonterminal on top, so the machine can only ever expand that one. A run of the machine is therefore a leftmost derivation, and conversely.

Step by step.

  1. Initially the stack holds S, which is the leftmost remaining nonterminal.
  2. A production move replaces the top nonterminal by its right-hand side, written so that the leftmost symbol ends on top. The next nonterminal to be expanded is again the leftmost one.
  3. A terminal on top is popped only when it matches the next input symbol, which forces the derivation to proceed in the order the string is read.
  4. So each accepting computation is exactly one leftmost derivation, and each leftmost derivation gives exactly one accepting computation.
  5. (B) and (C) are wrong because other expansion orders are never simulated; (D) confuses the set of generated strings with the derivations of one fixed string.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Thinking the NPDA enumerates all derivations. It simulates only leftmost ones, which is why the number of accepting computations equals the number of parse trees — each tree has exactly one leftmost derivation.

Takeaway. Accepting computations of the single-state NPDA ↔ leftmost derivations of w.

TOC-U3B-Q11MSQmedium

Select every statement about the class of languages accepted by nondeterministic pushdown automata that is TRUE.

  1. It is closed under union.
  2. It is closed under intersection.
  3. It is closed under complement.
  4. It is closed under concatenation.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. This is exactly the class of context-free languages, and the CFL closure table is highly asymmetric: all the "combine two machines" operations work, the "restrict" operations do not.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: run the two NPDAs from a new start state, guessing which one to follow.
  2. (B) false: {anbncm} ∩ {ambncn} = {anbncn}, which is not context-free.
  3. (C) false: if the CFLs were closed under complement, then by closure under union and the identity L1 ∩ L2 = ¬(¬L1 ∪ ¬L2) they would also be closed under intersection, contradicting (B).
  4. (D) true: connect the final states of the first machine to the start of the second with ε-moves.

Answer: Options (A) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming the Boolean algebra of regular languages carries over. It does not: the CFLs are closed under union but under neither intersection nor complement.

Takeaway. CFL: closed under union, concatenation, Kleene star, reversal, homomorphism; not under intersection or complement.

TOC-U3B-Q12MCQhard

Let L = { w ∈ {a, b}* : w = wR }, the set of palindromes over {a, b}. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. L is regular.
  2. L is deterministic context-free but not regular.
  3. L is context-free but not deterministic context-free.
  4. L is not context-free.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Palindromes are the textbook example of a language that a single stack can handle only nondeterministically: the machine must guess where the middle of the word is.

Step by step.

  1. L is generated by S → aSa | bSb | ε | a | b, so it is context-free.
