  2. A deterministic PDA has to decide, at each position, whether it is still in the first half or already in the second half. With no marker in the string, both readings are consistent with the input it has seen so far.
  3. Any deterministic strategy therefore fails on some word: choose a palindrome whose middle is not where the machine decided to switch, and it either pops too early or too late.
  4. So L is not a DCFL, which rules out (B); it is not regular because a DFA cannot store the first half, which rules out (A); (D) is false by the grammar above.
  5. Compare { w c wR }: the literal c is a visible marker, and that language is a DCFL.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Confusing palindromes with marked palindromes. {w c wR} is deterministic; the unmarked version is not.

Takeaway. No marker at the midpoint ⟹ the machine must guess ⟹ nondeterminism is required.

TOC-U3B-Q13NATmedium

Consider the NPDA M with states {q0, q1}, stack alphabet {Z0, A}, start state q0, final state q1, initial stack Z0, and moves
δ(q0, a, Z0) = {(q0, A Z0)}
δ(q0, a, A) = {(q0, A A)}
δ(q0, b, A) = {(q0, ε), (q1, ε)}
δ(q1, b, A) = {(q1, ε)}
δ(q0, ε, Z0) = {(q1, Z0)}
How many distinct sequences of moves take M from its start configuration to a configuration that has read the whole of aabb and is in the final state?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Counting accepting computations means walking the computation tree and keeping only the branches that consume the entire input and finish in the final state.

Step by step.

  1. Start: (q0, aabb, Z0). The first a must use δ(q0, a, Z0), giving (q0, abb, A Z0).
  2. The second a must use δ(q0, a, A), giving (q0, bb, A A Z0). No choice yet.
  3. The first b has two choices. Branch 1: (q0, ε) gives (q0, b, A Z0). Branch 2: (q1, ε) gives (q1, b, A Z0).
  4. Branch 1 again has two choices on the last b: staying in q0 gives (q0, ε, Z0), and switching gives (q1, ε, Z0), which is accepting at once.
  5. Branch 2 has only one choice, δ(q1, b, A), giving (q1, ε, Z0), which is accepting.
  6. The leftover branch (q0, ε, Z0) can still take δ(q0, ε, Z0) to (q1, ε, Z0), which is accepting too.
  7. The three accepting computations are therefore: switch on the first b; switch on the second b; and stay in q0 throughout, using the ε-move at the end.

Answer: 3

Common misconception. Stopping at two branches. The ε-move out of (q0, Z0) creates a third accepting route, and it is easy to overlook because no input symbol is consumed.

Takeaway. Count along the whole computation tree, and remember that ε-moves branch too.

Acceptance: empty store vs final state · 12
TOC-U3-Q14MCQeasy

A pushdown automaton is said to accept a string by empty stack when:

  1. it reaches a final state at some point while reading the string.
  2. the stack is empty at some point during the computation.
  3. after reading the whole string the stack is empty, whatever the state.
  4. it reaches a final state after reading the whole string and the stack is also empty.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The two acceptance modes look at different things. Acceptance by final state looks at the state after the input is consumed; acceptance by empty stack looks at the stack after the input is consumed and ignores the state entirely.

Step by step.

  1. A computation must consume the whole input before it can accept — that rules out option (A).
  2. In empty-stack acceptance the set of final states is irrelevant, so option (D) imposes an extra condition that is not required.
  3. Option (B) drops the requirement that the input be exhausted: a machine may empty its stack halfway and then keep reading.
  4. The correct condition is: the whole input is consumed and the stack is empty, with no condition on the state.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Treating 'empty stack' as an event that can happen mid-string. Acceptance always requires the input to be finished first.

Takeaway. Final-state acceptance ignores the stack; empty-stack acceptance ignores the state. Both require the input to be exhausted.

TOC-U3-Q15NATmedium

A pushdown automaton has 2 states and stack alphabet {Z0, A}. A configuration is the current state together with the entire contents of the stack. How many distinct configurations does the automaton have in which the stack holds exactly one or two symbols?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A configuration of a PDA is (state, remaining input, stack contents). Counting configurations of bounded stack height is a counting problem on the stack alphabet, multiplied by the number of states.

Step by step.

  1. Stacks of length 1: any single symbol of the stack alphabet, so 2 possibilities (Z0 and A).
  2. Stacks of length 2: two independent choices, so 22 = 4 possibilities (Z0Z0, Z0A, AZ0, AA).
  3. Stack contents altogether: 2 + 4 = 6.
  4. Multiply by the 2 states: 2 × 6 = 12 configurations.

Answer: 12.

Common misconception. Counting only stacks that respect the bottom marker, i.e. requiring Z0 to be at the bottom. That is a restriction on legal computations, not on configurations, and it would give 4 stacks instead of 6.

Takeaway. Configurations are state × stack, and the number of stacks of length L over a k-symbol alphabet is kL.

TOC-U3-Q16MCQeasy

For nondeterministic pushdown automata, which statement about the two acceptance modes is TRUE?

  1. Empty-stack acceptance is strictly weaker than final-state acceptance.
  2. A language is accepted by some NPDA by final state if and only if it is accepted by some NPDA by empty stack.
  3. Final-state acceptance is strictly weaker than empty-stack acceptance.
  4. Neither mode can accept the empty string.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. For NPDAs the two acceptance modes are interchangeable: each can simulate the other, so they define the same class of languages, the context-free languages.

Step by step.

  1. Final state → empty stack. Add a new bottom marker below the old one and a new single final state; from every old final state add ε-moves that empty the stack. The new machine accepts by empty stack exactly when the old one reached a final state.
  2. Empty stack → final state. Add a new bottom marker and a new single final state; whenever the old stack would empty, move on ε to the new final state instead. Acceptance by empty stack becomes acceptance by final state.
  3. Both simulations are language-preserving, so neither mode is weaker.
  4. Option (D) is false: ε is accepted whenever the start configuration already satisfies the acceptance condition.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Importing the deterministic picture, where the two modes really are not interchangeable. The equivalence is a property of nondeterministic machines.

Takeaway. NPDA: the two modes are equivalent. DPDA: they are not, and that asymmetry is a favourite exam point.

TOC-U3-Q17MSQmedium

Select every statement about the two acceptance modes of pushdown automata that is TRUE.

  1. Every NPDA that accepts by final state can be converted into an NPDA that accepts the same language by empty stack.
  2. Every DPDA that accepts by final state can be converted into a DPDA that accepts the same language by empty stack.
  3. In a PDA that accepts by empty stack, acceptance depends only on the stack and not on the state.
  4. If a DPDA accepts a language by empty stack, then no proper prefix of a word of the language belongs to the language.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The conversion between acceptance modes is free for NPDAs and is not free for DPDAs, because the conversion has to empty the stack at the right instant and a deterministic machine cannot postpone or duplicate that decision.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true. Add a fresh bottom marker and a fresh single final state, and from each old final state add ε-moves that pop the stack away. Nothing here needs a choice, so the construction works for NPDAs.
  2. (B) false. A DPDA may reach a final state while input still remains. Emptying the stack at that moment would also accept every longer string with that prefix, which changes the language. Since the machine is deterministic it cannot keep the stack and empty it 'later instead'. The classic witness is L = {anbn} ∪ {anbnc}.
  3. (C) true. That is the definition: the final-state set plays no part.
  4. (D) true. Suppose x ∈ L and x is a proper prefix of y ∈ L. After reading x the stack is empty, and with an empty stack no further move is possible, so y could never be accepted. This is the prefix property.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming the NPDA equivalence carries over to DPDAs. It does not: only DCFLs with the prefix property can be accepted by a DPDA by empty stack.

Takeaway. Empty-stack acceptance forces the prefix property; final-state acceptance does not. That is the whole difference.

TOC-U3-Q18MCQmedium

Why can a DPDA that accepts by final state not always be turned into a DPDA that accepts the same language by empty stack?

  1. Because a DPDA has no final states to remove.
  2. Because determinism forbids ε-moves, which such a conversion needs.
  3. Because the empty stack cannot be represented in a DPDA.
  4. Because the machine may reach a final state while input remains, so emptying the stack there would also accept every longer string having that prefix.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Empty-stack acceptance is a strictly stronger requirement: the stack must empty at the exact instant the last input symbol is read. A deterministic machine cannot arrange that if it was designed to stop at an earlier moment.

Step by step.

  1. Take a DPDA M accepting by final state and a word x ∈ L(M). In many DPDAs the machine enters a final state with input still unread — nothing forbids that.
  2. The natural conversion adds ε-moves that empty the stack on entering a final state. Applied at that moment, the stack empties before the input is finished.
  3. With an empty stack no move is possible, so the machine stops and the rest of the input is never read. Under the convention that acceptance requires the whole input to be consumed, it rejects; but under the naive reading it would accept every extension xy as well.
  4. Because the machine is deterministic it cannot both keep the stack and empty it 'later', so there is no way to delay the emptying until the input ends. Hence the conversion can fail.
  5. Options (A), (B) and (C) are all false: DPDAs do have final states (which the conversion simply stops using), DPDAs may use ε-moves, and the empty stack is of course representable — it is the acceptance condition itself.

Answer: Option (D).

Common misconception. Believing the conversion is always safe because it is safe for NPDAs. Nondeterminism lets the NPDA keep both options open; a DPDA must commit.

Takeaway. A DPDA accepting by empty stack accepts a language with the prefix property. Languages without it can be accepted by final state only.

TOC-U3-Q19MCQhard

Let L be a language accepted by some DPDA by empty stack. Which property must L necessarily have?

  1. No word of L is a proper prefix of another word of L.
  2. L is regular.
  3. L is closed under reversal.
  4. L is finite.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Once a stack is empty there is no symbol left to consult, so no further move is possible. That single observation forces the prefix property.

Step by step.

  1. Suppose x, y ∈ L with x a proper prefix of y, so y = xz for some non-empty z.
  2. The accepting computation on y must, after reading x, still have consumed only the prefix x. Since the machine is deterministic it is in the same configuration as it would be after reading x alone — and that configuration accepts x, so the stack is empty.
  3. With an empty stack, δ has no stack top to read, so no move exists and the remaining input z can never be consumed. Contradiction.
  4. So no word of L can be a proper prefix of another — the prefix property.
  5. The other options are far too strong: {anbn : n ≥ 1} is accepted by a DPDA by empty stack, is not regular, is not closed under reversal ({bnan} is not even deterministic in the required sense) and is infinite.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Confusing the prefix property with 'prefix-closed'. The prefix property says the language contains no word together with a proper prefix of it — the exact opposite of prefix-closed.

Takeaway. Empty-stack acceptance ⇒ prefix property. Conversely, a language with the prefix property that is accepted by a DPDA by final state can be accepted by empty stack as well.

TOC-U3B-Q14MCQeasy

A PDA that accepts by final state reaches a final state after reading a proper prefix of w, and then goes on reading the rest of w. Does it accept w?

  1. Yes, because a final state was reached during the computation.
  2. No, because acceptance requires that the whole input has been read at the moment the final state is entered.
  3. Yes, provided the stack is also empty at that moment.
  4. No, unless the automaton has no ε-moves.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Acceptance by final state is a statement about the end of the computation: the input must be exhausted and the state final. Passing through a final state earlier counts for nothing.

Step by step.

  1. The definition reads: w is accepted when some computation starting from the start configuration consumes all of w and halts in a final state.
  2. A final state visited in the middle of the input is only an intermediate configuration, so it is not acceptance — option (B).
  3. (A) and (C) both treat the intermediate visit as acceptance, and (C) additionally mixes in the other acceptance mode.
  4. (D) invents a restriction on ε-moves that plays no part here.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Forgetting the "all input consumed" clause, which is exactly the clause that makes the empty-stack and final-state modes differ in power.

Takeaway. Final-state acceptance = input exhausted ∧ state final, at the same instant.

TOC-U3B-Q15MCQeasy

Which one of the following statements about acceptance by empty stack is TRUE?

  1. The stack must become empty at some point during the computation.
  2. The automaton must have no final states.
  3. The state in which the computation ends plays no part in the decision.
  4. The stack must be empty before the whole input has been read.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Acceptance by empty stack is decided purely by the stack, evaluated after the whole input has been read. The final state is irrelevant to it.

Step by step.

  1. The definition reads: w is accepted when some computation consumes all of w and ends with an empty stack, whatever state it is in.
  2. So the ending state does not matter — option (C).
  3. (A) and (D) both move the emptiness to the wrong time: the stack must be empty at the end, not "at some point" and certainly not before the input is finished.
  4. (B) is false. A machine may perfectly well have final states; they are simply ignored when acceptance is by empty stack.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Thinking a final state must also be reached, i.e. combining both acceptance conditions. The two modes are alternatives, not a conjunction.

Takeaway. Empty-stack acceptance = input exhausted ∧ stack empty; the state is irrelevant.

TOC-U3B-Q16NATeasy

The standard construction converts a PDA that accepts by final state into one that accepts the same language by empty stack. It adds a new start state, which pushes a fresh bottom marker below the old one, and a new state that erases the stack, reachable by an ε-move from every old final state. How many states does the construction add?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two things must happen that the original machine cannot be trusted to do: the marker must be protected from being popped mid-computation, and the stack must be wiped at acceptance. Each needs one new state.

Step by step.

  1. The new start state pushes a fresh marker and then enters the old start configuration, so that the old machine cannot pop the bottom marker by accident. That is one new state.
  2. The erasing state is entered by an ε-move from every old final state, and pops symbols until the stack is empty. That is the second new state.
  3. No other state is needed: the old states and moves are kept unchanged.
  4. Total added: 1 + 1 = 2.

Answer: 2

Common misconception. Answering 1 by counting only the erasing state, or answering 3 by adding a separate accepting state — the empty stack itself is the accepting condition, so no final state is needed.

Takeaway. Final-state ⟶ empty-stack conversion: +2 states (fresh start, eraser).

TOC-U3B-Q17MSQmedium

Regarding acceptance by empty stack and acceptance by final state, select every statement that is TRUE.

  1. For nondeterministic pushdown automata the two modes accept exactly the same class of languages.
  2. If a DPDA accepts a language by empty stack, then no word of that language is a proper prefix of another word of it.
  3. If a DPDA accepts a language by final state, then no word of that language is a proper prefix of another word of it.
  4. Every language accepted by a DPDA by empty stack is also accepted by some DPDA by final state.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. For NPDAs the two modes are interchangeable; for DPDAs only one direction is free. The obstruction is the prefix property, which empty-stack acceptance forces on a deterministic machine.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: both constructions (add an eraser; add a marker and a fresh start) are nondeterministic-safe, so each mode can simulate the other.
  2. (B) true: a DPDA has one computation per input. If it empties the stack after reading w, no move is possible from an empty stack, so it can never read on to a longer word having w as prefix.
  3. (C) false: L = {a} ∪ {ab} is accepted by a DPDA by final state, and a is a proper prefix of ab.
  4. (D) true: from a DPDA accepting by empty stack, add a single new final state reachable by an ε-move whenever the stack becomes empty. Determinism is preserved.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming the equivalence for NPDAs carries over to DPDAs in both directions. It does not: final-state to empty-stack can fail, and the prefix property is exactly why.

Takeaway. NPDA: the modes are equivalent. DPDA: empty-stack ⊆ final-state, strictly.

TOC-U3B-Q18MCQmedium

Which one of the following statements about the prefix property is TRUE?

  1. Empty-stack acceptance forces the prefix property for both deterministic and nondeterministic PDAs.
  2. Empty-stack acceptance forces the prefix property for deterministic PDAs only.
  3. Empty-stack acceptance forces the prefix property for nondeterministic PDAs only.
  4. Empty-stack acceptance never forces the prefix property.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The prefix property (no word of the language is a proper prefix of another) follows from empty-stack acceptance only when the machine has a single computation per input.

Step by step.

  1. Deterministic case: once the stack is empty there is no move available, so the machine cannot continue past an accepted word. Hence no accepted word is a proper prefix of another — (B).
  2. Nondeterministic case: different branches may empty the stack at different lengths. For L = {a, ab} one branch empties the stack after the a and another after the b, so the property fails. Hence (A) and (C) are wrong.
  3. (D) is wrong because the deterministic case does force it.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Believing that any machine accepting by empty stack is stuck after acceptance. Only a deterministic machine is: a nondeterministic one may have other live branches.

Takeaway. Prefix property: forced by DPDA + empty stack; not forced by NPDA + empty stack.

TOC-U3B-Q19MCQhard

Let L = { a, ab }. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. L is accepted by some DPDA by final state but by no DPDA by empty stack.
  2. L is accepted by some DPDA by empty stack.
  3. L is not deterministic context-free.
  4. L is not context-free.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A finite language is regular, hence deterministic context-free. But the prefix property is a hard constraint on empty-stack acceptance by a deterministic machine, and this language breaks it.

Step by step.

  1. L is finite, so it is regular, so it is a DCFL: a DPDA accepting by final state reads a and accepts, or reads ab and accepts. That kills (C) and (D).
  2. Suppose a DPDA M accepted L by empty stack. On input a it must end with an empty stack after consuming the single symbol.
  3. An empty stack admits no move, so M is stuck on input a and can never read the extra b needed for ab. Hence ab would not be accepted, a contradiction.
  4. Equivalently, L lacks the prefix property, which empty-stack acceptance forces on deterministic machines. So (B) is false and (A) is true.

Answer: Option (A).

Common misconception. Reasoning "finite ⟹ regular ⟹ accepted by every kind of PDA". The class of the language is not in question; the acceptance mode is.

Takeaway. Prefix property is the sharp test: {a, ab} is a DCFL that no DPDA accepts by empty stack.

Equivalence with CFG · 12
TOC-U3-Q20MCQeasy

Which statement about context-free grammars and pushdown automata is TRUE?

  1. Every context-free grammar has an equivalent deterministic pushdown automaton.
  2. Every context-free grammar has an equivalent nondeterministic pushdown automaton, and every NPDA has an equivalent context-free grammar.
  3. Every NPDA has an equivalent regular expression.
  4. A context-free grammar can generate only regular languages.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. CFGs and NPDAs are two presentations of one class: the context-free languages. The equivalence fails as soon as 'nondeterministic' is replaced by 'deterministic'.

Step by step.

  1. CFG → NPDA. The top-down construction: one state, initial stack Z0S, an ε-move per production and a pop per matching terminal.
  2. NPDA → CFG. The triple construction with variables [q, A, p].
  3. Option (A) is false: {w wR} is context-free but no DPDA accepts it.
  4. Options (C) and (D) confuse the context-free class with the regular class; the regular languages are a proper subclass.

Answer: Option (B).

Common misconception. Writing 'PDA' when 'NPDA' is meant. The default reading of 'PDA' in most textbooks is nondeterministic; determinism must be stated explicitly.

Takeaway. CFG ≡ NPDA, strictly weaker than nothing and strictly stronger than regular expressions.

TOC-U3-Q21NATmedium

The grammar S → AB, A → aA | a, B → bB | b is converted to an NPDA by the standard top-down construction: one state, initial stack Z0S, a production replaces the top nonterminal by its right-hand side, and a terminal on top is popped when it matches the next input symbol. While the automaton accepts the string aabb, what is the largest number of symbols the stack ever holds, counting the bottom marker Z0?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The stack of the top-down NPDA holds the unexpanded part of the sentential form, with the leftmost symbol on top. Its height therefore tracks how much of the derivation is still outstanding.

Step by step.

  1. Start: stack Z0S — height 2.
  2. Expand S → AB: Z0AB — height 3.
  3. Expand A → aA: Z0aAB — height 4. This is the maximum.
  4. Pop the matched a: Z0AB — height 3.
  5. Expand A → a: Z0aB — height 3; pop: Z0B — height 2.
  6. Expand B → bB: Z0bB — height 3; pop: Z0B — height 2.
  7. Expand B → b: Z0b — height 2; pop: Z0 — height 1, stack empty of everything but the marker, input exhausted, accepted.
  8. The largest height reached on this (the only accepting) computation is 4.

Answer: 4.

Common misconception. Counting only nonterminals, or forgetting that expanding A → aA momentarily puts the terminal a on the stack as well. That terminal is a real stack symbol until it is matched.

Takeaway. Height = 1 (marker) + number of symbols in the current sentential form.

TOC-U3-Q22MCQmedium

In the standard construction of a context-free grammar from a pushdown automaton, the variables are triples [q, A, p]. What does the variable [q, A, p] generate?

  1. All strings accepted by the automaton when it starts in state q.
  2. All strings whose length is at most the stack height reached in state p.
  3. All strings w such that the automaton, starting in state q with A on top of the stack, can read w and end in state p having popped A, never inspecting the symbols below A.
  4. All strings that take the automaton from its start state to a final state.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The triple construction records a whole sub-computation: from a state with a particular symbol on top, to another state with that symbol gone.

Step by step.

  1. The automaton's behaviour depends on the state and on the top stack symbol only — the symbols below it are invisible until the top is popped. That is why a variable needs exactly three pieces of information.
  2. [q, A, p] describes a computation that starts in q with A on top, ends in p with A removed, and never looks below A in between.
  3. Productions encode single moves: for a move that replaces A by B1…Bk and steps from q to r, add [q, A, p] → a [r, B1, r1] … [rk−1, Bk, p].
  4. The start variable is [q0, Z0, p] for each final state p — the whole computation is one such triple with the bottom marker.
  5. Options (A) and (D) drop the stack symbol and the end state, which the construction needs; option (B) is not a language-theoretic notion at all.

Answer: Option (C).

Common misconception. Thinking that [q, A, p] generates a string of the language. Only the start triples do; the others generate fragments of the computation.

Takeaway. Three pieces of information — start state, stack top, end state — because the rest of the stack is hidden.

TOC-U3-Q23NATeasy

A grammar has 5 productions and terminal alphabet {a, b}. In the standard single-state NPDA construction, one move is added for each production and one for each terminal symbol. How many moves does the resulting NPDA have?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The construction mirrors the grammar piece by piece: one ε-move per production (expand a nonterminal) and one input-consuming move per terminal (match and pop).

Step by step.

  1. Production moves: 5, each of the form δ(q, ε, A) ∋ (q, α).
  2. Terminal moves: 2, namely δ(q, a, a) = {(q, ε)} and δ(q, b, b) = {(q, ε)}.
  3. Total: 5 + 2 = 7 moves.
  4. No move is needed for the start-up step: the initial stack is simply defined to be Z0S.

Answer: 7.

Common misconception. Adding an extra move for the start symbol or for the bottom marker. Neither is a move — both are part of the initial configuration.

Takeaway. Moves = |P| + |T| for the single-state construction.

TOC-U3-Q24MCQhard

The grammar S → aSb | ε is converted to an NPDA by the standard top-down construction with initial stack Z0S. During the computation that accepts aabb, what is the largest number of symbols the stack ever holds, counting Z0?

  1. 3
  2. 4
  3. 5
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each application of S → aSb adds two symbols to the sentential form; each matched terminal removes one. The peak comes just after an expansion, before the terminal is matched.

Step by step.

  1. Start: Z0S — height 2.
  2. Expand S → aSb: Z0aSb — height 4.
  3. Match the first a: Z0Sb — height 3.
  4. Expand S → aSb again: Z0aSbb — height 5. This is the maximum.
  5. Match the second a: Z0Sbb — height 4.
  6. Expand S → ε: Z0bb — height 3.
  7. Match both b's: height 2, then 1. Input exhausted, only Z0 left, accepted by empty stack.
  8. Maximum height: 5.

Answer: Option (C) — 5.

Common misconception. Option (D) 6 is reached by expanding S → aSb a third time, but that branch dies: only two a's are available in aabb. The question asks about the accepting computation, not about every branch the nondeterministic machine can explore.

Takeaway. For S → aSb | ε on anbn the peak height is 2n + 1: n unmatched S-expansions plus the marker.

TOC-U3-Q25MSQmedium

Select every statement about the equivalence of context-free grammars and pushdown automata that is TRUE.

  1. A grammar in Greibach Normal Form is simulated by an NPDA that has a single state and accepts by empty stack.
  2. The class of languages accepted by nondeterministic pushdown automata is exactly the class of context-free languages.
  3. Every context-free grammar has an equivalent deterministic pushdown automaton.
  4. If an NPDA accepts a language by empty stack, then some context-free grammar generates that language.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The equivalence CFG ≡ NPDA is exact, and it has a concrete constructive form on each side. The single-state simulation works especially cleanly for Greibach Normal Form, where every production begins with a terminal.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true. In GNF every production is A → aα. The single-state automaton reads a from the input and replaces A by α in one move; after |w| moves the stack is empty exactly when w has been generated.
  2. (B) true. This is the standard equivalence, proved by the top-down and triple constructions.
  3. (C) false. {w wR} has a context-free grammar but no DPDA, because the midpoint is unmarked.
  4. (D) true. That is the direction of the equivalence that converts a machine into a grammar.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Reading 'equivalent' as 'equivalent deterministically'. The equivalence is with the nondeterministic machine; the deterministic subclass is strictly smaller.

Takeaway. GNF gives the cleanest CFG → NPDA simulation: one state, one move per production application.

TOC-U3B-Q20MCQeasy

A context-free grammar in Greibach Normal Form is simulated by the standard top-down construction. How many states does that NPDA have?

  1. 1
  2. |V|
  3. |V| + |T|
  4. 2
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. In Greibach Normal Form every production starts with a terminal, so the machine can pop the nonterminal and push the rest of the right-hand side in one move that consumes exactly that terminal. No state is needed to remember anything.

Step by step.

  1. A GNF production has the form A → a X1 … Xk with a ∈ T.
  2. The machine uses one move δ(q, a, A) ∋ (q, X1 … Xk) that consumes a and replaces A by the rest of the right-hand side.
  3. All the information about which nonterminal is being expanded sits on the stack, so a single state suffices.
  4. The machine accepts by empty stack when the input is exhausted and the stack is empty.

Answer: Option (A) — one state.

Common misconception. Thinking each nonterminal needs its own state. The nonterminal is on top of the stack, not in the control unit, so the state carries no information.

Takeaway. GNF ⟹ one-state NPDA. The stack, not the state, holds the grammar.

TOC-U3B-Q21MSQmedium

Regarding the equivalence of context-free grammars and nondeterministic pushdown automata, select every statement that is TRUE.

  1. Every context-free grammar has an equivalent NPDA that accepts by empty stack.
  2. Every NPDA has an equivalent context-free grammar.
  3. The standard grammar-to-NPDA construction uses one state per nonterminal of the grammar.
  4. The standard NPDA-to-grammar construction produces a regular grammar.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Context-free grammars and nondeterministic pushdown automata generate and accept exactly the same class of languages; the two standard constructions prove each direction.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: the single-state top-down construction turns any grammar into an NPDA that accepts by empty stack.
  2. (B) true: the triple construction builds a grammar from any NPDA, with variables [q, X, p].
  3. (C) false: that construction uses exactly one state for the whole machine; the nonterminals live on the stack.
  4. (D) false: the grammar produced has variables of the form [q, X, p] and can generate non-regular languages such as {anbn}, so it is context-free and in general not regular.

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Taking "equivalent" to mean "the same shape". The constructions preserve the language, not the number of states, the number of variables or the class of the artefact built.

Takeaway. CFG ≡ NPDA. One state is enough in one direction; triples [q, X, p] are the price in the other.

TOC-U3B-Q22MCQeasy

In the standard single-state construction that turns a grammar G = (V, T, P, S) into an NPDA, which move is added for a terminal symbol a ∈ T?

  1. δ(q, ε, a) = {(q, ε)}
  2. δ(q, a, ε) = {(q, a)}
  3. δ(q, a, Z0) = {(q, a Z0)}
  4. δ(q, a, a) = {(q, ε)}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Once a terminal reaches the top of the stack, it must be matched against the next input symbol and removed. That is one move per terminal, independent of the grammar.

Step by step.

  1. A terminal can appear on top of the stack only after some production has pushed it there, and the string it belongs to must now start at the current input position.
  2. So the machine reads that same terminal from the input and pops it: δ(q, a, a) = {(q, ε)} — option (D).
  3. (A) pops the terminal without consuming input, so it would accept strings that do not contain the terminal.
  4. (B) and (C) push symbols instead of removing them, and use a stack top that has nothing to do with the matching rule.

Answer: Option (D).

Common misconception. Writing the move with the input symbol in the stack position, or with ε as the input symbol. Both break the one-to-one matching between stack top and input.

Takeaway. Grammar-to-NPDA: one move per production (replace a nonterminal) and one per terminal (δ(q, a, a) = {(q, ε)}).

TOC-U3B-Q23MCQhard

An NPDA has 4 states and stack alphabet Γ = {Z0, A, B}. The standard construction builds a context-free grammar whose variables are triples [q, X, p]. How many variables does it create?

  1. 12
  2. 16
  3. 48
  4. 64
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A variable [q, X, p] is indexed by an ordered pair of states and one stack symbol, so the count is |Q|2 × |Γ| — the pair is ordered because the two states play different roles.

Step by step.

  1. The first state q: 4 choices.
  2. The second state p: 4 choices, independent of the first, so 4 × 4 = 16 ordered pairs.
  3. The stack symbol X: 3 choices.
  4. Total: 16 × 3 = 48.
  5. Note that [q, X, p] and [p, X, q] are different variables: the first generates strings that take the machine from q to p while popping X, and the second does the reverse journey.

Answer: Option (C) — 48.

Common misconception. Using combinations instead of ordered pairs and answering 10 × 3 = 30, or forgetting the stack symbol and answering 16.

Takeaway. Variables in the triple construction = |Q|2|Γ|, with ordered pairs.

TOC-U3B-Q24MCQhard

G is the grammar S → SS | a, and M is the NPDA built from G by the standard single-state top-down construction with acceptance by empty stack. How many accepting computations does M have on the string aaa?

  1. 1
  2. 2
  3. 3
  4. 6
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Accepting computations of the single-state NPDA are in one-to-one correspondence with leftmost derivations, which are in one-to-one correspondence with parse trees. So the question is a Catalan count in disguise.

Step by step.

  1. The number of parse trees for a string of n terminals under S → SS | a is the Catalan number Cn−1, because the tree is a full binary tree with n leaves.
  2. For aaa we have n = 3, so the count is C2 = 2.
  3. Tree 1: S ⇒ SS ⇒ aS ⇒ aSS ⇒ aaS ⇒ aaa — the left S splits first.
  4. Tree 2: S ⇒ SS ⇒ SS S ⇒ aSS ⇒ aaS ⇒ aaa — the right S splits first, i.e. a grouped with aa.
  5. Each tree has exactly one leftmost derivation, and each leftmost derivation is one accepting computation, so the machine has 2 accepting computations.

Answer: Option (B) — 2.

Common misconception. Answering 1 by assuming the NPDA behaves deterministically, or 3 by counting the three positions at which the split could occur. The two splits are not distinguishable in that way: the outer S → SS always fires first, and the ambiguity is inside.

Takeaway. Accepting computations = leftmost derivations = parse trees; for S → SS | a that is a Catalan number.

TOC-U3B-Q25MSQmedium

Select every statement about the standard construction of a context-free grammar from a pushdown automaton that is TRUE.

  1. The variables are triples [q, X, p], where q and p are states and X is a stack symbol.
  2. For every state q and every stack symbol X the grammar contains the production [q, X, q] → ε.
  3. The grammar produced by the construction is always unambiguous.
  4. The start variable is [q0, Z0, p] for a final state p of the automaton.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The variable [q, X, p] generates exactly the strings that carry the machine from q to p while popping X without ever looking below it. The base case is the empty journey.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true: that is the definition of the variables in this construction.
  2. (B) true: the machine may stay in the same state and pop nothing at all, so the empty string is generated by [q, X, q] for every state and stack symbol. This is the base case that lets derivations terminate.
  3. (C) false: the construction produces a grammar that mirrors the automaton, and a nondeterministic automaton generally yields an ambiguous grammar.
  4. (D) true: a string is accepted when some computation starts at q0 with Z0 on the stack, ends in a final state p, and pops Z0 on the way — precisely the language of [q0, Z0, p].

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Expecting the construction to produce a tidy or unambiguous grammar. It is a correctness construction, not a parsing construction: the grammar faithfully copies every choice the automaton can make.

Takeaway. [q, X, p] generates the strings read while popping X from q to p; [q, X, q] → ε is the base case.

Review the 50 solved questions from Unit 4 — Turing Machines & Decidability
Standard TM models & variants · 10
TOC-U4-Q01MCQeasy

Which one of the following statements about variants of the Turing machine is TRUE?

  1. A Turing machine whose tape is infinite in both directions accepts strictly more languages than a Turing machine whose tape is infinite in only one direction.
  2. A Turing machine with k work tapes accepts strictly more languages than a single-tape Turing machine.
  3. A tape infinite in both directions and a tape infinite in one direction accept exactly the same class of languages; only the running time may change.
  4. A nondeterministic Turing machine accepts strictly more languages than a deterministic Turing machine.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. All the usual variants — a two-way infinite tape, several tapes, a stay-put head, a two-dimensional tape, nondeterminism — accept exactly the class of recursively enumerable languages. They differ in time and space complexity, never in recognition power.

Step by step.

  1. A one-way infinite tape simulates a two-way infinite tape by folding it: cells to the left of the origin are stored in a second track, in reverse order, with an end marker. Each simulated move costs O(1) real moves.
  2. A single tape simulates k tapes by storing all k tapes on one tape, separated by markers, and carrying a "virtual head" mark on each track. One simulated move costs O(k) real moves, so t simulated steps cost O(k·t²).
  3. A deterministic machine simulates a nondeterministic one by a breadth-first search over the tree of configurations (equivalently, by a 3-tape machine that enumerates and tests). This is slower — exponential in the worst case — but it recognises the same language.
  4. So every variant in the options recognises exactly the r.e. languages, and only the options that claim a strictly larger class are false.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) and (B) both claim extra recognition power. The fold construction and the multi-tape simulation above show there is none.
  • (D) confuses nondeterminism with extra power. Nondeterminism gives at most an exponential speed-up and is conjectured not to enlarge the class at all: a deterministic machine can search the whole computation tree, so it still accepts only r.e. languages.

Common misconception. "More hardware means more power." The model is already maximal: every reasonable extension is simulated with a slowdown, and the class of accepted languages never grows. Extra tapes buy time, not languages.

Takeaway. Variants of the Turing machine are equivalent in recognition power; the Church–Turing thesis is what makes this the definition of "computable" rather than a coincidence.

TOC-U4-Q02MCQeasy

A Turing machine has |Q| = 8 states, tape alphabet Γ with |Γ| = 5 (the blank is included in Γ) and input alphabet Σ ⊂ Γ with |Σ| = 3. Its transition function is the partial function δ : Q × Γ → Q × Γ × {L, R}. How many ordered pairs belong to the domain Q × Γ, that is, what is the largest number of entries the transition table can have?

  1. 13
  2. 40
  3. 24
  4. 120
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The domain of δ is the Cartesian product Q × Γ: one entry for every (state, tape symbol) pair, whether or not that pair is reachable.

Step by step.

  1. Number of states: |Q| = 8.
  2. Number of tape symbols: |Γ| = 5 — this is the tape alphabet, so it already contains the blank and every input symbol.
  3. Size of the domain: |Q| × |Γ| = 8 × 5 = 40.
  4. The input alphabet Σ plays no part in this count; it only restricts which symbols may appear in the initial tape contents.
  5. Each of the 40 pairs may be left undefined, which is why δ is a partial function and why 40 is a maximum.

Answer: Option (B) — 40 entries.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • 13 is 8 + 5, adding the two sizes instead of multiplying them.
  • 24 is 8 × 3: it uses the input alphabet and forgets the blank and the auxiliary tape symbols.
  • 120 is 8 × 5 × 3: it multiplies in the three head actions, but the action is the value of the entry, not part of its key.

Common misconception. Using |Σ| where |Γ| is meant. A machine may write any tape symbol, including the blank, on any cell, so the table is indexed by the tape alphabet.

Takeaway. A Turing machine is a finite object: |Q| · |Γ| is finite, so Turing machines can be enumerated and encoded as strings — which is exactly what the undecidability proofs in this unit exploit.

TOC-U4-Q03MCQeasy

Some textbook definitions allow the head to stay where it is, giving δ : Q × Γ → Q × Γ × {L, R, S}. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. The stay-put option can be removed without changing the accepted language, at the cost of at most one extra state and a constant factor more moves.
  2. Allowing the head to stay strictly increases the class of languages accepted.
  3. A machine that uses the stay-put option can accept a language that is not recursively enumerable.
  4. Allowing the head to stay makes the machine equivalent to a two-way finite automaton.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A "stay" move is not a new kind of power: it is a write followed by a move right and a move back left. Any machine using S can be rewritten to use only L and R with a bounded overhead.

Step by step.

  1. Suppose a move is δ(p, a) = (q, b, S). Replace it by two moves through a fresh state p′: first δ(p, a) = (p′, b, R), then δ(p′, c) = (q, c, L) for every symbol c.
  2. The fresh state p′ only ever moves left and changes nothing, so one extra state per machine is enough; the construction can even be made uniform with a fixed number of states.
  3. Each original move becomes two moves, so a computation of t moves becomes one of at most 2t moves. The language accepted is unchanged because the sequence of tape writes and the sequence of visited cells are the same.
  4. Therefore S is a normal-form abbreviation, not an extension.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) and (C) both claim extra recognition power. The machine is simulated exactly, so it accepts the same language; and no Turing-machine variant accepts a non-r.e. language.
  • (D) confuses the machine with a finite automaton. A two-way finite automaton has no tape to write on, so it accepts only regular languages, whereas a machine using S still writes and reads an unbounded tape.

Common misconception. Believing that any change to the move set changes the model's power. The test is always the same: can a standard machine simulate the variant, and can the variant simulate a standard machine? If both answers are yes, the classes coincide.

Takeaway. A Turing machine is characterised up to simulation. Fixed numbers of extra states, extra tapes or extra move options never matter; unbounded resources such as tape length do.

TOC-U4-Q04MSQmedium

Which of the following machines accept exactly the recursively enumerable languages?

  1. A deterministic Turing machine whose tape is infinite in both directions.
  2. A Turing machine with one read-only input tape and two work tapes.
  3. A deterministic Turing machine whose tape is restricted to at most 1000 cells.
  4. A nondeterministic Turing machine with a single tape.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A model accepts exactly the r.e. languages when it can simulate a standard TM and can itself be simulated by one. A bounded tape breaks the second half: finite memory plus finitely many states makes the machine a finite automaton.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true. A two-way infinite tape is simulated on a one-way tape by folding, and the one-way tape is a special case of the two-way tape, so the two models are mutually simulable.
  2. (B) true. A read-only input tape plus work tapes is the standard "off-line" Turing machine. It is simulated by an ordinary multi-tape machine (copy the input first), and it simulates one by never writing on the input tape.
  3. (C) false. With at most 1000 cells and a fixed state set the number of configurations is finite — at most |Q| · 1000 · 3¹⁰⁰⁰ for |Q| states and a 3-symbol alphabet — so the machine is a finite-state device and accepts only a regular language.
  4. (D) true. Nondeterminism is removed by a breadth-first search over configurations, so the accepted class is unchanged.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why option (C) fails. A machine with a fixed finite tape and finitely many states has finitely many configurations, and a computation is a path in a finite graph; the accepted language is therefore regular. Unbounded tape is the ingredient that separates a Turing machine from a finite automaton.

Common misconception. Reading "restricted to 1000 cells" as "a linear-bounded automaton". An LBA's tape is bounded by the input length n, so the bound grows with the input; a fixed bound of 1000 cells does not.

Takeaway. The three ingredients of the TM model are an unbounded tape, a finite control and a head. Drop the unbounded tape and you get a finite automaton; keep it and every other variant is equivalent.

TOC-U4-Q05NATmedium

A linear-bounded automaton is a Turing machine whose tape is restricted to the input cells. Consider a particular machine whose tape is restricted to exactly 4 cells, with |Q| = 3 states, tape alphabet |Γ| = 3 (the blank included, so a cell may hold a blank) and one head that always stands on one of the 4 cells. Treating a configuration as the triple (state, head position, tape contents), what is the largest possible number of distinct configurations of this machine?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A configuration is fixed by three independent facts: which state the machine is in, where the head is, and what each cell holds. Because the tape is bounded, all three ranges are finite, so the number of configurations is a finite product — and that finiteness is exactly why an LBA can be decided by brute force.

Step by step.

  1. States: |Q| = 3 choices.
  2. Head positions: 4 choices, since the tape has exactly 4 cells.
  3. Tape contents: each of the 4 cells independently holds one of the 3 tape symbols, so |Γ|⁴ = 3⁴ = 81.
  4. Multiply the independent choices: 3 × 4 × 81 = 972.
  5. If the machine ran for more than 972 moves it would have to repeat a configuration, and a repeated configuration means an infinite loop — so acceptance is decidable by simulating at most 972 moves.

Answer: 972 configurations.

How the answer was checked. By direct evaluation: 3⁴ = 81, then 3 × 4 × 81 = 972. The same argument with n cells gives |Q| · n · |Γ|ⁿ, which is finite for every fixed tape length.

Common misconception. Counting only the 3 states and the 4 head positions, or forgetting that the blank is a legal cell content. Every cell may independently be blank, so the blank belongs in the |Γ|⁴ factor.

Takeaway. Bounded space plus finite control gives finitely many configurations. This single counting fact yields the decidability of LBA acceptance and of context-sensitive membership — and it is why the Chomsky hierarchy stops being decidable only at Type 0.

TOC-U4-S2-Q01MCQeasy

In the standard formulation a Turing machine has a unique start state q₀ and two halting states, qaccept and qreject, for which no transition is defined. Which one of the following statements about the behaviour of such a machine is TRUE?

  1. Entering q reject does not stop the machine; it keeps moving its head to the right until it reads a blank cell.
  2. The computation stops as soon as q accept or q reject is entered; a computation that never enters either of them is said to loop on that input.
  3. A machine that has entered q accept may leave it again if a transition happens to be defined there.
  4. Every computation that loops must eventually enter q reject , because that is the state that records failure.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A halting state is a state in which the transition function is undefined. Reaching it ends the computation, and the outcome is read off from which halting state was reached. A computation that never reaches a halting state simply never ends: that is what “loops” means for a Turing machine.

Step by step.

  1. Both qaccept and qreject are halting states, so δ has no entry for any pair whose first component is one of them. The machine stops on entry, and accepts exactly when the state is qaccept.
  2. There is no third outcome. A machine either accepts, rejects, or runs forever; “running forever” is not a rejection, it is the absence of an answer.
  3. Option (A) is false because rejection is a halt, not a scan for a blank.
  4. Option (C) is false because qaccept is halting: nothing is defined there to take.
  5. Option (D) is false because a loop is precisely a computation that never enters a halting state.

Answer: Option (B) — entering either halting state ends the computation, and a computation that enters neither loops.

Common misconception. Reading a non-halting computation as a rejection. A machine that loops on w gives no answer at all; that is why the halting problem is not the same as the acceptance problem, and why an unbounded simulation of a machine can never be turned into a decision procedure.

Takeaway. Undefined transitions are not errors — they are the mechanism by which a Turing machine stops. Everything about decidability rests on this: a decider is a machine whose transitions are undefined only after it has produced a yes or a no.

TOC-U4-S2-Q02MCQeasy

A universal Turing machine U takes a pair ⟨M, w⟩ and simulates the machine M on the string w. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. U cannot exist, because a Turing machine cannot read the encoding of another machine.
  2. U exists, and therefore the halting problem is decidable: run U on ⟨M, w⟩ and see whether it stops.
  3. U exists only for machines that have a fixed, finite number of states, so it is not really universal.
  4. U exists and is an ordinary Turing machine; its existence does not decide the halting problem, because U itself may run forever on inputs for which M does.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Machines can be encoded as strings, and one machine can decode and step another. That is the universal machine. Simulation preserves the halting behaviour of the simulated machine, but it does not bound it: a simulator that faithfully follows a looping machine loops too.

Step by step.

  1. Encoding. Every tuple (Q, Σ, Γ, δ, q₀, qaccept, qreject) is a finite object, so it can be written as a binary string ⟨M⟩. The pair ⟨M, w⟩ is likewise a string.
  2. Simulation. U keeps the encoding ⟨M⟩ on one part of its tape and the simulated tape on another, and repeats: read the current simulated state and symbol, look up the entry in the encoded table, update. Each simulated move costs a bounded number of moves of U.
  3. Consequence for halting. If M halts on w then U halts on ⟨M, w⟩; if M loops then U loops. So U is a recogniser for the halting problem, not a decider.
  4. Option (B) is false: a decider must halt on every input, and U does not.
  5. Option (C) is false because the encoding is just a string; nothing in the definition restricts what a machine may read.
  6. Option (D) is false because U is a single fixed machine, and its own state set is finite whatever the simulated machine is.

Answer: Option (D) — a universal machine exists and simulates any other, but simulating is not deciding.

Common misconception. The most common error is to conclude that universality solves the halting problem. A simulator inherits the loop of the machine it simulates, so “run U and watch” is exactly the semi-decision procedure that is known to be incomplete.

Takeaway. Universality is the reason one machine can run another program, and it is also the reason the diagonal argument works: a machine that could decide halting would decide it for U itself, and that leads straight to a contradiction.

TOC-U4-S2-Q12MCQmedium

A machine with k tapes, where k is a fixed constant, runs in time t(n) on inputs of length n. The standard simulation of this machine by a single-tape Turing machine runs in time:

  1. O(t(n))
  2. O(t(n) log t(n))
  3. O(t(n)²)
  4. O(2^{t(n)})
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The single-tape simulation stores all k tapes on one tape, separated by markers, with a mark under each simulated head. Every simulated move must first bring the real head to the relevant marked cell and then bring it back, and the distance travelled is bounded by the number of tape cells that have been used, which is at most O(t(n)).

Step by step.

  1. After t(n) simulated moves no tape can hold more than t(n) + 1 non-blank cells, because the head writes at most one cell per move.
  2. A single simulated move requires a walk of length O(t(n)) along the packed tape, so it costs O(t(n)) real moves.
  3. There are t(n) simulated moves, so the total is O(t(n)) · O(t(n)) = O(t(n)²).
  4. The quadratic factor is the price of the packing, and it is the reason the single-tape model is used for definitions rather than for complexity results.
  5. Option (D) is the cost of removing nondeterminism, not of removing extra tapes.

Answer: Option (C) — O(t(n)²).

Common misconception. Assuming that extra tapes are free because a constant number of them is used. The constant hides a factor that depends on the length of the tape, and the tape length grows with the running time.

Takeaway. Multitape and single-tape machines accept the same languages and differ by a square in time. The pair (same class, polynomial difference) is what makes the model robust for computability and fragile for fine-grained complexity.

TOC-U4-S2-Q15NATmedium

A Turing machine has |Q| = 5 states and tape alphabet Γ = {␣, 1}, so |Γ| = 2. Count a configuration as the triple (state, head position, tape contents), where the tape contents is the string held in the first six cells of the tape, blank cells included, and the head stands on one of those six cells. How many distinct configurations of this shape are there?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A configuration is fixed independently by three facts: the state, the head position and what each cell holds. When all three ranges are finite the count is a product, and that product is what bounds the running time of a machine whose space is bounded.

Step by step.

  1. States: |Q| = 5 choices.
  2. Head positions: 6 choices, one for each of the six cells.
  3. Tape contents: each of the 6 cells independently holds one of the 2 tape symbols, giving 2⁶ = 64 strings. The blank is a legal cell content, so blank-filled strings are counted too.
  4. Multiply the independent choices: 5 × 6 × 64 = 1920.
  5. Sanity check on the shape of the formula: with n cells it is |Q| · n · |Γ|ⁿ, which grows without bound as n grows — the tape is not bounded here, only the window that is being counted.

Answer: 1920 configurations.

Common misconception. Forgetting that a cell may be blank, which reduces the content factor to 2⁵ and gives 960. The blank is a symbol of Γ, so it is one of the 2 choices for every one of the 6 cells.

Takeaway. Bounded space plus finite control gives finitely many configurations, and the count is |Q| · (cells) · |Γ|^{cells}. That product is the engine behind the decidability of LBA acceptance and of context-sensitive membership.

TOC-U4-S2-Q23MSQhard

Which of the following statements about the robustness of the Turing-machine model are TRUE? Select all that apply.

  1. A machine with k tapes can be simulated by a single-tape machine with only a quadratic slowdown.
  2. A machine whose tape is infinite in both directions can be simulated by one whose tape is infinite in one direction with no asymptotic slowdown.
  3. A nondeterministic Turing machine can be simulated by a deterministic Turing machine, so both accept exactly the recursively enumerable languages.
  4. A Turing machine whose tape is a two-dimensional grid accepts exactly the same class of languages as the standard one.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Robustness means the accepted class survives every reasonable change of the model. Time and space may blow up, sometimes badly, but the class of languages does not move — except when the change bounds the storage.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true. Pack the k tapes onto one tape with markers and one mark per simulated head. Each simulated move costs a walk of length at most O(t(n)), so the total is O(t(n)²). Same class, quadratic time.
  2. (B) false. Folding a two-way tape onto a one-way tape is possible, but the fold marker sits at a fixed position and the head must travel to it and back on every move that changes halves. The distance grows with the number of cells in use, so the slowdown is O(n) in the worst case, not constant.
  3. (C) true. Breadth-first search over configurations removes nondeterminism. The search may take exponential time, but it terminates exactly when some branch accepts, so the accepted language is unchanged.
  4. (D) true. A two-dimensional tape is simulated by numbering the cells row by row on a one-dimensional tape; the head then has to walk to the target cell, which costs time but not expressive power.

Answer: Options (A), (C) and (D).

Common misconception. Assuming that every simulation is asymptotically free. The multi-tape and two-way-tape constructions preserve the language but not the running time, and the two-way-tape one is the sharper trap because it looks like a mere relabelling of cells.

Takeaway. Separate the question “same languages?” from “same time?”. Almost every model variant answers yes to the first; almost none answers yes to the second.

Recursive vs recursively enumerable · 10
TOC-U4-Q06MCQeasy

Let L be a language over a finite alphabet and let L̄ be its complement. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. If L is recursively enumerable then L̄ is recursively enumerable.
  2. Every recursively enumerable language is recursive.
  3. If both L and L̄ are recursively enumerable then L is recursive.
  4. If L is recursive then L̄ is not recursively enumerable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Recursive means "there is a decider — an algorithm that always halts with yes or no". Recursively enumerable means "there is a recogniser — an algorithm that halts with yes on members and may run forever on non-members". Two recognisers, one for L and one for L̄, combine into a decider by running them in parallel.

Step by step.

  1. Assume L is recognised by M₁ and L̄ by M₂.
  2. On input w, run M₁ and M₂ alternately, one step each, in a dovetail.
  3. Every string lies in exactly one of L, L̄, so exactly one of the two machines eventually says yes.
  4. When it does, halt and answer accordingly. The combined procedure always halts, so L is recursive.
  5. Conversely, if L is recursive then L̄ is recursive too (swap the accepting and rejecting states), so both are r.e. Hence "both r.e." and "recursive" coincide for a language and its complement.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is false: the halting set K = {⟨M, w⟩ : M halts on w} is r.e. while its complement is not r.e. at all.
  • (B) is false: K is the standard example of an r.e. language that is not recursive.
  • (D) is false: every recursive language is r.e. — a decider is in particular a recogniser.

Common misconception. Thinking r.e. and recursive are the same because both have "an algorithm". The difference is whether the algorithm must halt on every input. A recogniser may loop forever on strings outside the language, and that is precisely the gap.

Takeaway. Recursive ⊂ RE, and the inclusion is strict. Equivalently, L is recursive iff both L and L̄ are r.e.

TOC-U4-Q07MCQmedium

Which one of the following classes of languages is NOT closed under complementation?

  1. The recursive languages
  2. The recursively enumerable languages
  3. The regular languages
  4. The context-sensitive languages
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Closure under complement is a property of the decider, not of the recogniser. If a class has a machine that always halts, complementing is free: swap the accept and reject outcomes. If a class only has machines that may loop, there is nothing to swap.

Step by step.

  1. Recursive: given a decider M for L, build M′ that runs M and reverses the answer. It still always halts, so L̄ is recursive. Closed.
  2. Regular: a DFA is total, so swapping final and non-final states gives a DFA for the complement. Closed.
  3. Context-sensitive: closed by the Immerman–Szelepcsényi theorem, since CSL = NSPACE(n) and nondeterministic space classes with a space-constructible bound are closed under complement. Closed.
  4. Recursively enumerable: not closed. The halting set K is r.e. by direct simulation, but K̄ is not r.e. — if it were, K would be recursive by the dovetail argument, contradicting the undecidability of halting.
  5. Only the r.e. class fails, so (B) is the answer.

Answer: Option (B) — the recursively enumerable languages.

Why the others are closed. All three are classes with total machines, or with a space bound that makes the configuration graph finite, so a complement can be decided by swapping the outcome or by exhaustive search.

Common misconception. Assuming every class in the Chomsky hierarchy is closed under complement because the regular and context-free cases are handled in the same chapter. Context-free languages are not closed under complement (and not under intersection), yet the larger class of context-sensitive languages is — the hierarchy is not monotone in its closure properties.

Takeaway. Complement closure fails exactly where the machine may not halt. That is the operational meaning of the recursive / r.e. divide.

TOC-U4-Q08MSQmedium

For a language L over a finite alphabet, which of the following statements are TRUE?

  1. If L and L̄ are both recursively enumerable, then L is recursive.
  2. If L is recursive, then L is recursively enumerable.
  3. If L is r.e. but not recursive, then L̄ is not r.e.
  4. If L is not r.e., then L̄ is r.e.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Three facts organise this question: recursive ⊂ r.e.; "both r.e." ⟺ recursive; and a language that is r.e. but not recursive has a complement that is not r.e. Statement (D) is the only one that goes wrong, because a language can be neither r.e. nor co-r.e.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true. Dovetail the two recognisers: exactly one of them accepts w, so the combined machine always halts. That is a decider for L.
  2. (B) true. A decider is a recogniser that happens to halt on every input, so every recursive language is r.e.
  3. (C) true. If L̄ were also r.e. then (A) would make L recursive, contradicting the hypothesis. So L̄ is not r.e.
  4. (D) false. Take the join J = {0x : x ∈ K} ∪ {1x : x ∉ K}, where K is the halting set. The join is r.e. exactly when K is, so J is not r.e.; and its complement is not r.e. either. Hence "not r.e." does not force the complement to be r.e.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why (D) fails. The four possibilities are: recursive (both r.e.), r.e. only (like K), co-r.e. only (like K̄), and neither (like the join above). "Not r.e." covers the last two, and only in the co-r.e. case is the complement r.e.

Common misconception. Believing every language is either r.e. or co-r.e. Most languages are neither: there are uncountably many languages but only countably many Turing machines.

Takeaway. "Recursive" is a decision procedure; "r.e." is a recognition procedure. Everything in this unit is a consequence of that one distinction.

TOC-U4-Q09NATmedium

Consider the following five languages, where M ranges over all Turing machines, G over all context-free grammars and w over all strings.
(i) {⟨M, w⟩ : M halts on w within 100 steps}
(ii) {⟨M⟩ : L(M) = ∅}
(iii) {⟨G⟩ : G is a context-free grammar and L(G) = ∅}
(iv) {⟨M⟩ : M accepts ε}
(v) {⟨M⟩ : M halts on every input}
How many of these five languages are recursive?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A language is recursive when a total algorithm decides it. A bounded simulation or a finite fixed-point computation is total; anything that needs to know how a machine behaves on all inputs is not.

Step by step.

  1. (i) recursive. Simulate M on w for exactly 100 steps and report whether it halted. The simulation is bounded, so the procedure always halts — this is the standard "bounded halting is decidable" fact.
  2. (ii) not recursive. L(M) = ∅ is a non-trivial property of L(M), so Rice's theorem applies. Intuitively, to say yes you would have to prove that no input is ever accepted, which needs unbounded search.
  3. (iii) recursive. Emptiness of a context-free language is decidable: compute the set of productive nonterminals by a fixed-point iteration, or test whether the start symbol can derive any terminal string. Always terminates.
  4. (iv) not recursive. It is r.e. — just run M on ε — but not recursive: deciding whether M accepts ε is undecidable by reduction from the halting problem.
  5. (v) not recursive. M halts on every input is a non-trivial property of L(M), undecidable by Rice's theorem; it is not even r.e.
  6. Counting: exactly (i) and (iii) are recursive, so the answer is 2.

Answer: 2

How the answer was checked. Each of the five is settled by a known result rather than by intuition: bounded halting is decidable by the 100-step clock; CFG emptiness is decidable by the productivity fixed point; (ii), (iv) and (v) are three different undecidable problems, two of them by Rice's theorem and one by reduction from halting.

Common misconception. Marking (i) undecidable "because halting is undecidable". The undecidability of halting concerns unbounded running time; once the time budget is fixed the problem becomes a finite simulation. Conversely, marking (iv) decidable because it looks like a single test on one string — the test may not terminate.

Takeaway. Bounded questions are decidable; unbounded questions about the whole behaviour of a machine are not. That single criterion answers most questions in this unit.

TOC-U4-Q10MCQhard

Let A and B be recursively enumerable languages over Σ such that A ∪ B = Σ* and A ∩ B = ∅. Which one of the following must hold?

  1. A and B are both recursive.
  2. A is regular.
  3. A is recursively enumerable but need not be recursive.
  4. At least one of A and B is finite.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Disjointness together with A ∪ B = Σ* makes the two languages exact complements of each other. A language whose complement is also r.e. is recursive — so both parts are recursive, whatever they look like.

Step by step.

  1. Since A ∪ B = Σ*, every string is in A or in B.
  2. Since A ∩ B = ∅, no string is in both. Together the two facts give B = Σ* \ A = Ā.
  3. So A is r.e. (given) and Ā is r.e. (given). Dovetail the two recognisers: on any w exactly one of them accepts, so the combined procedure always halts.
  4. Hence A is recursive, and B, being its complement, is recursive as well.
  5. No regularity and no finiteness follows: take A to be any recursive non-regular language, for instance {aⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 0}, and B its complement.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) fails with A = {aⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 0}, which is recursive but not regular.
  • (C) fails because the extra hypothesis "the complement is r.e." is exactly what rules out the r.e.-but-not-recursive case.
  • (D) fails with the same example: both A and B are infinite.

Common misconception. Using only A ∪ B = Σ* and concluding "one of them must be the whole thing". Disjointness and covering together are much stronger than either alone: they say the two languages split Σ* into a decidable pair.

Takeaway. A partition of Σ* into two r.e. parts is automatically a partition into two recursive parts. This is the standard route for showing that a language is recursive: exhibit recognisers for both it and its complement.

TOC-U4-S2-Q03MCQeasy

Which one of the following is an equivalent characterisation of the class of recursively enumerable languages over a finite alphabet Σ?

  1. Exactly the languages that are the range of some total computable function f : Σ* → Σ* .
  2. Exactly the finite languages.
  3. Exactly the languages whose complement is recursive.
  4. Exactly the languages that are the domain of some Turing machine that halts on every input.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Three descriptions of the same class: recognised by a machine that halts on members, enumerated by a printer, and equal to the range of a total computable function. The third is the least familiar and the most useful, because it removes the machine from the picture.

Step by step.

  1. Let L be the range of a total computable f. To recognise L, on input w compute f(0), f(1), f(2), … in a dovetail and accept when w appears. Every member appears at some finite index, so every member is accepted.
  2. Conversely, let M recognise L. Enumerate the pairs (i, k) in order of i + k; for each pair run M on the i-th string for k steps and print that string if M has accepted it. Define f to return the string printed at step n (or a fixed string if nothing is printed then). This f is total and its range is L.
  3. Option (B) is false: Σ* is r.e. and infinite.
  4. Option (C) is false: that describes the recursive languages, a strict subclass.
  5. Option (D) is false: the domain of a machine that halts on every input is decided by that machine, so it is recursive, not merely r.e.

Answer: Option (A) — the r.e. languages are exactly the ranges of total computable functions.

Common misconception. Confusing “domain of a machine that always halts” with “r.e.”. Requiring the machine to halt on every input builds a decider, which yields only the recursive languages; for the r.e. class the machine may be undefined outside the language.

Takeaway. Range, domain of a partial computable function, and recognition are three faces of one class. When a question phrases an r.e. set as a range, the dovetail enumeration is almost always the proof to use.

TOC-U4-S2-Q04MSQmedium

An enumerator is a Turing machine with a printer that writes out strings, one after another. Which of the following statements are TRUE? Select all that apply.

  1. A language is recursively enumerable exactly when some enumerator prints precisely its members.
  2. An enumerator for an infinite r.e. language must print its members in increasing order of length.
  3. If L is recursive, then some enumerator prints the members of L in lexicographic order.
  4. An enumerator for L also decides L , because it can be asked whether a given string is eventually printed.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. An enumerator converts “is recognised” into “is printed”. The two are equivalent for r.e. languages, but printing is weaker than deciding: an enumerator that never prints w never tells you so.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true. From an enumerator build a recogniser: run it and accept when w is printed. From a recogniser build an enumerator by dovetailing M over all strings and all step counts, printing a string whenever some computation accepts it.
  2. (B) false. Nothing in the definition constrains the order. The standard enumerator derived from a recogniser prints in whatever order the dovetail produces, which is by i + k and not by length.
  3. (C) true. A decider for L can test strings one at a time in lexicographic order (shorter strings first, alphabetically within a length) and print those it accepts. Lexicographic order is a well-order with finitely many predecessors of each string, so the printing runs through the whole list.
  4. (D) false. An enumerator gives a semi-decision: if w ∈ L it prints w eventually, but if w ∉ L it runs forever and the question is never answered. Deciding needs a machine that halts either way.

Answer: Options (A) and (C).

Common misconception. Assuming that an enumerator decides, or that enumeration must be tidy. The order is free; what a decider additionally needs is a bound on when to stop, which an enumerator never supplies.

Takeaway. Enumerability is recognition with the input removed. That is why the two notions coincide for r.e. languages and why neither of them coincides with decidability.

TOC-U4-S2-Q10NATmedium

Let L₁ be a recursive language, let L₂ be recursively enumerable but not recursive, and set L₃ = L̄₂ (the complement of L₂ in Σ*) and L₄ = L₂ ∪ L₃. How many of the four languages L₁, L₂, L₃, L₄ are recursive?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The class of recursive languages is closed under complement, union and intersection; the class of r.e. languages is not closed under complement. That single asymmetry settles every one of the four cases.

Step by step.

  1. L₁ is recursive by hypothesis. That is one.
  2. L₂ is r.e. and not recursive by hypothesis. That is not one.
  3. L₃ = L̄₂. If L₃ were recursive, its complement L₂ would also be recursive, because the recursive languages are closed under complement. So L₃ is not recursive. (Equivalently: L₂ r.e. and not recursive means L̄₂ is not even r.e.)
  4. L₄ = L₂ ∪ L̄₂ = Σ*, and Σ* is decided by the machine that accepts everything. That is one.
  5. The count is therefore 1 + 0 + 0 + 1 = 2.

Answer: 2 — only L₁ and L₄ are recursive.

Common misconception. Expecting the complement of a non-recursive language to be non-recursive for the same reason, or counting L₄ as non-recursive because it is built from L₂. The union of a set and its complement is the whole alphabet, which is trivially decidable.

Takeaway. A set and its complement are recursive together or not at all. The moment you see L ∪ L̄ or L ∩ L̄, evaluate it: the first is Σ* and the second is ∅, and both are decidable whatever L is.

TOC-U4-S2-Q16MCQeasy

Which one of the following statements about the class of recursively enumerable languages is TRUE?

  1. It is closed under union and under intersection, but not under complementation.
  2. It is closed under complementation.
  3. It is closed under union but not under intersection.
  4. It is closed under neither union nor intersection.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Two recognisers combine for union by running in parallel, and for intersection by running in sequence. Complementation is the operation that fails, because recognising a set gives no way to recognise its complement.

Step by step.

  1. Union. Dovetail the recognisers for L₁ and L₂ on the input and accept if either accepts.
  2. Intersection. Run the recogniser for L₁ to acceptance, then the recogniser for L₂. Both must accept, and both halt when they do, so the composite halts on every member of the intersection.
  3. Complementation fails. HALT is r.e. while its complement is not; if the class were closed under complement, both would be r.e. and HALT would be decidable.
  4. Option (C) is false because intersection closure holds; options (B) and (D) contradict the two facts above.

Answer: Option (A) — closed under union and intersection, not under complementation.

Common misconception. Believing that intersection is the operation that breaks, because intersection “feels” stricter. For recognition, strictness is cheap: a sequential run of two recognisers still halts on members.

Takeaway. Union and intersection are easy for recognisers; complement is the hard one, and it is exactly the operation that separates r.e. from recursive.

TOC-U4-S2-Q21MCQhard

Let K = {⟨M⟩ : M halts on the input ⟨M⟩}, and let ≤m denote mapping reducibility. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. Every recursively enumerable language L satisfies L ≤ m K .
  2. K ≤ m L for every recursively enumerable language L .
  3. K is recursive.
  4. The complement K̄ is recursively enumerable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. K is the halting problem with both arguments collapsed into one, and it is complete for the r.e. languages: every r.e. set reduces to it. That is what makes it the standard vehicle for undecidability proofs.

Step by step.

  1. K is r.e.: simulate M on ⟨M⟩ and accept when it halts.
  2. Let L be r.e., recognised by ML. Define f(w) = ⟨Mw⟩ where Mw on input x ignores x and runs ML on w. Then Mw halts on ⟨Mw⟩ exactly when w ∈ L, so w ∈ L ⇔ f(w) ∈ K.
  3. Hence L ≤m K for every r.e. L. Option (A) holds.
  4. Option (B) fails: reducing K to an arbitrary r.e. set would make every r.e. set as hard as K, which is false for ∅.
  5. Option (C) fails because a decider for K would decide the halting problem.
  6. Option (D) fails because K is r.e. and undecidable, so K̄ is not r.e.

Answer: Option (A) — K is complete for the recursively enumerable languages.

Common misconception. Assuming reducibility is symmetric. Mapping reductions form a preorder, not an equivalence: hard sets absorb everything below them, and reducing a hard set to an easy one is impossible.

Takeaway. One complete set is enough for every undecidability proof. To show an r.e. set L undecidable, reduce K (or HALT) to it; to show it is r.e., exhibit a recogniser.

Halting problem & undecidability · 10
TOC-U4-Q11MCQeasy

Which one of the following languages is recursive (decidable)?

  1. {⟨M, w⟩ : M halts on w}
