  2. {⟨M⟩ : L(M) = Σ*}
  3. {⟨M⟩ : M halts on every input}
  4. {⟨M, w⟩ : M halts on w within 1000 steps}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A language is decidable when there is a procedure that always halts. The only option with a built-in clock is (D): once the number of steps is bounded, the simulation is a finite, terminating computation.

Step by step.

  1. Decider for (D): on input ⟨M, w⟩, decode the pair, then simulate M on w for at most 1000 steps. If the machine halts inside the budget, accept; otherwise reject.
  2. The simulation takes at most 1000 steps of work, so the decider halts on every input. Hence (D) is recursive.
  3. Option (A) is the halting problem itself, undecidable by the diagonal argument.
  4. Option (C) asks whether a machine halts on all inputs, a non-trivial property of L(M), undecidable by Rice's theorem (indeed not even r.e.).
  5. Option (B) is also a non-trivial property of L(M), undecidable by Rice's theorem.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) is the canonical undecidable problem: the diagonal machine differs from every machine on its own code.
  • (B) and (C) are semantic properties of L(M) that hold for some machines and fail for others, so Rice's theorem applies.

Common misconception. Reading "halts on w within 1000 steps" as a special case of "halts on w". The bound is what makes the difference: with a bound the search space is finite, without one it is not.

Takeaway. Bounded simulation is always decidable. Undecidability enters only when a procedure would have to run an unbounded computation to answer "no".

TOC-U4-Q12MCQeasy

To prove that a language L is undecidable by reduction from the halting problem HALT = {⟨M, w⟩ : M halts on w}, which one of the following must be constructed?

  1. A computable function f such that ⟨M, w⟩ ∈ HALT ⟺ f(⟨M, w⟩) ∈ L for every pair (M, w).
  2. A computable function f such that x ∈ L ⟺ f(x) ∈ HALT for every string x.
  3. A Turing machine that decides L.
  4. An enumeration of all the strings in L.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A reduction from HALT to L is a computable map that translates questions about halting into questions about L, preserving the answer. If such a map exists and L were decidable, halting would be decidable too — a contradiction.

Step by step.

  1. What must be built: a computable f with ⟨M, w⟩ ∈ HALT ⟺ f(⟨M, w⟩) ∈ L. Then a decider for L composed with f would decide HALT, which is impossible.
  2. In practice f is described by a construction: on input ⟨M, w⟩, build the description of a new machine M′ that behaves as required, and output ⟨M′⟩.
  3. This direction — from the known-undecidable problem to the new one — is what transfers undecidability.
  4. Option (B) is the opposite direction, L ≤m HALT. It only shows that L is no harder than halting, i.e. that L is r.e.; every decidable language reduces to HALT in that direction, so nothing is proved.

Answer: Option (A).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (B) is the classic direction error: L ≤m HALT shows only L ∈ RE.
  • (C) would prove L decidable, the opposite of the goal — and it cannot exist.
  • (D) shows only that L is r.e., which is far weaker than decidability.

Common misconception. Getting the arrow backwards. Remember the mnemonic: to prove L hard, reduce a known hard problem to L, so that solving L would solve the hard one.

Takeaway. A ≤m B means "A is at most as hard as B". Reductions therefore flow from the problem already known to be hard to the problem to be proved hard.

TOC-U4-Q13MSQmedium

Which of the following problems are undecidable?

  1. Given a Turing machine M, does M halt on the empty string?
  2. Given a Turing machine M and a state q of M, does M ever enter the state q when started on the empty string?
  3. Given a DFA A, does A accept at least one string?
  4. Given a Turing machine M, does M ever write the symbol 1 on its tape when started on the empty string?
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Each option asks about the unbounded behaviour of a machine, except (C), which asks about a finite automaton — and finite automata can be decided by graph search. The three Turing-machine questions are undecidable, each by a small reduction from halting.

Step by step.

  1. (A) undecidable. HALT reduces to it: given ⟨M, w⟩, build M′ that first writes w on its tape and then simulates M. Then M′ halts on ε iff M halts on w.
  2. (B) undecidable. Given ⟨M, w⟩, build M′ that simulates M and enters a fresh state q only when that simulation halts. Then M′ enters q iff M halts on w.
  3. (C) decidable. Do a reachability search from the start state of A over its finitely many states, and accept iff some final state is reachable. The graph is finite, so the search terminates.
  4. (D) undecidable. Given ⟨M, w⟩, build M′ that simulates M and writes 1 only when the simulation halts. Then M′ ever writes 1 iff M halts on w.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (D).

Why (C) survives. A DFA has finitely many states, so "is a final state reachable?" is a finite graph question with a terminating algorithm. Emptiness, finiteness and universality of a DFA are decidable for the same reason.

Common misconception. Believing that a "simple" question about a Turing machine must be decidable. "Does it ever enter state q?" and "does it ever write 1?" look local, but the answer may depend on an unbounded computation, and unbounded is what makes it undecidable.

Takeaway. Undecidability is about the quantifier structure: a question of the form "does there exist a time t at which ..." over an unbounded t is undecidable, while the same question over a finite object is decidable.

TOC-U4-Q14NAThard

Consider the deterministic Turing machine with states q₀, q₁, q₂, q₃, q_f, tape alphabet {0, 1, X, Y, ␣} (␣ is the blank) and the transition function below. X and Y are "crossed-off" marks; every pair not listed sends the machine to the halting reject state q_r.
δ(q₀,0)=(q₁,X,R)   δ(q₀,Y)=(q₃,Y,R)   δ(q₁,0)=(q₁,0,R)   δ(q₁,Y)=(q₁,Y,R)   δ(q₁,1)=(q₂,Y,L)
δ(q₂,0)=(q₂,0,L)   δ(q₂,Y)=(q₂,Y,L)   δ(q₂,X)=(q₀,X,R)   δ(q₃,Y)=(q₃,Y,R)   δ(q₃,␣)=(q_f,␣,R)
The head starts on the leftmost cell, which holds the first input symbol. Started on the input 0011, how many transitions (applications of δ) does the machine make before it halts?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The machine is the standard "cross off one 0 at the left and one 1 at the right" decider for {0ⁿ1ⁿ : n ≥ 1}. Counting its moves is a matter of walking the head across the tape; nothing has to be guessed.

Step by step.

  1. Write the tape as 0 0 1 1 ␣ ␣ … with the head on the first cell, and run the table literally.
  2. Moves 1–3: (q₀,0)→(q₁,X,R) crosses the first 0; (q₁,0)→(q₁,0,R) skips the second 0; (q₁,1)→(q₂,Y,L) crosses the second 1 and turns left. Tape: X 0 Y 1.
  3. Moves 4–5: the head walks left over the 0 and the X — (q₂,0)→(q₂,0,L) then (q₂,X)→(q₀,X,R) — so q₀ now faces the first uncrossed 0.
  4. Moves 6–10: the second pass crosses the remaining 0 (move 6), skips the crossed Y (move 7), crosses the last 1 (move 8), then walks left over Y and X (moves 9–10). Tape: X X Y Y.
  5. Moves 11–13: (q₀,Y)→(q₃,Y,R) moves to the check phase, (q₃,Y)→(q₃,Y,R) skips the second Y, and (q₃,␣)→(q_f,␣,R) reads the blank and halts. Total: 13 transitions.
  6. Cross-check with the closed form: this machine needs 2n² + 2n + 1 moves on 0ⁿ1ⁿ, giving 5, 13 and 25 for n = 1, 2, 3, and the trace above reproduces 13 exactly.

Answer: 13 transitions.

How the answer was checked. The machine was executed step by step and the transition count recorded at each move; the 13 moves above are the complete computation, ending when δ(q₃,␣) is applied to the blank. The same run on 000111 gives 25 moves and on 01 gives 5, both matching 2n² + 2n + 1, which confirms the trace is not an off-by-one.

Common misconception. Counting cells visited or passes instead of applications of δ, or forgetting the final transition that reads the blank and enters q_f. Every application of δ counts as one move, including the one that halts the machine.

Takeaway. A Turing machine is a completely explicit object: given the table, the computation and its length are determined. This is why "does it halt within k steps" is decidable — the trace is computable — while "does it halt at all" is not.

TOC-U4-Q15MCQhard

Let ≤m denote mapping reducibility. Which one of the following statements is TRUE for all languages A and B?

  1. If A ≤m B and B is undecidable, then A is undecidable.
  2. If A ≤m B and A is r.e., then B is r.e.
  3. If A ≤m B and B is decidable, then A is undecidable.
  4. If A ≤m B and A is undecidable, then B is undecidable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A ≤m B means: there is a computable f with x ∈ A ⟺ f(x) ∈ B. So A is at most as hard as B. Hardness therefore flows backwards along the arrow, and decidability flows forwards.

Step by step.

  1. Assume A ≤m B via f.
  2. If B were decidable with decider N, then N ∘ f would decide A: compute f(x), then run N. Since f is computable and N always halts, the composite always halts.
  3. So decidability of B gives decidability of A. In contrapositive form: if A is undecidable then B is undecidable. That is statement (D).
  4. The same argument with "recognisable" in place of "decidable" shows: if A is r.e. then B is r.e.
  5. The converses all fail, because an easy language reduces to a hard one: ∅ reduces to every language, including the undecidable ones.

Answer: Option (D).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) reverses the arrow. A ≤m B says nothing about A when B is the hard one: ∅ reduces to any language, yet ∅ is decidable.
  • (B) has the same defect: r.e.-ness of the left side does not transfer to the right side.
  • (C) contradicts the transfer of decidability proved above.

Common misconception. Reading A ≤m B as "equally hard". It is a one-way statement: the left side is no harder than the right. Hardness of B says nothing about A.

Takeaway. To prove B undecidable, reduce a known undecidable language to B. To prove A decidable, reduce A to a known decidable language. Both are applications of the single composition rule above.

TOC-U4-S2-Q06MCQeasy

Let HALT = {⟨M, w⟩ : M halts on the input w}. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. HALT is recursive but not recursively enumerable.
  2. HALT is not recursively enumerable.
  3. HALT is recursively enumerable but not recursive.
  4. HALT and its complement are both recursively enumerable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Recognising is easy and deciding is impossible. A recogniser for HALT is the universal machine; a decider would have to answer no for a machine that loops, and no finite amount of simulation can establish that a computation will never end.

Step by step.

  1. Recognisable. On input ⟨M, w⟩, simulate M on w. Accept as soon as the simulation halts. Every halting pair is accepted, so HALT is r.e.
  2. Not decidable. Suppose a decider D existed. Build N which on input ⟨M⟩ runs D on ⟨M, ⟨M⟩⟩ and then does the opposite of what M does. Then N on ⟨N⟩ halts exactly when it does not — a contradiction.
  3. Hence HALT is r.e. and not recursive.
  4. Option (D) is false: if both a set and its complement were r.e., running the two recognisers in parallel would decide the set, contradicting the previous step.

Answer: Option (C) — recursively enumerable but not recursive.

Common misconception. Believing that a long simulation is evidence of non-halting. No finite run can rule out a halt on the next step, so simulation yields a recogniser and never a decider.

Takeaway. The halting problem is the canonical r.e.-but-not-recursive set. Any question that asks for its status should be answered on the two axes separately: recognisable (yes) and decidable (no).

TOC-U4-S2-Q13MCQmedium

Let HALTε = {⟨M⟩ : M halts when started on the empty string}. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. HALT ε is decidable, because the input to M is fixed and very short.
  2. HALT ε is recursively enumerable but not recursive, and HALT ≤ m HALT ε .
  3. HALT ε is co-recursively enumerable but not recursively enumerable.
  4. HALT ε is neither recursively enumerable nor co-recursively enumerable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Fixing the input does not bound the computation. HALTε is the halting problem with the second component frozen, and the freezing is undone by a one-line construction.

Step by step.

  1. Reduction. Given ⟨M, w⟩, build Mw which on any input erases that input, writes w on the tape and then runs M. The construction is computable and Mw halts on ε exactly when M halts on w. Hence HALT ≤m HALTε.
  2. Consequence. If HALTε were decidable then HALT would be, so HALTε is undecidable.
  3. Recognisable. Simulating M on ε accepts every machine that halts, so HALTε is r.e.
  4. Option (A) fails because a short input can drive a very long computation; length of input is not a bound on running time.
  5. Options (C) and (D) fail because the set is r.e.

Answer: Option (B) — r.e. but not recursive, with HALT ≤m HALTε.

Common misconception. Assuming that a short input makes a problem easy. Decidability depends on whether the computation can be bounded, not on the size of the input string; a machine on ε can run for any number of steps.

Takeaway. Restricting the input is a reduction, not a simplification. Every “does M do X on ε” question is a candidate for undecidability, and the way to settle it is to encode an arbitrary ⟨M, w⟩ into a machine that ignores its input.

TOC-U4-S2-Q14MSQmedium

Which of the following problems are decidable? Select all that apply.

  1. Given a Turing machine M and an integer n , does M halt on ε within n steps?
  2. Given a Turing machine M , does M , started on ε , ever move its head to the left of the cell on which it started?
  3. Given a Turing machine M , is L(M) finite?
  4. Given a deterministic Turing machine M and a string w , does M halt on w within 2^{|w|} steps?
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A bounded question is decidable because the simulation has a clock. An unbounded question about behaviour or about the language is not, and the standard reductions apply.

Step by step.

  1. (A) decidable. Simulate M on ε for at most n steps. If it has not halted by then, answer no. The simulation terminates by construction.
  2. (B) undecidable. Reduce HALT to it: given ⟨M, w⟩, build M′ that simulates M on w and, once that halts, moves its head left. Then M′ moves left exactly when M halts on w. There is no bound on when the move happens, so simulation cannot answer no.
  3. (C) undecidable. Finiteness of L(M) is a non-trivial semantic property, so Rice’s theorem applies.
  4. (D) decidable. The bound is a function of the input: simulate M on w for at most 2^{|w|} steps and answer no if it has not halted. Determinism matters only for the step count; the simulation is still finite.

Answer: Options (A) and (D).

Common misconception. Treating a bound written in terms of |w| as if it were no bound at all. A function of the input length is computable from the input, so it serves as a clock exactly like a constant does.

Takeaway. Ask where the clock comes from. If the number of steps is fixed in advance, or computable from the input, the problem is decidable by brute simulation; if it is not, expect a reduction from HALT.

TOC-U4-S2-Q20NATmedium

How many of the following five languages are recursively enumerable?
(i) {⟨M⟩ : L(M) = ∅} (ii) {⟨M⟩ : L(M) ≠ ∅} (iii) {⟨M⟩ : M halts on ε} (iv) {⟨M⟩ : M does not halt on ε} (v) {⟨M⟩ : M halts on ε within 10 steps}

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A language is r.e. when membership can be confirmed by a finite computation. A question whose yes-answer requires proving that something never happens is generally not r.e., even when it is decidable in a bounded form.

Step by step.

  1. (i) not r.e. “L(M) = ∅” is the complement of the r.e. set {⟨M⟩ : L(M) ≠ ∅}, and it is not itself r.e. Confirming emptiness would require showing that no input is ever accepted, which is an infinite check.
  2. (ii) r.e. Dovetail M over all inputs; accept as soon as some computation accepts.
  3. (iii) r.e. Simulate M on ε and accept when it halts.
  4. (iv) not r.e. This is the complement of (iii), and (iii) is r.e. without being recursive, so its complement is not r.e.
  5. (v) r.e. It is in fact recursive: simulate for 10 steps and answer. Every recursive language is r.e.
  6. Count: (ii), (iii) and (v) — three languages.

Answer: 3 — languages (ii), (iii) and (v) are recursively enumerable.

Common misconception. Counting only the obviously semi-decidable ones and forgetting that recursive languages are r.e. as well. A bounded question is decidable, hence r.e., and must be included in the count.

Takeaway. Sort the five into three piles: recursive (bounded questions), r.e. but not recursive (unbounded positive questions), and neither (unbounded negative questions). Only the first two piles are r.e.

TOC-U4-S2-Q22MCQhard

Let A = {⟨M⟩ : M halts on ε} and B = {⟨M⟩ : M halts on every input}. Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

  1. A ≤ m B , and both A and B are recursively enumerable.
  2. Both A and B are recursive.
  3. B ≤ m A ; A is recursively enumerable and B is not.
  4. Neither A nor B is recursively enumerable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Halting on one input is a positive existential question and is semi-decidable. Halting on every input is a universal question, and universality is what pushes a set out of the r.e. class.

Step by step.

  1. A is r.e. Simulate M on ε; accept when it halts.
  2. B ≤m A. Given ⟨M⟩, build M′ which on input x dovetails M over all inputs in order of i + k, halting when every input has been seen to halt. Then M′ halts on ε exactly when M halts on every input, so ⟨M⟩ ∈ B ⇔ ⟨M′⟩ ∈ A.
  3. B is not r.e. If it were, then since B ≤m A and A is r.e. we would have B decidable together with its complement; but B̄ is r.e. (halt on some input is confirmable by simulation) and B is not decidable, so B cannot be r.e.
  4. Option (A) fails because the reduction goes the other way: a machine that halts on ε tells you nothing about other inputs.
  5. Options (B) and (D) fail on the facts established above.

Answer: Option (C) — B ≤m A, with A r.e. and B not r.e.

Common misconception. Reversing the reduction because both sets are “about halting”. The direction follows the quantifier: a universal statement is stronger, so it reduces to the existential one, not the other way round.

Takeaway. Existential questions are r.e., universal questions are co-r.e., and only the decidable ones are both. When two halting sets are compared, decide first which one carries the “for all”.

Rice’s theorem & reductions · 10
TOC-U4-Q16MCQeasy

By Rice's theorem, which one of the following languages is undecidable?

  1. {⟨M⟩ : M has at least three states}
  2. {⟨M⟩ : the transition table of M contains the symbol 0}
  3. {⟨M⟩ : L(M) is regular}
  4. {⟨M⟩ : M halts on ε within 100 steps}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Rice's theorem: every non-trivial semantic property of L(M) is undecidable. Non-trivial means some r.e. language has the property and some does not; semantic means the property depends only on which strings the machine accepts, not on how the machine is built.

Step by step.

  1. Check the four options against the two conditions.
  2. (A) and (B) are syntactic: they are decided by inspecting the description of M — count its states, scan its table. Both are decidable.
  3. (D) is bounded: simulate for 100 steps and report. Decidable.
  4. (C) is semantic — it speaks only of L(M) — and non-trivial: ∅ is regular, while {aⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 0} is not. Rice's theorem therefore makes it undecidable.
  5. So exactly one option is undecidable.

Answer: Option (C).

Why the other options are decidable. Each is answered by a finite inspection: count the states, search the transition table, or run a clocked simulation. None requires knowing the language the machine accepts.

Common misconception. Applying Rice's theorem to any question about Turing machines. It applies only to properties of L(M). "Has three states" and "contains the symbol 0" are properties of the program text, and program text is a finite string that can be examined directly.

Takeaway. The test is always: does the property change if I replace M by an equivalent machine? If yes, it is semantic; if it is also non-trivial, it is undecidable.

TOC-U4-Q17MCQmedium

Rice's theorem applies only to properties of the language L(M), and only to non-trivial ones. To which one of the following languages does Rice's theorem NOT apply?

  1. {⟨M⟩ : M has an even number of states}
  2. {⟨M⟩ : L(M) = ∅}
  3. {⟨M⟩ : L(M) is infinite}
  4. {⟨M⟩ : L(M) contains at least one palindrome}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Rice's theorem is a statement about properties of L(M). A property of the machine's description — its number of states, its alphabet, the length of its encoding — is outside its scope, and such properties are usually decidable outright.

Step by step.

  1. Option (A) counts the states of M. Two machines accepting the same language may have different state counts, and an equivalent machine can always be padded with unreachable states. So the property is not semantic, and Rice's theorem says nothing about it.
  2. In fact (A) is decidable: read the description, count the states, test parity.
  3. Options (B), (C) and (D) are each properties of L(M) alone, and each is non-trivial: ∅ satisfies (B) but not (C); Σ* satisfies (C) but not (B); and Σ* satisfies (D) while {ab} does not.
  4. So Rice's theorem applies to (B), (C) and (D) and makes them undecidable, and it does not apply to (A).

Answer: Option (A).

Why the others are covered. Emptiness, infiniteness and "contains a palindrome" all speak only about which strings are accepted. Each holds for some machines and fails for others, so each is a non-trivial semantic property and Rice's theorem applies.

Common misconception. Thinking a syntactic property must be trivial. Syntactic properties can be interesting — "uses more than 10 states", "has a transition on the blank" — but because the description is a finite string they are all decidable by inspection. Rice's theorem is silent about them, not permissive.

Takeaway. Semantic properties are undecidable by Rice; syntactic properties are decidable by inspection. When a question mixes the two, split them before reaching for the theorem.

TOC-U4-Q18MSQmedium

Which of the following are non-trivial properties of the language L(M), and therefore undecidable by Rice's theorem?

  1. L(M) is finite.
  2. L(M) = Σ*.
  3. L(M) contains at least one string of length 5.
  4. M uses at most 50 tape cells on every input.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A property qualifies for Rice's theorem when (i) it is decided by L(M) alone and (ii) it is neither always true nor always false over the r.e. languages. The first three pass both tests; the fourth speaks about the machine's space usage.

Step by step.

  1. (A) qualifies. Finiteness depends only on L(M). Non-trivial: ∅ is finite, Σ* is infinite.
  2. (B) qualifies. Non-trivial: Σ* has L(M) = Σ* while ∅ has not. Undecidable by Rice's theorem.
  3. (C) qualifies. The set Σ⁵ is fixed and finite (32 strings over a binary alphabet), so "does M accept one of them" is a property of L(M). Non-trivial: ∅ accepts none, Σ* accepts all.
  4. (D) does not qualify. Space usage is a property of the computation, not of the accepted language: two machines accepting the same language can use very different space. It is still undecidable — by a reduction from halting — but not by Rice's theorem.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why (D) is excluded. Rice's theorem is about the extensional object L(M). Whether a machine stays within 50 cells is intensional: a wasteful machine and a frugal one may accept exactly the same language. The theorem has no purchase on it.

Common misconception. Treating "undecidable" and "undecidable by Rice's theorem" as the same thing. (D) is undecidable, yet Rice's theorem is not the reason; the correct tool is a direct reduction, since a halting computation can be encoded by making the machine write far out on the tape.

Takeaway. Read the property literally: if it mentions cells, steps, states or tape usage, Rice does not apply. If it mentions only the set of accepted strings, and some language has it while another does not, it does.

TOC-U4-Q19MCQmedium

The standard reduction proving that NE = {⟨M⟩ : L(M) ≠ ∅} is undecidable maps ⟨M, w⟩ to ⟨M′⟩, where M′ on input x ignores x, simulates M on w and accepts if that simulation halts. Which one of the following statements about this construction is correct?

  1. It is not a valid reduction, because M′ ignores its input x.
  2. ⟨M, w⟩ ∈ HALT ⟺ L(M′) ≠ ∅, so HALT ≤m NE and NE is undecidable.
  3. ⟨M, w⟩ ∈ HALT ⟺ L(M′) = ∅, so HALT ≤m NE.
  4. It shows only that NE is r.e., not that it is undecidable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The construction makes M′ a "witness machine": it accepts every input if M halts on w, and no input otherwise. That turns a question about one computation into a question about emptiness of a language.

Step by step.

  1. Case 1: M halts on w. Then M′ accepts every x it is given, so L(M′) = Σ*, in particular L(M′) ≠ ∅.
  2. Case 2: M does not halt on w. Then M′ loops on every input and accepts nothing, so L(M′) = ∅.
  3. Hence ⟨M, w⟩ ∈ HALT ⟺ L(M′) ≠ ∅, which is exactly the biconditional a reduction from HALT to NE requires. The map is computable: from the descriptions of M and w one writes down the description of M′ mechanically.
  4. If NE were decidable, composing its decider with this map would decide HALT, which is impossible. So NE is undecidable.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) misreads the technique. Ignoring the input is the whole point: it makes acceptance independent of x, so the only remaining question is whether the simulation of M on w halts.
  • (C) has the biconditional inverted. If M halts on w then M′ accepts everything, so L(M′) is not empty.
  • (D) understates the result. The reduction transfers undecidability, not merely r.e.-ness — though NE does happen to be r.e. as well.

Common misconception. Forgetting to check both directions of the biconditional. A reduction must map yes-instances to yes-instances and no-instances to no-instances; case 2 supplies the second half.

Takeaway. The "ignore the input, simulate, then accept" gadget is the workhorse of undecidability proofs. Rice's theorem packages it once and for all: any non-trivial property of L(M) can be forced to hold or fail by choosing whether the simulated computation halts.

TOC-U4-Q20NAThard

Consider the following six properties of L(M).
(i) L(M) = ∅
(ii) L(M) is regular
(iii) 0101 ∈ L(M)
(iv) L(M) is co-finite, that is Σ* \ L(M) is finite
(v) L(M) ⊆ Σ*
(vi) |L(M)| ≤ 3
How many of these six are undecidable by Rice's theorem?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Rice's theorem needs two things of a property: it must be a property of L(M), and it must be non-trivial — true for at least one r.e. language and false for at least one. Five of the six pass both tests; the exception is trivially true.

Step by step.

  1. (i) undecidable. Non-trivial (∅ has it, Σ* does not) and semantic. Rice applies.
  2. (ii) undecidable. Regularity depends only on L(M); ∅ is regular and {aⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 0} is not. Rice applies.
  3. (iii) undecidable. Membership of one fixed string is a semantic property; Σ* contains 0101, ∅ does not. Rice applies.
  4. (iv) undecidable. Co-finiteness is semantic and non-trivial: Σ* is co-finite, ∅ is not. Rice applies.
  5. (v) decidable. Every language over Σ satisfies L(M) ⊆ Σ*, so the property is trivial and the language in question is all of Σ*; a decider simply accepts everything. Rice's theorem requires non-triviality and therefore does not apply.
  6. (vi) undecidable. Bounded cardinality is semantic and non-trivial: ∅ satisfies it, Σ* does not. Rice applies.
  7. Counting: (i), (ii), (iii), (iv) and (vi) are undecidable by Rice's theorem, and (v) is not. The answer is 5.

Answer: 5

How the answer was checked. Each of the six was tested against the two conditions rather than judged by feel. For every one of (i), (ii), (iii), (iv) and (vi) a witness pair was produced — a language with the property and a language without it — which is exactly the non-triviality certificate Rice's theorem needs. Property (v) has no such pair, because it holds for every language over Σ.

Common misconception. Assuming every property of L(M) is undecidable by Rice. The theorem excludes the two trivial properties — always true and always false — and those are decidable precisely because a decider can ignore its input. It also excludes properties of the machine rather than of its language.

Takeaway. Before invoking Rice, ask: can I name a language with the property and a language without it? If yes, the property is non-trivial and undecidable. If no, it is trivial, and trivially decidable.

TOC-U4-S2-Q07MCQeasy

Rice’s theorem speaks of non-trivial properties of the language L(M). Which one of the following is a trivial property of L(M), that is, a property that every recursively enumerable language has?

  1. L(M) = ∅
  2. L(M) ⊆ Σ*
  3. L(M) is finite
  4. ε ∈ L(M)
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A property is trivial when it holds for every r.e. language or for none. Rice’s theorem needs non-triviality, because a trivial property is decided by a machine that ignores its input and always answers the same way.

Step by step.

  1. Every language is a subset of Σ* by definition, so L(M) ⊆ Σ* holds for every machine. The property is trivial and the language of encodings satisfying it is Σ* itself, which is decidable.
  2. The other three are true of some r.e. languages and false of others, so each is non-trivial: ∅ = ∅ but Σ* ≠ ∅; {ε} is finite but Σ* is not; ε ∈ Σ* but ε ∉ ∅.
  3. By Rice’s theorem all three of those are undecidable.

Answer: Option (B) — L(M) ⊆ Σ* is true of every machine, so it is trivial.

Common misconception. Treating “trivial” as “easy to check”. Triviality is about the truth set: a property is trivial when it is satisfied by all r.e. languages or by none, not when it looks simple.

Takeaway. Rice’s theorem has two hypotheses: the property must be semantic (about L(M)) and non-trivial (neither always true nor always false). A question that offers a universally true property is testing the second hypothesis.

TOC-U4-S2-Q09MSQmedium

Which of the following languages are undecidable by Rice’s theorem? Select all that apply.

  1. {⟨M⟩ : M has exactly seven states}
  2. {⟨M⟩ : L(M) is a finite set}
  3. {⟨M⟩ : the encoding of M contains the substring 101}
  4. {⟨M⟩ : L(M) contains every string of length at most 5}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Rice’s theorem needs the property to be about L(M) and to be non-trivial. A property of the encoding — how many states, which symbols appear — is not a property of L(M) at all, and is usually decidable by inspection.

Step by step.

  1. (A) false. The number of states is written into the encoding. Decode and count; no simulation is needed. The property is syntactic, so Rice’s theorem does not apply, and the language is decidable.
  2. (B) true. Finiteness is a property of L(M). It is non-trivial: ∅ is finite, Σ* is not. Both are r.e. languages, so the property is neither always true nor always false, and Rice’s theorem applies.
  3. (C) false. Containing the substring 101 is a property of the encoding string. It is checked by scanning that string.
  4. (D) true. Containing all strings of length at most 5 is a property of L(M). It is non-trivial: Σ* has it, ∅ does not. Rice’s theorem applies.

Answer: Options (B) and (D).

Common misconception. Applying Rice’s theorem to the machine instead of its language. “Has seven states” and “contains 101 in its encoding” are facts about the description, and the description is a finite object that can be read directly.

Takeaway. Before invoking Rice, ask two questions: is the property about L(M), and does some r.e. language have it while another does not? If either answer is no, Rice gives nothing.

TOC-U4-S2-Q17MCQmedium

To prove that INF = {⟨M⟩ : L(M) is infinite} is undecidable, the standard reduction from HALT maps ⟨M, w⟩ to ⟨M′⟩, where M′ on input x simulates M on w for exactly |x| steps and accepts x if the simulation has halted by then. Why does the bound |x|, rather than a fixed constant, matter?

  1. Because it guarantees that M′ halts on every input.
  2. Because it bounds the running time of M′ by a polynomial in |x| .
  3. Because without a bound M′ would not be a legal Turing machine.
  4. Because with it L(M′) is infinite exactly when M halts on w , so ⟨M, w⟩ ∈ HALT ⇔ ⟨M′⟩ ∈ INF .
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The reduction must convert a question about one machine halting into a question about another machine having an infinite language. A threshold that grows with the input is what turns “halts in t steps” into “accepts infinitely many inputs”.

Step by step.

  1. If M halts on w after t steps, then for every x with |x| ≥ t the simulation finishes inside the budget, so x is accepted. There are infinitely many such x, so L(M′) is infinite.
  2. If M does not halt on w, the simulation never finishes inside any budget, so no x is accepted and L(M′) is empty, hence finite.
  3. Therefore ⟨M, w⟩ ∈ HALT ⇔ ⟨M′⟩ ∈ INF, which is exactly a mapping reduction HALT ≤m INF. With a fixed constant bound c the accepted set would be all x or nothing, and it would no longer track halting.
  4. Option (A) is false: M′ always halts, because its simulation is bounded. Halting is not what is being tested.
  5. Options (B) and (C) are irrelevant to the correctness of the reduction.

Answer: Option (D) — the growing bound makes L(M′) infinite exactly when M halts on w.

Common misconception. Thinking the bound is there to make M′ terminate. M′ terminates for any bound; the point of |x| is that it creates a threshold, so that a single halting time t controls infinitely many inputs.

Takeaway. In reductions to INF, FIN or REG, the gadget is always the same: a budget that grows with the input. The budget is the bridge between a finite event and an infinite language.

TOC-U4-S2-Q19MSQmedium

Consider Rice’s theorem and the properties of Turing machines listed below. Which of the following statements are TRUE? Select all that apply.

  1. Rice’s theorem shows that {⟨M⟩ : L(M) = Σ*} is undecidable.
  2. Rice’s theorem applies to the property “ L(M) contains the string 0101”.
  3. Rice’s theorem applies to the property “ M has an odd number of states”.
  4. Rice’s theorem shows that {⟨M⟩ : M uses more than 100 tape cells on some input} is undecidable.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Rice’s theorem is about the set of strings a machine accepts, never about how the machine is built or how much space it uses. Semantic and non-trivial are the two conditions; everything else is outside the theorem.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true. “L(M) = Σ*” is a property of L(M). It is non-trivial: Σ* has it and ∅ does not. Rice applies, so the set is undecidable.
  2. (B) true. Containing a particular string is a property of L(M), and it is non-trivial: Σ* contains 0101 and ∅ does not. Rice applies.
  3. (C) false. The number of states is read off the encoding; it is a syntactic property, so Rice’s theorem says nothing about it, and the language is in fact decidable.
  4. (D) false. Space usage is a property of the machine’s behaviour, not of L(M): two machines accepting the same language can use very different amounts of tape. Rice does not apply. (The problem is undecidable, but for other reasons.)

Answer: Options (A) and (B).

Common misconception. Assuming that any undecidable problem about machines is undecidable “by Rice’s theorem”. Many are — space usage, for instance — but the theorem covers only properties of L(M).

Takeaway. Two tests, both required: is the property a fact about L(M), and is it true of some r.e. language while false of another? Rice’s theorem is a one-line proof when both answers are yes, and silent otherwise.

TOC-U4-S2-Q25NAThard

The language L = {⟨M⟩ : M halts on every input of length at most 3 within 1000 steps} is decidable: for each such input the simulation is bounded, so it always terminates. Over the alphabet Σ = {0, 1}, on how many input strings must the decider simulate M?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The bound is on the length of the input and on the number of steps, and both bounds together make the whole check finite. What remains is to count the strings that fall inside the length bound.

Step by step.

  1. Strings of length 0: the empty string ε, so 1 string.
  2. Strings of length 1: 0 and 1, so 2 strings.
  3. Strings of length 2: 2² = 4 strings.
  4. Strings of length 3: 2³ = 8 strings.
  5. Total 1 + 2 + 4 + 8 = 15. Each is simulated for at most 1000 steps, so the decider runs for at most 15 × 1000 simulated steps and always halts.
  6. Note why the step bound cannot be dropped: {⟨M⟩ : M halts on every input of length at most 3} without a time bound is undecidable, because it asks for a halting answer on 15 specific strings and each of those questions is itself undecidable.

Answer: 15 input strings.

Common misconception. Summing only the strings of length exactly 3 and answering 8, or forgetting ε altogether and answering 14. The phrase “at most 3” includes length 0.

Takeaway. A decidable bounded-halting question is a finite conjunction over finitely many inputs. Count the inputs with Σ_{i=0}^{k} |Σ|ⁱ, then note that each conjunct is decidable only because the step budget is fixed.

Chomsky hierarchy summary · 10
TOC-U4-Q21MCQeasy

Which one of the following statements about the classes of the Chomsky hierarchy is TRUE?

  1. Every recursive language is context-sensitive.
  2. Every context-sensitive language is recursive.
  3. Every recursively enumerable language is context-sensitive.
  4. The class of context-free languages equals the class of context-sensitive languages.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The Chomsky hierarchy is a chain of strict inclusions: Regular ⊂ CFL ⊂ CSL ⊂ RE. Each step up adds a more powerful machine, and each step up keeps everything the level below can do.

Step by step.

  1. A context-sensitive language is accepted by a linear-bounded automaton, whose tape is bounded by the input length n. With |Q| states and n cells there are only |Q| · n · |Γ|ⁿ configurations — finitely many — so a brute-force search of the configuration graph decides membership.
  2. A total decision procedure therefore exists, so every CSL is recursive. Option (B) is true.
  3. The inclusion is strict at the top: the halting set K is r.e. and not recursive, hence not context-sensitive. Option (C) is false.
  4. The inclusion is strict at the bottom too: there are recursive languages that no non-contracting grammar generates, so some recursive languages are not context-sensitive. Option (A) is false.
  5. And {aⁿbⁿcⁿ : n ≥ 1} is context-sensitive but not context-free, so the two classes differ. Option (D) is false.

Answer: Option (B).

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) reverses a strict inclusion: recursion is above context-sensitivity, not below it.
  • (C) ignores the top of the hierarchy; Type 0 is strictly larger than Type 1.
  • (D) contradicts the standard witness {aⁿbⁿcⁿ : n ≥ 1}, which an LBA accepts but no PDA does.

Common misconception. Reading the hierarchy as "bigger type number, bigger class" and then assuming every level is decidable. Only Type 0 escapes decidability: Types 1, 2 and 3 are all recursive.

Takeaway. Type 3 ⊂ Type 2 ⊂ Type 1 ⊂ Type 0. Decidability is lost exactly once, between Type 1 and Type 0, when the tape bound is removed.

TOC-U4-Q22MCQeasy

The class of languages accepted by a linear-bounded automaton is exactly the class of:

  1. regular languages
  2. context-free languages
  3. recursive languages
  4. context-sensitive languages
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The Chomsky hierarchy pairs each language class with a machine: finite automaton with regular, pushdown automaton with context-free, linear-bounded automaton with context-sensitive, Turing machine with recursively enumerable.

Step by step.

  1. A linear-bounded automaton is a Turing machine whose tape is confined to the input cells. That restriction is exactly what a non-contracting grammar describes: every production either keeps the length or increases it, never shrinks it.
  2. By the standard equivalence, a language is context-sensitive iff some LBA accepts it — the tape bound on the machine matches the non-shrinking condition on the grammar.
  3. The class is strictly larger than the context-free languages ({aⁿbⁿcⁿ : n ≥ 1} is accepted by an LBA but by no PDA) and strictly smaller than the recursive languages.
  4. So the answer is the context-sensitive class.

Answer: Option (D) — the context-sensitive languages.

Why the other options are wrong.

  • (A) belongs to the one-way finite automaton and (B) to the pushdown automaton; both machines have strictly less storage than an LBA.
  • (C) is the class of languages decided by a Turing machine with no tape bound; every CSL is recursive, but not conversely.

Common misconception. Confusing "bounded tape" with "no tape". An LBA can still scan and rewrite its whole input many times; it simply cannot run off the end. That is why it reaches beyond the context-free languages.

Takeaway. Machine to language class: DFA gives regular, PDA gives context-free, LBA gives context-sensitive, TM gives recursively enumerable.

TOC-U4-Q23MSQeasy

Which of the following statements about the Chomsky hierarchy and its machine models are TRUE?

  1. Every regular language is context-free.
  2. Every context-free language is recursive.
  3. The membership problem for context-sensitive languages is decidable.
  4. Every recursively enumerable language is recursive.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Climbing the hierarchy never loses a language, and the first three levels are decidable. Only Type 0 is undecidable.

Step by step.

  1. (A) true. A DFA is a pushdown automaton that never uses its stack, so every regular language has a PDA and hence a context-free grammar.
  2. (B) true. The CYK algorithm decides membership in a context-free language in O(n³) time given the grammar in Chomsky normal form, and it always halts.
  3. (C) true. An LBA has finitely many configurations — at most |Q| · n · |Γ|ⁿ — so membership is decided by exploring the configuration graph and testing whether an accepting configuration is reachable. The search is finite, hence terminating.
  4. (D) false. The halting set is r.e. and not recursive; the inclusion Recursive ⊂ RE is strict.

Answer: Options (A), (B) and (C).

Why (D) fails. If every r.e. language were recursive, the halting problem would be decidable, which the diagonal argument rules out. R.e. means recognisable, not decidable.

Common misconception. Believing context-sensitive membership is undecidable because the grammar is complicated. Complexity is not the issue: the configuration space of an LBA is finite, so even a crude search terminates. It may take exponential time, but decidability asks only for termination.

Takeaway. Types 1, 2 and 3 are decidable; Type 0 is not. The dividing line is the tape bound: bounded space makes the configuration graph finite.

TOC-U4-Q24MCQmedium

Which one of the following languages is context-sensitive but NOT context-free?

  1. {aⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 1}
  2. {w wᴿ : w ∈ {a, b}*}
  3. {aⁿbᵐ : n ≠ m}
  4. {aⁿbⁿcⁿ : n ≥ 1}
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A context-free language is recognised by a pushdown automaton, whose single stack can match one nested pair of counts. Three counts that must all agree need two independent comparisons, which one stack cannot make.

Step by step.

  1. Option (A) is context-free: S → aSb | ab generates it, and the stack counts the a's and matches them with the b's.
  2. Option (B) is context-free: guess the midpoint nondeterministically, then match the second half against the stack in reverse.
  3. Option (C) is context-free: it is the union {aⁿbᵐ : n > m} ∪ {aⁿbᵐ : n < m}, and context-free languages are closed under union.
  4. Option (D) is not context-free. By the pumping lemma, pumping any sufficiently long string of {aⁿbⁿcⁿ} produces a string that must still be in the language; but pumping changes one block while leaving the others fixed, so the three counts disagree.
  5. It is context-sensitive: the grammar with S → aSBC | aBC, CB → BC, aB → ab, bB → bb, bC → bc, cC → cc is non-contracting, so an LBA accepts the language.

Answer: Option (D) — {aⁿbⁿcⁿ : n ≥ 1}.

Why the others are context-free. Each of (A), (B) and (C) can be recognised with one stack: (A) and (B) by matching symbols against the stack, (C) by a nondeterministic union of two one-stack machines.

Common misconception. Assuming {aⁿbⁿcⁿ} is context-free because it "looks like" {aⁿbⁿ}. Adding a third block that must equal the first two is exactly what one stack cannot do — the stack is popped while reading the b's, so no record of n remains to check the c's against.

Takeaway. One stack handles one nesting depth. {aⁿbⁿcⁿ} is the standard witness separating context-free from context-sensitive, exactly as {aⁿbⁿ} separates regular from context-free.

TOC-U4-Q25NAThard

Consider the following six languages over Σ = {a, b, c}.
(i) {aⁿbⁿcⁿ : n ≥ 1}
(ii) {aⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 1}
(iii) {w w : w ∈ {a, b}*}
(iv) {w wᴿ : w ∈ {a, b}*}
(v) {aⁿbⁿcⁿdⁿ : n ≥ 1}
(vi) {aⁱbʲcᵏ : i = j or j = k}
How many of these six are context-free?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A pushdown automaton has one stack, so it can enforce one "counted" equality. Languages demanding two independent equalities among three or more blocks are outside the class; languages that are a union of two one-equality conditions are inside it, because context-free languages are closed under union.

Step by step.

  1. (i) not context-free. Three blocks must agree; one stack cannot remember n while checking b's against it and also check c's. This is the standard pumping-lemma counterexample.
  2. (ii) context-free. Generated by S → aSb | ab.
  3. (iii) not context-free. Copy languages need to compare two halves without a marker at the midpoint; a PDA must guess where the middle is, and a wrong guess cannot be recovered from because the stack has been consumed. The pumping lemma confirms it.
  4. (iv) context-free. Generated by S → aSa | bSb | ε: guess the midpoint nondeterministically and match the second half against the stack in reverse. The reversal is what makes it fit one stack.
  5. (v) not context-free. Four blocks and three independent equalities — even further outside the class.
  6. (vi) context-free. It is {aⁱbʲcᵏ : i = j} ∪ {aⁱbʲcᵏ : j = k}, a union of two context-free languages, and context-free languages are closed under union.
  7. Counting: (ii), (iv) and (vi) are context-free, so the answer is 3.

Answer: 3

How the answer was checked. The three members were exhibited by grammar or construction, not merely asserted: S → aSb | ab for (ii), S → aSa | bSb | ε for (iv), and the union decomposition for (vi). The three non-members each require two independent equalities — (i) and (v) directly, (iii) through the midpoint argument — which one stack cannot supply.

Common misconception. Counting (iii) {w w} as context-free because {w wᴿ} is. The reversal in (iv) is what a stack can do; plain copying is not, since the machine would have to remember the first half in order and compare it forwards, but a stack returns symbols in reverse.

Takeaway. Context-free means "one counted comparison". Unions of such conditions stay context-free; conjunctions of two independent ones generally do not.

TOC-U4-S2-Q05NATeasy

Consider the four levels of the Chomsky hierarchy: Type 3 (regular), Type 2 (context-free), Type 1 (context-sensitive) and Type 0 (recursively enumerable). For how many of these four classes is the union of any two languages of the class always again a language of the same class?

Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A class is closed under union when a machine for L₁ ∪ L₂ can be built from machines for L₁ and L₂ without leaving the class. Each level of the hierarchy has such a construction, because each level has a machine model that can guess which side to follow.

Step by step.

  1. Regular. Take the two DFAs, form the product automaton and make a state final when either coordinate is final. Closed.
  2. Context-free. Take grammars G₁ = (V₁, Σ, P₁, S₁) and G₂ with disjoint nonterminal sets, add a fresh start symbol S and the productions S → S₁ | S₂. Closed.
  3. Context-sensitive. Take the non-contracting grammars, rename nonterminals apart, add S → S₁ | S₂. Every production still satisfies |α| ≤ |β|. Closed.
  4. Recursively enumerable. Run two recognisers in parallel (dovetail) and accept when either accepts. Closed.
  5. So all four levels are closed under union: the count is 4.

Answer: 4 — all four classes are closed under union.

Common misconception. Carrying the failure of one closure property over to another. The context-free languages are not closed under intersection or complementation, and that fact is often misremembered as a failure of union closure as well.

Takeaway. Union closure is nearly universal in the hierarchy; intersection and complementation are the properties that separate the levels. When a question asks which level breaks a closure property, expect the answer to be intersection or complement, not union.

TOC-U4-S2-Q08MCQeasy

Which one of the following statements is FALSE?

  1. Every regular language is context-sensitive.
  2. The class of context-sensitive languages is closed under complementation.
  3. Membership of a string in a context-free language can be decided in polynomial time in the length of the string.
  4. Every context-sensitive language is context-free.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The hierarchy is a chain of strict inclusions Regular ⊂ CFL ⊂ CSL ⊂ RE. Each level is contained in the next, so a statement that reverses one of the inclusions is the false one.

Step by step.

  1. Regular languages are accepted by DFAs, which are linear-bounded automata with no tape use, so Regular ⊆ CSL. (A) is true.
  2. Context-sensitive languages are closed under complement: this is the Immerman–Szelepcsényi theorem, proved by counting reachable configurations of the linear-bounded automaton. (B) is true.
  3. Membership in a context-free language is decided by the CYK algorithm in O(n³) time, polynomial in n. (C) is true.
  4. The inclusion CFL ⊂ CSL is strict: {aⁿbⁿcⁿ : n ≥ 1} is context-sensitive and not context-free. So (D) is false.

Answer: Option (D) — context-sensitive languages strictly contain the context-free ones.

Common misconception. Reversing the direction of an inclusion. Every context-free language is context-sensitive, but the converse fails; the witness {aⁿbⁿcⁿ} needs a tape that can compare three counts, which a pushdown store cannot do.

Takeaway. Keep the chain in one direction: Regular ⊂ CFL ⊂ CSL ⊂ RE. Any option that asserts the opposite inclusion is the false one, whatever else it says.

TOC-U4-S2-Q11MCQeasy

A grammar is context-sensitive when every production α → β satisfies |α| ≤ |β|, with the single usual exception that S → ε is allowed when S is the start symbol and does not appear on any right-hand side. Which one of the following productions is allowed in a context-sensitive grammar?

  1. ABC → AB
  2. A → ε
  3. AB → BA
  4. AB → A
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. The condition is purely length-based: no production may shorten the sentential form. A production with two symbols on the left needs at least two on the right, and the special empty production is reserved for the start symbol.

Step by step.

  1. Option (A): |ABC| = 3 and |AB| = 2. The form shrinks, so it is not allowed.
  2. Option (B): |A| = 1 and |ε| = 0. It shrinks, and A is not declared to be the start symbol, so it is not allowed.
  3. Option (C): |AB| = 2 and |BA| = 2. The lengths are equal, so the production is allowed. It is a genuine context-sensitive production: it swaps two adjacent nonterminals, which is how a context-sensitive grammar sorts a string without ever shortening it.
  4. Option (D): |AB| = 2 and |A| = 1. It shrinks, so it is not allowed.
  5. Exactly one option satisfies |α| ≤ |β|.

Answer: Option (C) — AB → BA keeps the length at 2, so it is allowed.

Common misconception. Reading the definition as a requirement on the form of the left side, for example insisting on αAβ → αγβ with γ ≠ ε. The length condition is the operational test, and it is the one that makes the language class decidable: no derivation ever shortens the string, so only finitely many sentential forms of each length exist.

Takeaway. Context-sensitive means non-shortening. Whenever a production is offered for inspection, compare the lengths of the two sides first.

TOC-U4-S2-Q18MCQeasy

The language L = {w ∈ {a, b}* : w contains the same number of a’s as b’s} belongs to which of the following classes, taking the smallest class that contains it?

  1. The regular languages
  2. The context-free languages, but not the regular languages
  3. The context-sensitive languages, but not the context-free languages
  4. None of the four Chomsky classes
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. A pushdown store can count a difference but a finite automaton cannot, and a single counter suffices here. So the language sits at Type 2 and no lower.

Step by step.

  1. Context-free. Use the grammar S → aSb | bSa | SS | ε. Each production adds one a and one b together, or nests a balanced block inside another, so every generated string has equal counts.
  2. Not regular. Intersect with a*b*, which is regular. The result is {aⁿbⁿ : n ≥ 0}, which is not regular by the pumping lemma. Regular languages are closed under intersection with a regular language, so L cannot be regular.
  3. Since L is context-free, it is also context-sensitive and r.e., so options (C) and (D) are wrong: (C) claims it is not context-free and (D) claims it is in none of the classes.

Answer: Option (B) — context-free but not regular.

Common misconception. Confusing this language with {aⁿbⁿ} or assuming that any counting language must be context-sensitive. Equal counts of two symbols need one counter; equal counts of three symbols need two, and that is where context-freeness ends.

Takeaway. For a two-letter alphabet, “equal counts” is context-free: one stack holds the running difference. For three letters, the same idea needs a second stack, and the language becomes context-sensitive only.

TOC-U4-S2-Q24MCQhard

Which one of the following statements about closure properties in the Chomsky hierarchy is TRUE?

  1. The context-free languages are not closed under complementation.
  2. Every context-sensitive language is context-free.
  3. The context-free languages are closed under intersection.
  4. The context-sensitive languages are closed under complementation, while the recursively enumerable languages are not.
Show answer & solution

Detailed solution

Concept. Complement closure does not follow the hierarchy in a straight line. It fails at the top and, surprisingly, holds for context-free languages; for context-sensitive languages it is a deep theorem.

Step by step.

  1. Regular. Closed: swap final and non-final states of a total DFA.
  2. Context-free. Closed. The classical proof uses the fact that L is context-free exactly when L is the homomorphic image of the intersection of a Dyck language with a regular set, and the complement of such a set can be written in the same shape.
  3. Context-sensitive. Closed by the Immerman–Szelepcsényi theorem: a linear-bounded automaton can count the configurations reachable from its start configuration, and counting is enough to decide non-reachability.
  4. Recursively enumerable. Not closed: HALT is r.e. and its complement is not.
  5. Hence (A) is false, (B) reverses a strict inclusion, (C) fails because {aⁿbⁿcⁿ} is the intersection of two context-free languages and is not context-free, and (D) is the true statement.

Answer: Option (D) — context-sensitive languages are closed under complement, recursively enumerable ones are not.

Common misconception. The two standard traps are the context-free languages, which are closed under complement, and the context-sensitive languages, which look too weak to be. Both facts contradict the intuition that “harder class, better closure”.

Takeaway. Closure properties must be learned per class, not extrapolated up or down the hierarchy. The safe anchors are: regular is closed under everything; r.e. fails on complement; context-free fails on intersection but not on complement.

What this test contains

25 questions on Unit 3 (Pushdown Automata), Set 1 of 2 of the second pass over Theory of Computation. All four subtopics are covered: deterministic pushdown automata; nondeterministic pushdown automata; acceptance by empty stack against acceptance by final state; and the equivalence between pushdown automata and context-free grammars. Composition: 15 MCQ, 5 MSQ, 5 NAT; difficulty 10 easy, 10 medium, 5 hard; 60 minutes, 35 marks. MCQ carries 1 mark, MSQ and NAT carry 2 marks, there is no negative marking, and an MSQ scores only when the exact set of correct options is selected. This is a practice marking scheme, not the official GATE scheme.

Concept map used to build the questions

  • Deterministic PDAs. The determinism condition as a restriction on δ (at most one move per state, input symbol and stack top, and no ε-move competing with an input move); runs, configurations and stack behaviour; determinism against termination, since a deterministic machine may still diverge; the closure table of the deterministic context-free languages, where complement is safe and union, intersection and star are not.
  • Nondeterministic PDAs. Subset-valued transition functions and existential acceptance; guessing a position in the input as the source of extra power; palindromes and {w wR} as the canonical witnesses that need a guess; inherent ambiguity as the grammar-side shadow of the same fact; stacks as a measure of power, with two stacks reaching the recursively enumerable class.
  • Acceptance modes. Final state looks at the state and the input pointer only; empty stack looks at the stack and the input pointer only; the two are interchangeable for nondeterministic machines but not for deterministic ones, where the empty-stack languages are exactly the prefix-free ones; the ε-moves needed for the translation as the reason determinism is lost.
  • Equivalence with grammars. The single-state top-down construction and its empty-stack acceptance; the triple construction [q, A, p] and the shared intermediate state it needs; normal forms, with Greibach Normal Form giving one terminal per step and Chomsky Normal Form giving binary branching; the LR(1) characterisation of the deterministic languages and the undecidability of ambiguity.
  • Connections. Unit 3 is the machine-side twin of Unit 2 (grammars) and the stepping stone to Unit 4: add a second stack or unrestricted tape access and the class jumps to the recursively enumerable languages.

Source categories

Every question carries a source tag. Standard textbook marks a question built on the definitions, theorems and proof techniques of the standard theory-of-computation references. Original synthesis marks a question written for this series. A tag records the academic tradition a question comes from; it is not a claim of provenance. Only a question with a verified year, paper and official answer key is ever called an official GATE question, and this paper contains none — no Theory of Computation previous-year question on this unit has been verified against an official paper and key.
